New topological restrictions for spaces with nonnegative Ricci curvature
Alessandro Cucinotta, Mattia Magnabosco, and Daniele Semola

Abstract
. We obtain new topological restrictions for complete Riemannian manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature and RCD ⁡ ( 0 , n ) \RCD(0,n) spaces. Our main results are a Betti number rigidity theorem which answers a question open since work of M.-T. Anderson in 1990, and a vanishing theorem for the simplicial volume generalizing a theorem of M. Gromov from 1982. Combining such results we obtain a new proof of the classification of noncompact 3 3 -manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature, originally due to G. Liu in 2011, which extends to the synthetic setting.



1.
Introduction

The topology of complete noncompact Riemannian manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature has been studied extensively since the earliest developments of geometric analysis. In dimension
2
, where
\Ric\geq 0
is equivalent to nonnegative sectional curvature, a complete classification up to diffeomorphism was obtained by S. Cohn-Vossen in 1935, see
[
29
]
. In dimension
3
, the classification was achieved much more recently in 2011 by G. Liu, see
[
63
]
. The strategy in
[
63
]
is based on minimal surfaces methods. It develops in a highly nontrivial way some ideas introduced by R. Schoen and S.-T. Yau in
[
81
]
along their proof that a complete
(M^{3},g)
with
\Ric>0
must be diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For
n\geq 4
, a classification up to diffeomorphism seems far too ambitious. We note that even the classification up to homeomorphism of complete Ricci-flat
4
-manifolds is a longstanding open question. At the same time, several families of complete
(M^{n},g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
and infinite topological type have been constructed in the past decades, see for instance
[
7
,
82
,
68
]
.

1.1.
Main results

In this paper we present a new proof of the classification of
3
-manifolds with
\Ric\geq 0
up to diffeomorphism which:

i)

is based on two key tools whose validity extends to all dimensions;

ii)

generalizes to the singular setting, thus yielding a classification of
\RCD(0,3)
spaces without boundary up to homeomorphism.

The first step towards the classification of
3
-manifolds with
\Ric\geq 0
in
[
63
]
is proving that any such
(M^{3},g)
is either simply connected or its universal cover splits a line isometrically. Thanks to
[
81
, Lemma 2]
, which shows that
\pi_{2}(M)
is trivial unless the universal cover of
(M,g)
splits a line isometrically, it suffices to deal with the case when
\pi_{1}(M)
is torsion free.
Thus the key ingredient is the following:

Theorem 1.1

(
[
63
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete Riemannian
3
-manifold with
\Ric\geq 0
. If
\pi_{1}(M)
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, then the universal cover
(\overline{M},\overline{g})
of
(M,g)
splits a line isometrically.

Theorem
1.1
strengthened an earlier result of Schoen and Yau who proved that if
(M^{3},g)
has
\Ric>0
then it is simply connected, see
[
81
, Lemma 3]
.

Our first main result is a generalization of Theorem
1.1
to all dimensions:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete Riemannian
n
-manifold with
\Ric\geq 0
for
n\geq 3
. If
\pi_{1}(M)
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
, then the universal cover
(\overline{M},\overline{g})
of
(M,g)
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
isometrically.

Remark 1.3
.

In
[
74
]
, P. Nabonnand constructed a complete
(M^{4},g)
with
\Ric>0
and
\pi_{1}(M^{4})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. L. Bérard Bergery generalized such construction later in
[
10
]
by building examples of
(M^{n},g)
with
\Ric>0
and
\pi_{1}(M^{n})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n-3}
. These examples show that Theorem
1.2
is sharp, in the sense that its conclusion might fail if we just assume that
\pi_{1}(M^{n})
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-3}
.

Remark 1.4
.

Schoen and Yau conjectured in
[
81
, p. 211]
that for every
n\geq 4
and every complete Riemannian
(M^{n},g)
with
\Ric>0
the rank of a free abelian subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M^{n})
should be less than or equal to
n-3
. Their conjecture was settled by M.-T. Anderson in
[
5
]
. In the same paper, Anderson obtained Theorem
1.2
under the additional assumption that
(M^{n},g)
has bounded sectional curvature, after settling the
3
-dimensional case jointly with L. Rodriguez in
[
8
, Theorem 2]
. The validity of Theorem
1.1
in all dimensions without further assumptions had remained an open question since then. Very recently, it was stated explicitly as a conjecture by Z. Ye in
[
92
, Conjecture 1]
(see also the more recent
[
77
, Question 1.6]
).

Remark 1.5
.

While we were writing up the paper, the interesting preprint
[
53
]
by H. Huang and X.-T. Huang appeared on arXiv. One of their main results establishes Theorem
1.2
under the additional assumption that the universal cover
(\overline{M},\overline{g})
has maximal volume growth. Although there is an overlap between the two results (and the methods of the proof) they were obtained independently.

Remark 1.6
.

Theorem
1.2
generalizes to
\RCD(0,n)
metric measure spaces
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
, see Theorem
3.1
below for the precise statement. Such generalization is crucial for the topological classification of
\RCD(0,3)
spaces without boundary.

In the second part of the proof in
[
63
]
the author argues that a contractible
(M^{3},g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
is simply connected at infinity. This is sufficient to complete the classification up to diffeomorphism in view of the previous considerations,
[
54
, Theorem 2]
,
[
69
]
, and G. Perelman’s solution of the Poincaré conjecture. A similar strategy was pursued earlier by Schoen and Yau in
[
81
]
, though they avoided relying on the Poincaré conjecture by proving directly that
M
is irreducible, based again on minimal surfaces methods.
We do not know whether a contractible
(M^{n},g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
must be simply connected at infinity when
n\geq 4
. To the best of our knowledge the question is open even for
n=4
and
\Ric\equiv 0
.

The second main result of this paper is a new topological restriction on manifolds supporting a (complete) metric with
\Ric\geq 0
based on the notion of simplicial volume. The simplicial volume of a connected (possibly noncompact) manifold
M
is a proper homotopy invariant denoted by
||M||\in[0,\infty]
. It was introduced by M. Gromov in
[
46
]
. Here we obtain the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
n\geq 2
. If
(M^{n},g)
is a smooth, complete, oriented Riemannian manifold with
\Ric\geq 0
, then
||M||=0
.

Despite the simplicial volume being a topological invariant, its interplay with (Ricci) curvature became clear since the earliest developments of the theory. In particular, Gromov in
[
46
, p. 14]
obtained the following
Isolation Theorem
for the simplicial volume under lower Ricci curvature bounds:

Theorem 1.8

(
[
46
]
)
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
\varepsilon(n)>0
such that the following holds. Let
(M^{n},g)
be a complete Riemannian manifold with
\Ric\geq-(n-1)
. If
\mathrm{vol}(B_{1}(p))\leq\varepsilon(n)
for every
p\in M
, then
||M||=0
.

An immediate corollary of Theorem
1.8
is that the simplicial volume of any closed manifold with
\Ric\geq 0
vanishes. Thanks to Theorem
1.7
, the closedness assumption can be dropped.

Remark 1.9
.

Under an additional, most likely technical, condition, Theorem
1.7
generalizes to
\RCD(0,n)
spaces which are topological
n
-manifolds, see Theorem
4.1
below for the precise statement.

While Theorem
1.7
yields new topological restrictions to the existence of complete metrics with
\Ric\geq 0
in all dimensions, see Proposition
6
below, our most direct application is to the
3
-dimensional case. G. Bargagnati and R. Frigerio proved in
[
9
]
that a contractible
3
-manifold with vanishing simplicial volume must be homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Combining their result with Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.2
, we obtain a new proof of the topological classification of open
3
-manifolds with
\Ric\geq 0
. Such proof is independent of minimal surfaces methods. More generally, we can classify
\RCD(0,3)
spaces whose underlying space is a topological
3
-manifold up to homeomorphism.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{3})
be a non-compact
\RCD(0,3)
space and assume that
X
is a topological manifold. Then either
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or its universal cover splits a line isometrically. In the second case,
X
admits a complete smooth metric with nonnegative sectional curvature.

Theorem
1.10
answers a question raised by G. Besson around seven years ago and advertised in various occasions since then. See, for instance,
[
12
, Question 4.1]
,
[
13
, Question 2.10]
and
[
88
, Section 6.5.2]
. The result is original also in the case of noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces
(M^{3}_{i},g_{i},p_{i})\to(X,\mathsf{d})
with
\Ric_{i}\geq-1/i
and
\mathrm{vol}(B_{1}(p_{i}))>v>0
for every
i\geq 1
and some
v>0
.

We expect that the ideas that we introduce for the proof of Theorem
1.10
will find applications in the study of collapsing
3
-manifolds under a lower Ricci curvature bound. For the sake of motivation, we note that the classification of open
3
-dimensional Alexandrov spaces with nonnegative curvature plays a key role in T. Shioya and T. Yamaguchi’s study of collapsing
3
-manifolds under a lower sectional curvature bound
[
83
]
. See also
[
20
,
11
,
60
,
73
]
.

Combining Theorem
1.10
with the recent works on orientability of
\RCD
spaces
[
14
,
36
]
, one obtains the full classification of non-collapsed
\RCD(0,3)
spaces without boundary:

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{3})
be an
\RCD(0,3)
space without boundary. Then, either
(X,\mathsf{d})
is an Alexandrov space of nonnegative sectional curvature, or
X
is homeomorphic to one among:
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
C(\mathbb{RP}^{2})
, a spherical 3-manifold, a spherical suspension over
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
.

Remark 1.12
.

Regarding the more general case of
\CD(0,3)
manifolds, when we drop the infinitesimally Hilbertian assumption, the classification remains open, although we can obtain partial progress with our methods, in particular in the contractible case. The classification remains an open question also for
\RCD(0,3)
spaces with nonempty boundary.

1.2.
Discussion of the key new ideas

The proof of Theorem
1.2
hinges on two main steps. The first step is a blow-down argument. It is not difficult to check that the every equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff blow-down of
(\overline{M},\overline{g},\overline{p},\mathbb{Z}^{n-2})
is an
\RCD(0,n)
space whose isomorphisms group contains a closed
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
subgroup. Here
(\overline{M},\overline{g},\overline{p},\mathbb{Z}^{n-2})
is the universal cover of
(M,g)
endowed with the action by deck transformations of
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}<\pi_{1}(M)
and
\overline{p}\in\overline{M}
is any reference point. See Lemma
2.1
below for the precise statement, which is essentially
[
77
, Lemma 3.1]
. Our first key ingredient is the following splitting result:

Proposition 1.13
.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\widetilde{q},G)
denote any equivariant blow-down of
(\overline{M},\overline{g},\overline{p},\mathbb{Z}^{n-2})
. Then
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\widetilde{q},G)
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
isometrically and
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}<G
acts by translations on this Euclidean factor.

Proposition
1.2
will follow from a more general splitting theorem via
\mathbb{R}^{k}
action, valid for any
\RCD(0,N)
space:

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space satisfying the following conditions for some integer
1\leq k\leq N
:

(1)

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq k+1
, where
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)
denotes the essential (also known as rectifiable) dimension of
X
.

(2)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{k+1}}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))}\,dt=+\infty
for some
p\in X
.

(3)

There exists a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Then,
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
as metric measure space for some
\RCD(0,N-k)
space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y},\mathfrak{m}_{Y})
.

Remark 1.15
.

There is no analogue of Proposition
1.2
under the weaker assumption that there is an effective action of
\mathbb{R}^{n-3}
by measure preserving isometries, even in the case of complete Riemannian
n
-manifolds with
\Ric\geq 0
. Indeed, in
[
56
, p. 913]
A. Kasue and T. Washio constructed a complete
(M^{4},g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
which does not split any line isometrically, although its isometry group contains a closed
\mathbb{R}
subgroup. More recently, J. Pan and G. Wei constructed examples of
\RCD(0,n)
spaces with rectifiable dimension
2
which do not split any line isometrically although the group of measure preserving isometries contains a closed
\mathbb{R}
subgroup; see
[
76
]
and their subsequent joint work with X. Dai and S. Honda where the construction was made more explicit. Their examples illustrate the role of the
\RCD
dimension as opposed to the rectifiable dimension in Proposition
1.2
.

The proof of Proposition
1.2
relies on some recently developed
\RCD
technology. The most delicate case is when the blow-down is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and has rectifiable dimension
n-1
. We are going to argue that
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
is a measured warped product, i.e., ignoring the possible nonsmoothness, the metric has the form
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{2}+f^{2}(r)g_{\mathbb{R}^{n-2}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}=h(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}
, for some functions
f,h:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The
\RCD(0,n)
condition in this setting is equivalent to a lower bound on the weighted Ricci tensor interpreted in the sense of distributions, thanks to
[
71
]
. Such distributional lower Ricci bound yields some restrictions on
f
and
h
which, eventually, imply that
f
must be constant. Hence
\widetilde{X}
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.

Remark 1.16
.

In general, the splitting of all blow-downs of a complete manifold with
\Ric\geq 0
does not imply the splitting of the original manifold. Kasue and Washio’s construction from
[
56
, p. 913-914]
was the first counterexample. Later Anderson observed that the
n
-dimensional Schwarzschild metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{n-2}
for
n\geq 4
are Ricci-flat counterexamples, cf. with
[
4
, p. 71]
. Counterexamples can be constructed in dimension
3
as well by using the methods developed by T.-H. Colding and A. Naber in
[
31
]
.

Remark 1.17
.

For the sake of comparison, we note that the splitting of blow-downs, which is a key step of the proof in the general case, is an immediate consequence of the cone-splitting principle under the assumption that the universal cover has Euclidean volume growth. This is the case considered in the recent preprint
[
53
]
.

That we can obtain the splitting of the universal cover starting from the splitting of the blow-downs is very much special to our situation.
Thanks to the Cheeger-Colding theory, see in particular
[
24
,
26
]
, the following holds. If a blow-down of a complete
(M^{n},g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{k}
isometrically for some
0\leq k\leq n-1
, then there exist two sequences
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
,
R_{i}\to\infty
and points
p_{i}\in M
such that

(1.1)

\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{GH}}\left(B_{r}(p_{i}),B^{\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y_{r,i}}_{r}((0,q_{r,i}))\right)<\varepsilon_{i}r\,,\,\,\text{for every}\,\,i\in\mathbb{N}\,\,\text{and }\,\,0<r<R_{i}\,,

where
Y_{r,i}
denotes some complete metric space,
q_{r,i}\in Y_{r,i}
, and
\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{GH}}
denotes the Gromov-Hausdorff distance. We will argue that the points
p_{i}
can be chosen to remain at a uniformly bounded distance from some fixed point
p\in M
as
i\to\infty
and this is clearly enough to complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
Ruling out the possibility that the points
p_{i}
escape to infinity requires two new ideas. The first one is the use of J. Cheeger and Naber’s slicing theorem, see
[
28
, Theorem 1.23]
. Such tool crucially refines the general picture that we sketched above under the assumption that
k=n-2
. Very imprecisely, it guarantees that if the points
p_{i}
escape to infinity, this can only happen in the directions of the (approximate)
(n-2)
-splitting. To deal with this scenario, the second key tool is a careful study of the behaviour of
(n-2)
linearly independent harmonic functions with almost linear growth under compositions with the isometric action by
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
. The existence of such functions follows from Proposition
1.2
together with
[
52
, Theorem 1.10]
, obtained by H. Huang and X.-T. Huang by developing the methods previously introduced by Cheeger, W. Jiang, and Naber in
[
27
]
.

Remark 1.18
.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
goes in the
opposite direction
with respect to the proof of the analogous result in dimension
3
in
[
63
]
. Indeed, in
[
63
]
an infinitesimal splitting originally obtained along a stable minimal hypersurface is propagated through the whole manifold. In our case we first obtain the splitting at infinity, i.e., at the level of blow-downs, and then upgrade such information to a splitting at finite scales.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
hinges on a general vanishing theorem for the simplicial volume of noncompact manifolds originally stated by Gromov in
[
46
, p. 58]
with a sketch of proof, and proved in all details more recently by Frigerio and M. Moraschini in
[
39
, Theorem 9, Corollary 11]
. Their result, stated below as Theorem
4.6
, guarantees that the simplicial volume of an open triangulable
n
-manifold
M
vanishes if one can construct a countable open cover of
M
with relatively compact domains which is amenable at infinity (see Definition
4
below for the relevant terminology) and has multiplicity less than or equal to
n
. To construct such cover in our setting we distinguish two cases. If the volume growth of
(M^{n},g)
is Euclidean, then the cover basically consists of metric annuli. To obtain amenability at infinity we show that the relative fundamental group of a large metric annulus inside a slightly larger one is always finite in this setup, see Theorem
4.8
below for the precise statement. The proof relies on a contradiction argument based on the Cheeger-Colding theory. When the volume growth is not maximal the construction of the sought cover is more delicate. We are going to combine a conformal deformation argument together with a general result due to P. Papasoglu in
[
79
]
(motivated by some earlier work of L. Guth
[
48
,
49
]
and Gromov’s
[
46
]
) to show that
(M^{n},g)
has small
(n-1)
-Uryson width. This helps us to build a geometrically well-behaved cover of
M
with multiplicity
\leq n
. The amenability at infinity of such cover will follow from V. Kapovitch and B. Wilking’s Margulis lemma for manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature from
[
55
]
.

The extension of Theorem
1.7
to triangulable
\RCD(0,n)
manifolds follows from the same strategy. The only point one has to be careful about is the application of the Margulis lemma. While a Margulis lemma for
\RCD
spaces has been recently obtained in
[
37
]
, the local version corresponding to
[
55
, Theorem 1]
is not yet available in the literature. In Appendix
A
we obtain a local Margulis lemma for
\RCD(K,N)
spaces (and, more generally, for essentially nonbranching and semi-locally simply connected
\CD(K,N)
spaces) where the bound on the index of the nilpotent subgroup is not effective. Despite being weaker than the statement obtained in the smooth case in
[
55
]
, our result, whose proof relies on
[
16
]
, is sufficient for the purposes of the present paper.

[
37
]
.

\backslash
R1

2.
Preliminaries

In a metric space
(X,\mathsf{d})
, we denote by

\mathsf{Lip}(X)
the space of all Lipschitz functions. Moreover, for a given Lipschitz function
\varphi
, we denote by
\mathsf{L}(\varphi)
the global Lipschitz constant and by
\lip(\varphi)(\cdot)
the local Lipschitz constant

(2.1)

\lip(\varphi)(x):=\limsup_{y\to x}\frac{|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)|}{\mathsf{d}(x,y)}\,.

In the following,
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
denotes the topological group of isometries of a metric space
X
. When working on a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, the notation
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
will denote the collection of
measure-preserving
isometries of
X
.

2.1.
Equivariant Gromov Hausdorff convergence

We recall some basic facts about equivariant pointed Gromov Hausdorff convergence, the main references being
[
40
,
41
]
.
In the next definition, a quadruple
(Y,\mathsf{d},G,p)
consists of a pointed metric space
(Y,\mathsf{d},p)
(which is assumed to be a complete length space), and a closed subgroup
G\subset\mathrm{Iso}(Y)
. We denote
G(R):=\{g\in G:\mathsf{d}(p,gp)\leq R\}
.

Definition 2.1
.

[Equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff convergence]
Two spaces
(Y_{1},\mathsf{d}_{1},y_{1},G_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathsf{d}_{2},y_{2},G_{2})
are
\varepsilon
-close in the
equivariant pointed Gromov-Hausdorff
sense, if there exists a triple
(f,\psi,\varphi)
of maps

f:B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1})\to B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{2})\,,\,\,\varphi:G_{1}(1/\varepsilon)\to G_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\,,\,\,\psi:G_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\to G_{1}(1/\varepsilon)\,,

with the following properties:

(1)

f(y_{1})=y_{2}
;

(2)

f(B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1}))
is an
\varepsilon
-net in
B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{2})
;

(3)

|\mathsf{d}_{2}(f(a),f(b))-\mathsf{d}_{1}(a,b)|<\varepsilon
for every
a,b\in B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1})
;

(4)

If
g\in G_{1}(1/\varepsilon)
, and
x,gx\in B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1})
, then
\mathsf{d}_{2}(f(gx),\varphi(g)f(x))<\varepsilon
;

(5)

If
h\in G_{2}(1/\varepsilon)
, and
x,\psi(h)x\in B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1})
, then
\mathsf{d}_{2}(f(\psi(h)x),hf(x))<\varepsilon
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(X_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},G_{i},x_{i})
be a sequence converging in equivariant pGH sense to
(X,\mathsf{d},x,G)
. Let
\gamma_{i}\in G_{i}
be such that
\gamma_{i}\cdot x_{i}
converges to
y\in X
in the space realizing the pGH convergence. Then there exists
\gamma\in G
such that for every
y\in X
and every sequence
y_{i}\to y
, it holds
\gamma_{i}\cdot y_{i}\to\gamma\cdot y
. In this case we say that
\gamma_{i}\to\gamma
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
(Y_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},y_{i})
be a sequence of metric spaces converging in pGH sense to
(X,\mathsf{d},x)
. Let
G_{i}
be closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Iso}(Y_{i})
. Then, up to a not relabelled subsequence, there exists a closed subgroup
G<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
such that

(Y_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},G_{i},y_{i})\to(X,\mathsf{d},G,x)

in equivariant pGH sense. Moreover,

(Y_{i}/G_{i},{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{/\sim},[y_{i}])\to(X/G,{\mathsf{d}}_{/\sim},[x])

in pGH sense.

In the next definition, a quintuple
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},G,p)
consists of a pointed metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},p)
and a closed subgroup
G\subset\mathrm{Iso}(X)
.

Definition 2.4

(Equivariant measured Gromov-Hausdorff convergence)
.

Two metric measure spaces
(Y_{1},\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},G_{1},y_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},G_{2},y_{2})
are
\varepsilon
-close in the
equivariant ponted measured Gromov-Hausdorff
sense, if there exists a triple
(f,\psi,\varphi)
of maps

f:B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1})\to B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{2}),\,\,\varphi:G_{1}(1/\varepsilon)\to G_{2}(1/\varepsilon),\,\,\psi:G_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\to G_{1}(1/\varepsilon)\,,

satisfying the properties of Definition
2.1
and such that
f_{\#}(\mathfrak{m}_{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{1}))
is
\varepsilon
-close to
\mathfrak{m}_{2}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B_{1/\varepsilon}(y_{2})
w.r.t. to weak convergence in
Y_{2}
. We stress that, in this case, the groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are required to act by measure-preserving isometries.

Combining Gromov’s Precompactness Theorem, the stability of the
\RCD(K,N)
condition under pGH convergence
[
44
]
, and a simple argument which relies on Theorem
2.3
, one obtains the following.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(Y_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},\mathfrak{m}_{i},y_{i})
be a sequence of normalized
\RCD(K,N)
spaces, and let
G_{i}
be closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Iso}(Y_{i})
. Then, up to passing to a subsequence, there exists an
\RCD(K,N)
space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x)
, and a closed subgroup
G<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
, such that

(Y_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},\mathfrak{m}_{i},G_{i},y_{i})\to(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},G,x)

in equivariant pmGH sense.

In the next lemmas, when we say that a subgroup
G<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
(or
\mathbb{R}
), we implicitly mean that
G
is equipped with the topology induced by
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
, that
\mathbb{Z}
(or
\mathbb{R}
) has its natural topology, and that there exists an isomorphism of topological groups
\varphi:G\to\mathbb{Z}
(or
\varphi:G\to\mathbb{R}
). We denote this by writing
G\cong\mathbb{Z}
(or
G\cong\mathbb{R}
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
. Let
\mathbb{R}^{k}\cong G<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
be a closed subgroup. Then, the orbit
Gp\subset X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to G
be the isomorphism of topological groups given by the statement. Since
G
acts freely, the map
\psi:G\to Gp\subset X
is an homeomorphism, so that
\psi\circ\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to Gp
is a homeomorphism as well.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(X_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},\mathfrak{m}_{i},x_{i})
be a sequence of normalized
\RCD(K,N)
spaces. Let
\mathbb{Z}^{k}\cong G_{i}<\mathrm{Iso}(X_{i})
be closed subgroups with generators
\{\gamma_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
such that
\mathsf{d}_{i}(x_{i},\gamma_{i,j}\cdot x_{i})\to 0
as
i\uparrow\infty
, for every
j\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Then, up to passing to a subsequence, one has equivariant pmGH convergence

(X,\mathsf{d}_{i},\mathfrak{m}_{i},G_{i},x_{i})\to(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},G,x)

for a closed subgroup
G<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
such that
G
contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as
[
77
, Lemma 3.1]
using that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
is a Lie Group by
[
47
]
or
[
86
]
.
∎

2.2.
Some properties of
\RCD
spaces

We recall some specific properties of
\RCD(K,N)
spaces that we will use later on in the paper.

Definition 2.8

(Tangent spaces to an
\RCD(K,N)
space)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space, and consider a sequence
r_{i}\downarrow 0
. For every
x\in X
, by the compactness of
\RCD(K,N)
spaces w.r.t. pmGH convergence, up to a subsequence, the spaces
(X,\mathsf{d}/r_{i},\mathfrak{m}(B_{r_{i}}(x))^{-1}\mathfrak{m},x)
converge in pmGH sense to a limiting
\RCD(0,N)
space
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},x_{\infty})
. Such
X_{\infty}
is called a
tangent space to
X
in
x
. The set of tangent spaces of
X
in
x
is denoted
\mathrm{Tan}_{x}(X)

Definition 2.9

(Tangent cones at infinity of an
\RCD(0,N)
space)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x)
be a pointed
\RCD(0,N)
space, and consider a sequence
r_{i}\uparrow+\infty
. Up to a subsequence, the spaces
(X,\mathsf{d}/r_{i},\mathfrak{m}(B_{r_{i}}(x))^{-1}\mathfrak{m},x)
converge in pmGH sense to a limiting
\RCD(0,N)
space
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},x_{\infty})
. Such
X_{\infty}
is called a
tangent cone at infinity
(or
blow-down
) of
X
.

The proof of the next lemma is the same as
[
67
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space. Then, the set of its tangent cones at infinity is connected in the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff topology.

Similarly, given a pointed
\RCD(K,N)
space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x)
, the set
\mathrm{Tan}_{x}(X)
is connected in the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff topology.

The next definition is given in
[
23
]
for PI spaces.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space, and let
x\in X
be a point with
\mathrm{Tan}_{x}(X)=\{\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. A function
f\in\Lip(X)
is differentiable in
x
if the following happens. Let
r_{i}\downarrow 0
,
\delta_{i}\downarrow 0
, and let

\psi_{i}:(\overline{B}_{r_{i}}^{X}(x),\mathsf{d}/r_{i})\to(\overline{B}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(0),\mathsf{d}_{eu})\,,\quad\quad\varphi_{i}:(\overline{B}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(0),\mathsf{d}_{eu})\to(\overline{B}_{r_{i}}^{X}(x),\mathsf{d}/r_{i})

be
\delta_{i}
-GH approximations. Then, up to passing to a subsequence, there exists a linear function
f_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lip(f_{\infty})=\lip(f)(x)
, such that

\Big\|\frac{f-f(x)}{r_{i}}-f_{\infty}\circ\psi_{i}\Big\|_{\infty,\overline{B}^{X}_{r_{i}}(x)}+\Big\|\frac{f\circ\varphi_{i}-f(x)}{r_{i}}-f_{\infty}\Big\|_{\infty,\overline{B}^{\mathbb{R}^{d}}_{1}(0)}\to 0\,.

The next theorem follows from
[
23
, Theorem 10.2]
.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space, and let
f\in\Lip(X)
. Then,
f
is differentiable at
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
.

The following theorem follows from the proof of
[
72
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a
\RCD(0,n)
space such that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
acting cocompactly. Then,
X
splits off a factor
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

The next lemma follows repeating the proof of
[
93
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space such that all of its tangent cones at infinity are
1
-dimensional, and one tangent cone at infinity is
\mathbb{R}
. Then,
X\cong\mathbb{R}\times Y
isometrically for some compact metric space
Y
.

The following lemma is well-known, thus its proof will be omitted.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space. If
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
and
Y
contains no lines, then
\mathrm{Iso}(X)<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\times\mathrm{Iso}(Y)
.

The next theorem follows from
[
52
, Theorem 1.10]
.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space such that all its tangent cones at infinity are isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
where
Y
is some metric space that contains no lines. Then there exists a harmonic map
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}):X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
vanishing in
p\in X
such that

(1)

\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}\}
is a basis for

\displaystyle\Big\{v\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{loc}(X):\Delta v=0,\text{and for every }\varepsilon>0\text{ there exist }R_{\varepsilon},C_{\varepsilon}>0:

\displaystyle v(x)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathsf{d}(x,p)^{1+\varepsilon}+C_{\varepsilon}\text{ for every }x\in X\Big\}.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
R>R_{\varepsilon}
there exists a positive definite lower triangular matrix
T_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, such that
T_{R}u:B_{R}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an
\varepsilon
-splitting map.

3.
First Betti number rigidity

The goal of this section is to prove the following splitting-type theorem for
\RCD(0,n)
spaces:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
. If
\pi_{1}(X)
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
then the universal cover of
X
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
as a metric measure space.

It is clear that Theorem
3.1
implies the corresponding result for complete Riemannian manifolds that we stated in the introduction as Theorem
1.2
.

The first key ingredient towards the proof of Theorem
3.1
is the following splitting theorem for the blow-downs of
X
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\widetilde{q},G)
denote any equivariant blow-down of the universal cover
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{n},\overline{p},\mathbb{Z}^{n-2})
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
, endowed with the action of
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}<\pi_{1}(X)
by deck transformations. Then
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\widetilde{q},G)
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
as a metric measure space and there is a closed subgroup
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}<G
acting by translations on such Euclidean factor.

A corollary of Proposition
3
is that the blow-down of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is unique as a metric space. Such information is crucial for the proof of the following:

Proposition 3.3
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
, there exist linearly independent nonconstant harmonic functions
u_{1},\dots,u_{n-2}:\overline{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the following hold:

i)

for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
|u_{i}(x)|\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathsf{d}(x,\overline{p})^{1+\varepsilon}+C_{\varepsilon}
for every
x\in\overline{X}
and
i=1,\dots,n-2
, i.e., the harmonic functions have almost linear growth;

ii)

for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
R_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
r>R_{\varepsilon}
there is a lower triangular
(n-2)\times(n-2)
matrix
T_{r,\varepsilon}
such that
T_{r,\varepsilon}\circ U:B_{r}(\overline{p})\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
is an
\varepsilon
-splitting map. Here we set
U:=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n-2}):\overline{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
;

iii)

the map
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\ni\gamma\mapsto U(\gamma(\overline{p}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
is an injective group homomorphism;

iv)

if
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then

(3.1)

\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\left(T_{r,\varepsilon}\circ U(B_{1}(\overline{p}))\right)}{r^{n-2}}>0\,.

Items i) and ii) of Proposition
3
follow from
[
52
, Theorem 1.10]
, taking into account the metric uniqueness of the blow-down which follows from Proposition
3
(see Corollary
3.1
). On the other hand, the proofs of items iii) and iv) require some new ideas. The
non-degeneracy
condition (
3.1
), together with the almost-splitting property (with arbitrary precision) up to linear transformation in ii) and the equivariance from iii) play a key role in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Remark 3.4
.

For the sake of clarity we note that a posteriori the functions
u_{i}
could just be taken to be the coordinates of the
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
factor of
\overline{X}
. It is straightforward to check that with such choice items i)–iv) hold and one can choose all the matrices
T_{r,\varepsilon}=\mathrm{Id}
. However, at this stage of the proof we do not know that
\overline{X}
splits a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
yet.

We will prove Proposition
3
in Section
3.1
below. Proposition
3
is a slightly imprecise combination of Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
3.2
, which are the bulk of Section
3.2
. In Section
3.3
we will combine these tools to complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

3.1.
Splitting of the blow-downs

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
3
. The proof is much simpler if one restricts to the case where the universal covering of the space in question has Euclidean volume growth. In such case, which is the one dealt with in the recent
[
53
]
, the sought splitting follows from the so-called cone-splitting principle.
To treat the remaining cases, the key step is to prove the following splitting theorem via
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-action.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space satisfying the following conditions for some integer
1\leq k\leq N
:

(1)

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq k+1
.

(2)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{k+1}}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))}\,dt=+\infty
for some
p\in X
.

(3)

There exists a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Then,
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
as metric measure spaces for some
\RCD(0,N-k)
space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y},\mathfrak{m}_{Y})
.

As we shall see, Theorem
3.5
easily implies the following uniqueness and structure theorem for the blow-downs of the universal cover in the setting of Proposition
3
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
. If
\pi_{1}(X)
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
, then the blow-down of the universal cover
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{n})
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
is unique as a (pointed) metric space and (pointed) isometric to a metric cone (pointed at a tip).

Assuming the validity of Theorem
3.5
and Corollary
3.1
, whose proofs will be discussed later in this section, we complete the proof of Proposition
3
.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

We assume first that
\overline{X}
has Euclidean volume growth. Then every blow-down
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{q})
is an
n
-dimensional metric cone with tip
\widetilde{q}
by
[
33
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
34
, Theorem 1.3]
after
[
24
, Theorem 7.6]
. By Lemma
2.1
, there exists a closed subgroup of
G
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
which acts freely on
\widetilde{X}
since the only compact subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
is the trivial group. Hence, the set of tips of
\widetilde{X}
contains a subset homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
, so that
\widetilde{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times C(Z)
isometrically, by cone-splitting, where
Z
is either isometric to a circle
S^{1}_{r}
for
0<r<1
or to an interval
[0,a]
for
0<a\leq\pi
.

Assume now that
\overline{X}
does not have Euclidean volume growth. In this case, we have that
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(\widetilde{X})\leq\dim_{\mathscr{H}}(\widetilde{X})\leq n-1
, by
[
34
, Theorem 1.4]
after
[
25
, Theorem 3.1]
. By Lemma
2.1
there exists a closed subgroup of
G
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
. Moreover, by the Bishop-Gromov inequality, there exists
c>0
such that

(3.2)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(B_{r}(\widetilde{q}))\leq cr^{n}\quad\text{for every }r>1\,.

Hence, Theorem
3.5
applies with
k=n-2
thus yielding that
\widetilde{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
as metric measure space.

By Corollary
3.1
, the blow-down of
\overline{X}
is unique as a metric space and isometric to a metric cone. The second part of the statement follows immediately from
[
75
, Theorem 3.2]
. We note that such result is stated for smooth complete Riemannian manifolds therein but the proof works verbatim in the
\RCD
case.
∎

We are going to prove Theorem
3.5
by induction on the dimension
k
of the group
\mathbb{R}^{k}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
. The most challenging part of the proof is the base step of the induction, corresponding to
k=1
.
As shown by Proposition
3.1.1
, we can easily reduce to the case where
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
isometrically.
The proof of the splitting theorem in this case consists of two main steps.
The first step of the proof is to show that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

is isomorphic to a measured warped product
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R},\mathsf{d}_{\varphi},h(s)\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)\mathscr{L}^{1}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t))
for some
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},(0,+\infty])
and
h\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,+\infty))
. Establishing such structural result is the goal of Section
3.1.1

(see in particular Proposition
3.1.1
).
In Section
3.1.2
,
using the assumption that
X
has non-negative Ricci curvature in synthetic sense, we deduce that the warping factor
\varphi
is superharmonic on a suitably weighted half-line. Such weighted half-line turns out to have slow volume growth, thanks to assumption (2), thus forcing the warping factor
\varphi
to be constant and yielding the sought splitting (see in particular Proposition
3.33
). In Section
3.1.3
we deal with the inductive step. In Section
3.1.4
we exploit Theorem
3.5
to prove Corollary
3.1
.

3.1.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.5
, base step: measured warped product structure

Our setup in this section is that we consider an
\RCD(0,N)
space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
such that
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq 2
,

(3.3)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=+\infty\,,

for some
p\in X
and there is a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. To complete the proof of the base step of
Theorem
3.5
we are going to argue that
X
splits a line isometrically. To do so, as mentioned above, our first goal is to show that
X
is a measured warped product. Such result holds independently of the volume growth assumption (
3.3
). More precisely, we are going to prove the following.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space with
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq 2
such that there is a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Then either
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
splits a line or there exist an increasing function
h\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and a continuous and strictly positive function
\varphi:(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
such that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},H)
is equivariantly isomorphic to a measured warped product on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
with metric and measure given respectively by

(3.4)

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\varphi(r)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{2}\,,\quad\quad h(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

and with the
\mathbb{R}
-action being by translation on the
x
component.

Note that the conclusion of Proposition
3.1.1
would be pretty standard if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is assumed to be a smooth weighted Riemannian manifold. Such assumption is unfair in our setup, and this is a source of several headaches. Indeed, we will be forced to prove the measured warped product structure simultaneously with some regularity of the warping function
\varphi
and weight function
h
.

Remark 3.8
.

On any (sufficiently regular) measured warped product with the structure in (
3.4
), the projection onto the Euclidean component
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a harmonic function. Such observation is at the root of all the subsequent arguments in this section.

Remark 3.9
.

Proposition
3.1.1
generalizes in particular the structural results obtained in the recent
[
32
]
. In their case,
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is the blow-down of a complete Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with
\Ric\geq 0
and the explicit form of the metric
g
was exploited to obtain the measured warped product structure of the blow-down.

We start by observing that there is a natural
\RCD
structure on the quotient space
X/H
.

Remark 3.10

(Quotients of
\RCD
spaces by a group isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space and let
\mathbb{R}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
be a closed subgroup. Consider the discrete subgroup
\mathbb{Z}<H\cong\mathbb{R}
generated by
1\in\mathbb{R}
. We consider the quotient space
X/\mathbb{Z}
equipped with the quotient distance
\mathsf{d}^{\prime}
and the unique measure
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
such that the projection
\pi:X\to X/\mathbb{Z}
is locally measure preserving. Since
\pi
is locally an isomorphism of metric measure spaces, the quotient space
(X/\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{d}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
is locally
\RCD(K,N)
. Thus, by the local to global property
[
21
,
62
]
,
(X/\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{d}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
is
\RCD(K,N)
as well.

Consider the action of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
on
X/\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
is a compact subgroup of
\mathrm{Iso}(X/\mathbb{Z})
, thanks to
[
42
]
, the quotient space
(X/\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is an
\RCD(K,N)
space when equipped with the quotient distance
\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime}
and the quotient measure
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover the metric space
((X/\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}),\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime})
is isomorphic to
X/\mathbb{R}
with its natural quotient distance.

We continue with the following observation regarding the dimension of the quotient of an
\RCD
space with respect to an action of
\mathbb{R}
by measure preserving isometries.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space such that
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
is a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.
Consider the
\RCD(K,N)
structure
(X/H,\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
on the quotient space
X/H
given by Remark
3.1.1
.
Then, we have

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X/H)\leq\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)-1\,.

Proof.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
be the essential dimension of
(X/H,\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
and let
d^{\prime}
be the essential dimension of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
. Call
\pi:X\to X/H
the projection map. By
[
70
]
, there exists
x\in X
such that
\mathrm{Tan}_{x}(X)=\{\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}\}
and
\mathrm{Tan}_{\pi(x)}(X/H)=\{\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. By Lemma
2.1
, for a sequence
r_{i}\uparrow+\infty
, we have the pointed equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff convergence

(X,r_{i}\mathsf{d},H,x)\to(\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eu}},G,0)\,,

where
G<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}})
, and
G
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.
By Theorem
2.3
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}/G
, so that
d\leq d^{\prime}-1
, as claimed.
∎

We record two well-known consequences of the splitting theorem for
\RCD(0,N)
spaces from
[
43
]
. Since their proofs are standard we omit them.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space with a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
such that
X/H
is homeomorphic (and hence isometric) to
\mathbb{R}
. Then, there exists a splitting
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}
such that the
H
-action is trivial on the
\mathbb{R}
factor.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a non-compact
\RCD(0,N)
space such that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
contains a closed subgroup
H
, with
X/H
compact. Then,
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}
as metric measure spaces.

Combining Lemma
3.1.1
, Lemma
3.1.1
, Lemma
3.1.1
and the classification of
\RCD
spaces with essential dimension less than or equal to one obtained in
[
59
]
, we immediately obtain the following.

Lemma 3.14
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Proposition
3.1.1
,
either
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}
as a metric measure space and the
H
action is by translation on the second factor, or
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
as a metric space.

Thanks to Lemma
3.1.1
, to complete the proof of Proposition
3.1.1
we can assume without loss of generality that
X/H
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This is the most delicate case in the proof, and it is our focus for the rest of the section.
The next two lemmas show that, in this case,
X
admits an explicit and natural parametrization (as a set) with a map
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space with a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
such that
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
as metric spaces. Then there exists a ray
r:[0,+\infty)\subset X
such that

(3.5)

X=\bigcup_{t\geq 0}(H\cdot r(t))\,,

and

(3.6)

\mathsf{d}(H\cdot r(t),H\cdot r(s))=|t-s|\quad\text{ for every }t,s\geq 0\,.

In particular, the canonical projection to the quotient space
\pi:X\to X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
coincides with the distance
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,H\cdot r(0)):X\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
from the orbit of
r(0)
.

Proof.

Consider the canonical projection map
\pi:X\to X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Fix
s>0
, and let
H\cdot x_{0}
and
H\cdot x_{s}
be the preimages of
0
and
s
via
\pi
.
By definition of quotient distance, we have that

s=\mathsf{d}(H\cdot x_{0},H\cdot x_{s})\,.

It is elementary to check
that there exists a geodesic
\gamma_{s}:[0,s]\to X
realizing the distance between
H\cdot x_{0}
and
H\cdot x_{s}
. Modulo translating
\gamma_{s}
with an element of
H
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\gamma_{s}(0)=x_{0}
.

We claim that with such choice
\pi(\gamma_{s}(t))=t
for every
t\in[0,s]
.
To this aim observe that
\pi:X\to X/H
is a
1
-Lipschitz map, so that
\pi\circ\gamma_{s}
is a
1
-Lipschitz curve. At the same time
\pi(\gamma_{s}(0))=0
and
\pi(\gamma_{s}(s))=s
, so that
\pi(\gamma_{s}(t))=t
for every
t\in[0,s]
. It follows that

\mathsf{d}(H\cdot\gamma_{s}(t_{1}),H\cdot\gamma_{s}(t_{2}))=|t_{1}-t_{2}|\quad\text{for every }t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,s]\,.

Choose a sequence
s_{i}\uparrow+\infty
and consider the corresponding geodesics
\gamma_{s_{i}}\subset X
. Modulo passing to a subsequence, they converge to a limiting ray
r:[0,+\infty)\to X
, with the property that
\pi(r(t))=t
for every
t\geq 0
and

\mathsf{d}(H\cdot r(t_{1}),H\cdot r(t_{2}))=|t_{1}-t_{2}|\quad\text{for every }t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\,.

Since the image of
\pi\circ r
is the whole
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, also (
3.5
) follows.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space with a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
such that
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Let
r\subset X
be the ray given by Lemma
3.1.1
. Then the map

\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X\,,\quad\Phi(t,s):=t\cdot r(s)

is bijective. Moreover, denoting by
\mathsf{d}(\cdot,H\cdot r(0))
the distance from the orbit of
r(0)
in
X
, the inverse
\Psi:=\Phi^{-1}:X\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be written as
\Psi(x)=(f(x),\mathsf{d}(x,H\cdot r(0))
for some function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore,
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
is equivariant with respect to the
H\cong\mathbb{R}
-action on
X
and the standard
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\mathbb{R}
by translations.

Proof.

The map
\Phi
is surjective by (
3.5
). Thus we only need to check the injectivity. Let
(t_{1},s_{1}),(t_{2},s_{2})\in H\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such that

t_{1}\cdot r(s_{1})=t_{2}\cdot r(s_{2})\,.

Then
r(s_{1})
and
r(s_{2})
belong to the same orbit of
H
in
X
. Thus
s_{1}=s_{2}
by (
3.6
). Since the action of
H
is free (because the only compact subgroup of
H
is the trivial group), it follows,
t_{1}=t_{2}
, thus concluding the proof that
\Phi
is bijective. To prove the representation formula for the inverse
\Psi:=\Phi^{-1}
we observe that, given any
x=\Phi(t,s)=t\cdot r(s)
,

(3.7)

\mathsf{d}(x,H\cdot r(0))=\mathsf{d}(t\cdot r(s),H\cdot r(0))=\mathsf{d}(H\cdot r(s),H\cdot r(0))=s\,,

where we applied (
3.6
) for the last equality.
∎

Our next key goal is to show that the function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
above is harmonic. Such conclusion, and its proof, are of course motivated by the observation we made in Remark
3.1.1
. Nevertheless, the full argument is quite technical and it requires a few intermediate steps. We start from a mild regularity result for
f
. It will be our starting point for the forthcoming computations.

Lemma 3.17
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be the map given by Lemma
3.1.1
. Then,
f\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
K\subset\subset X
and assume by contradiction that there exist distinct points
x_{i},y_{i}\in K
such that

(3.8)

\frac{|f(x_{i})-f(y_{i})|}{\mathsf{d}(x_{i},y_{i})}\to+\infty\quad\text{as }i\uparrow+\infty\,.

Using the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
, we can write
x_{i}=t_{i}^{x}\cdot r(s_{i}^{x})
and
y_{i}=t_{i}^{y}\cdot r(s_{i}^{y})
for some
t_{i}^{x},t_{i}^{y}\in\mathbb{R}
and
s_{i}^{x},s_{i}^{y}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, so that (
3.8
) reads as

(3.9)

\frac{\mathsf{d}(x_{i},y_{i})}{|t_{i}^{x}-t_{i}^{y}|}\to 0\quad\text{as }i\uparrow+\infty\,.

By the equivariance of
f
we can assume without loss of generality that
t_{i}^{y}=0
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, we can also assume that
t_{i}^{x}>0
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
By the triangle inequality, we get that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}(r(s_{i}^{y}),1\cdot r(s_{i}^{y}))\leq

\displaystyle\Bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Bigg\rfloor\mathsf{d}\Big(r(s^{y}_{i}),(t_{i}^{x})\cdot r(s^{y}_{i})\Big)

(3.11)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{d}\Bigg(\Bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Bigg\rfloor t_{i}^{x}\cdot r(s^{y}_{i}),1\cdot r(s^{y}_{i})\Bigg)\,.

We claim that the right-hand side converges to zero as
i\uparrow+\infty
. If the claim holds, we readily get a contradiction using that
H\cong\mathbb{R}
acts freely on
X
.
To prove the claim, using (
3.6
) we can control the first summand as

(3.12)

\displaystyle\Bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Bigg\rfloor\mathsf{d}\Big(r(s^{y}_{i}),t_{i}^{x}\cdot r(s^{y}_{i})\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Big(\mathsf{d}(x_{i},y_{i})+\mathsf{d}(r(s_{i}^{x}),r(s_{i}^{y}))\Big)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\frac{\mathsf{d}(x_{i},y_{i})}{t_{i}^{x}}\,,

and note that the last term converges to zero by (
3.9
). Hence, to conclude, we need to show that

\mathsf{d}\Bigg(\Bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Bigg\rfloor t_{i}^{x}\cdot r(s^{y}_{i}),1\cdot r(s^{y}_{i})\Bigg)\to 0\,.

This follows combining that

\Bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{t_{i}^{x}}\Bigg\rfloor t_{i}^{x}\to 1\,,

and that the sequence
r(s_{i}^{y})
is precompact in
X
.
∎

Corollary 3.18
.

The map
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
is a homeomorphism. Moreover,
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)=2
.

Proof.

The first conclusion follows from Lemma
3.1.1
and Lemma
3.1.1
.
In particular,
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and thus it has topological dimension equal to
2
.
Combining with
[
70
, Theorem 1.1]
, it follows that the essential dimension of
X
is at most
2
.
At the same time, Lemma
3.1.1
implies that
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\geq 2
.
∎

We continue by studying the structure of the measure
\mathfrak{m}
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
, we have the following.
There exists a non-decreasing function
h\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that, for every
A\subset\subset X
,

(3.14)

\mathfrak{m}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}h(s)\,\mathscr{L}^{1}(f(A\cap\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}=s\}))\,ds\,.

Moreover,
h
is strictly positive on
(0,+\infty)
.

Proof.

Let
A\subset\subset X
. By the coarea formula applied to the function
\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}
,

\mathfrak{m}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},A)\,ds\,,

where
P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},A)
denotes the perimeter of the set
\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\}
evaluated on
A
.
We claim that there exists a nonnegative function
h\in\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
independent of
A
such that for
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
we have

(3.15)

P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},A)=h(s)\,\mathscr{L}^{1}(f(A\cap\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}=s\}))\,.

Since the measure
P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},\cdot)
is concentrated on the set
\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}=s\}
, and the restriction
f_{|\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}=s\}}:\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}=s\}\to\mathbb{R}
is bijective by Lemma
3.1.1
, (
3.15
) follows if we can prove the identity of measures

f_{\#}P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},\cdot)=h(s)\mathscr{L}^{1}\quad\text{for }\mathscr{L}^{1}\text{-a.e. }s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\,.

Fix
s\geq 0
. For every
a<b
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, by equivariance of
f
and invariance of the perimeter we have

f_{\#}P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},\cdot)(a,b)=f_{\#}P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},\cdot)(a+t,b+t)\,.

Since the only translation-invariant measures on
\mathbb{R}
are multiples of
\mathscr{L}^{1}
,
f_{\#}P(\{\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}>s\},\cdot)=h(s)\mathscr{L}^{1}
for some
g(s)\geq 0
. Since this holds for every
s\geq 0
, (
3.14
) holds for some function
h\in\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

We are left to show that
h\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))
and that
h
is increasing and strictly positive on
(0,+\infty)
. Let
\mathbb{Z}<H\cong\mathbb{R}
be the copy of
\mathbb{Z}
in
H
generated by
1\in H\cong\mathbb{R}
. Consider the quotient space
X/\mathbb{Z}
with its canonically induced
\RCD(0,N)
structure
(X/\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{d}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
(cf. Remark
3.1.1
). Let
f_{\mathbb{Z}}:X/\mathbb{Z}\to H/\mathbb{Z}
be defined such that
f_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}\cdot x):=\mathbb{Z}\cdot f(x)
, and let
\widetilde{r}\subset X/\mathbb{Z}
be the projection of
r
under the canonical quotient map.
Observe that

(3.16)

\bigcup_{t\in(0,1)\,,\,\,s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}t\cdot r(s)

is a fundamental domain of the covering
\pi:X\to X/\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, applying (
3.14
), we obtain that for every
A\subset\subset X/\mathbb{Z}

(3.17)

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}h(s)\,\mathscr{L}^{1}(f_{\mathbb{Z}}(A\cap\{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{(H/\mathbb{Z})\cdot\widetilde{r}(0)}=s\}))\,ds\,.

Consider now the quotient
X/H\cong(X/\mathbb{Z})/(H/\mathbb{Z})
, with its
\RCD(0,N)
structure
(X/H,\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime})
(cf. Remark
3.1.1
). Since
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
[
21
, Theorem A.2]
implies that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}=\overline{h}\mathscr{L}^{1}
for a non-decreasing, locally Lipschitz, and strictly positive function
\overline{h}:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
. On the other hand, for every
s\geq 0
, we have that

f_{\mathbb{Z}}(\{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{(H/\mathbb{Z})\cdot\widetilde{r}(0)}=s\})=H/\mathbb{Z}

and therefore

\mathscr{L}^{1}(f_{\mathbb{Z}}(\{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{(H/\mathbb{Z})\cdot\widetilde{r}(0)}=s\}))=1\,.

Thanks to (
3.17
), we infer that
\overline{h}=h
, thus completing the proof.
∎

The following corollary is an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.1.1
.

Corollary 3.20
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
, if

A\subset\subset X
is a Borel set, then
\mathfrak{m}(A)=0
if and only if
\mathscr{L}^{2}(\Psi(A))=0
.

For our next purposes it is helpful to study the behaviour of the tangent spaces of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)

space with a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
and such that
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
as metric spaces. Let
\pi:X\to[0,+\infty)
be the canonical projection to the quotient. Then, we have the following:

i)

every
x\in\pi^{-1}((0,+\infty))
is a regular point of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and the (unique) equivariant blow-up of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x,H)
is
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},\mathscr{L}^{2})
endowed with an
\mathbb{R}
-action by translations;

ii)

for every
x\in\pi^{-1}(\{0\})
the blow-up of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
at
x
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
; in particular
x
is not a regular point.

Proof.

If
x\in\pi^{-1}((0,+\infty))
, then the blow-up of
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
at
\pi(x)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. By Theorem
2.3
and Lemma
3.1.1
, every equivariant blow-up of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x,H)
admits a splitting
X=Y\times\mathbb{R}
with the blow-up action being trivial on the
\mathbb{R}
-factor. By lower semicontinuity of the essential dimension under pmGH convergence, see
[
58
, Theorem 1.5]
, and Corollary
3.1.1
,
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(Y)\leq 1
.
By Lemma
2.1
the limit group contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Item (i) thus follows from the classification of
1
-dimensional
\RCD
spaces, see
[
59
]
.

If
\pi(x)=0
, then every blow-up of
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
at
\pi(x)
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By Lemma
2.1
the limit group
H_{\infty}
of every equivariant blow-up
(X_{\infty},H_{\infty})
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},x,H)
contains a closed subgroup
H^{\prime}_{\infty}<H_{\infty}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.
By Corollary
3.1.1
, lower semicontinuity of the essential dimension again, and Lemma
3.1.1
, the quotient
X_{\infty}/H^{\prime}_{\infty}
has dimension at most
1
. At the same time,
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
by Theorem
2.3
, so that
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}^{\prime}
is non-compact. In particular,
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}^{\prime}
is either a ray or a line. If
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then
X_{\infty}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
by Corollary
3.1.1
.
If
X_{\infty}/H^{\prime}_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}
, then
X_{\infty}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by Lemma
3.1.1
. We show that this is not possible. Let
r\subset X
be the ray given by Lemma
3.1.1
and assume without loss of generality that
x=r(0)
. As
(X,\mathsf{d}/r_{i})\to X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
for some sequence
r_{i}\downarrow 0
, up to a subsequence, the ray
r\subset X
converges to a ray
r_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and the sets
\{y\in X:\mathsf{d}(y,r)\geq r_{i}\}
converge to
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}}(y,r_{\infty})\geq 1\}
in Hausdorff sense in the space realizing the pGH convergence. At the same time, one can write
\{y\in X:\mathsf{d}(y,r)\geq r_{i}\}
as the union of two sets at distance greater than
1
from each other and distance smaller than
2
from
r(0)
in
(X,\mathsf{d}/r_{i})
. Hence, also
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}}(y,r_{\infty})\geq 1\}
must admit such partition with
r(0)
replaced by
r_{\infty}(0)
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
let
\gamma:[0,T]\to X
be a segment. If the image of
\gamma
has three distinct points lying in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
, then it is fully contained in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
.

Proof.

The conclusion follows from Lemma
3.1.1
thanks to the Hölder continuity of tangent cones in the interior of geodesics, see
[
35
]
after
[
31
]
.
∎

Remark 3.23
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
3.1.1
it is possible that a segment
\gamma:[0,T]\to X
has
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(T)
lying in the singular orbit
\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)
with all the interior points lying in the regular part. Examples where this actually happens appear in
[
76
,
32
]
.

Motivated by Remark
3.1.1
, our next goal is to show that the function
f
we introduced before is harmonic. Such statement corresponds to Lemma
3.1.1
below. The next two lemmas are instrumental to its proof. Oversimplifying, we are going to rely on a blow-up argument to compute the partial derivatives of any sufficiently regular test function by using
\nabla\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}
and
\nabla f
as an orthogonal basis for the tangent bundle of
X
.

Lemma 3.24
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
for every
s>0
and every sequence
r_{i}\downarrow 0
, up to passing to a subsequence, there exist
\delta_{i}\downarrow 0
and
\delta_{i}
-GH approximations

\psi_{i}:(\overline{B}_{r_{i}}^{X}(r(s)),\mathsf{d}/r_{i})\to(\overline{B}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(0),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}})

satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\psi_{i}(r(s+r_{i}))=(0,1)
and
\psi_{i}(r(s-r_{i}))=(0,-1)
.

(2)

\psi_{i}(H\cdot r(s))\to\{y=0\}
in Hausdorff sense in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Assume in addition that the function
f
given by Lemma
3.1.1
is differentiable in
r(s)
according to Theorem
2.2
, and
\lip(f)(r(s))>0
. Modulo passing to a subsequence, the linear functions
f_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\lip(\mathsf{d}_{\infty})=1
and
\lip(f_{\infty})=\lip(f)(r(s))

obtained by blowing up
\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}
and
f
at
r(s)
have orthogonal gradients and they span the space of linear functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Tan}_{r(s)}(X)=\{\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
by Lemma
3.1.1
, up to passing to a (non-relabeled) subsequence, there exist
\delta_{i}\downarrow 0
, and
\delta_{i}
-GH approximations

{\psi}_{i}:(\overline{B}_{ir_{i}}^{X}(r(s)),\mathsf{d}/r_{i})\to(\overline{B}_{i}^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(0),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}})\,,

with
\psi_{i}(r(s))=0
.
It is a standard observation that one can take the second components of the GH-maps
\psi_{i}
to be just
(\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}-s)/r_{i}
, since
r(s)
is an intermediate point of a ray for
\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}(\cdot)
by (
3.6
). With such choice, condition
1
is trivially satisfied, up to a slight perturbation of the first component of the GH-maps. Condition
2
follows immediately from Lemma
3.1.1
(i).

Consider now the linear maps
\mathsf{d}_{\infty},f_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
obtained by blowing up
\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}
and
f
at
r(s)
via Theorem
2.2
. Since
\mathsf{d}_{H\cdot r(0)}(r(s+r_{i}))=s+r_{i}
and
\psi_{i}(r(s+r_{i}))=(0,1)
for every
i
, it follows that
\mathsf{d}_{\infty}((0,1))=1
. Since
|\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\infty}|\leq 1
,
\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\infty}
is vertical. Since
f(r(s+r_{i}))=0
,
f_{\infty}((0,1))=0
, so that
\nabla f_{\infty}
is horizontal. Since
\nabla f_{\infty}\neq 0
by assumption, the set
\{\mathsf{d}_{\infty},f_{\infty}\}
spans the space of linear functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 3.25
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
let
\varphi\in\Lip_{c}(X)
, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as
\varphi\circ\Psi^{-1}
, so that
\varphi(x)=\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
. Then we have the following:

(1)

For
h\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\lip(f)\neq 0
on
(\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)})^{-1}(s)
,

(3.18)

\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,s)\in\Lip_{c}(\mathbb{R})\,.

(2)

For
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
with
\lip(f)(x)\neq 0
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
is differentiable in
f(x)
, with

(3.19)

\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla f(x)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,.

(3)

For
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x),\cdot)
is differentiable in
\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x)
, with

(3.20)

\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{2}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))\,.

In particular, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
with
\lip(f)(x)\neq 0
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,\cdot)
has partial derivatives in
(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
, and

|\nabla\varphi|^{2}(x)=\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{2}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))+\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,.

Proof.

Proof of
1
:

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. We show that, for
h\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\lip(f)\geq\varepsilon
on
(\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)})^{-1}(s)
, (
3.18
) holds.
Fix such an
s\in(0,+\infty)
, and assume that for every
x\in(\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)})^{-1}(s)

the function
f
is differentiable in
x
. By Theorem
2.12
,

h\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\lip(f)\geq\varepsilon
on
(\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)})^{-1}(s)
has this property.
Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
and consider and sequence
t_{i}\to t
. We show that there exists
C>0
independent of
t_{i}
and
t
such that

(3.21)

\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\frac{|\widetilde{\varphi}(t_{i},s)-\widetilde{\varphi}(t,s)|}{|t_{i}-t|}<C\,,

which would then imply item
1
.
Let
x_{s}:=r(s)\in(\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)})^{-1}(s)
. We have that

\frac{|{\varphi}(t_{i}\cdot x_{s})-{\varphi}(t\cdot x_{s})|}{\mathsf{d}(t\cdot x_{s},t_{i}\cdot x_{s})}<\mathsf{L}(\varphi)\,,

where
\mathsf{L}(\varphi)
denotes the Lipschitz constant of
\varphi
,
so that

\frac{|\widetilde{\varphi}(t_{i},s)-\widetilde{\varphi}(t,s)|}{|t_{i}-t|}\frac{|t_{i}-t|}{\mathsf{d}(t\cdot x_{s},t_{i}\cdot x_{s})}<\mathsf{L}(\varphi)\,.

Hence, to conclude the proof of (
3.21
) it is enough to show that for
i
large enough

(3.22)

\frac{|t_{i}-t|}{\mathsf{d}(t\cdot x_{s},t_{i}\cdot x_{s})}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}>0\,.

Observe that
r_{i}:=\mathsf{d}(t\cdot x_{s},t_{i}\cdot x_{s})\to 0
as
i\uparrow+\infty
and, consider the GH maps

\psi_{i}:(\overline{B}_{r_{i}}^{X}(x_{s}),\mathsf{d}/r_{i})\to(\overline{B}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(0),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}})\,,

introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
. Up to to subsequences, since
f
is differentiable in
x_{s}
with
\lip(f)(x)\geq\varepsilon
, there exists a linear function
f_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lip(f_{\infty})\geq\varepsilon
, such that

(3.23)

\Big\|\frac{f-f(x_{s})}{r_{i}}-f_{\infty}\circ\psi_{i}\Big\|_{\infty,\,,\overline{B}^{X}_{r_{i}}(x_{s})}\to 0\,

as
i\to\infty
.
Thanks to
Lemma
3.1.1
,

(3.24)

|f_{\infty}(\pm 1,0)|\geq\varepsilon\,.

The same lemma gives that
\psi_{i}((t_{i}-t)\cdot x_{s})
converges to a point in
\{(\pm 1,0)\}
. Evaluating (
3.23
) in
(t_{i}-t)\cdot x_{s}
, we deduce

\Big|\frac{t_{i}-t}{\mathsf{d}(t\cdot x_{s},t_{i}\cdot x_{s})}-f_{\infty}(\pm 1,0)\Big|\to 0\,,

which, combined with (
3.24
), gives (
3.22
). This concludes the proof of item
1
.

Proof of
2
:
. Thanks to Lemma
3.1.1
and item
1
, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
with
\lip(f)(x)\neq 0
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
is differentiable in
f(x)
. Hence, we only need to check (
3.19
).

Let
x\in X\setminus(\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0))
be any point such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
is differentiable in
f(x)
,
\lip(f)(x)\neq 0
, and the functions
f
,
\varphi
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}
,
\varphi-f
, and
\varphi+f
are differentiable at
x
. Let
r_{i}\downarrow 0
and, up to passing to a subsequence, consider the corresponding linear functions
(\varphi+f)_{\infty},(\varphi-f)_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},f_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
obtained by blowing-up as in Definition
2.2
. By Lemma
3.1.1
, there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi_{\infty}=a\mathsf{d}_{\infty}+bf_{\infty}
. Furthermore,
(\varphi\pm f)_{\infty}=\varphi_{\infty}\pm f_{\infty}
. Hence, using that
\nabla d_{\infty}\cdot\nabla f_{\infty}=0
by Lemma
3.1.1
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla f(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{\lip(\varphi+f)^{2}(x)-\lip(\varphi-f)^{2}(x)}{4}

(3.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla(\varphi+f)_{\infty}|^{2}-|\nabla(\varphi-f)_{\infty}|^{2}}{4}

(3.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi_{\infty}\cdot\nabla f_{\infty}=b|\nabla f_{\infty}|^{2}=b|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,.

To conclude, we only need to show that
b=\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
. To this aim, let
t_{i}\downarrow 0
be such that
\mathsf{d}(x,t_{i}\cdot x)=r_{i}
.
Then, as
i\to\infty
, we have that

(3.28)

\Big|\frac{\varphi(t_{i}\cdot x)-\varphi(x)}{\mathsf{d}(x,t_{i}\cdot x)}-a\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))-bf_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))\Big|\to 0\,.

By item
2
of Lemma
3.1.1
, using also that
\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is vertical, we infer that
\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))\to 0
. Rewriting (
3.28
) in terms of
\widetilde{\varphi}
, we obtain

(3.29)

\Big|\frac{\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x)+t_{i},\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))-\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))}{t_{i}}\frac{t_{i}}{\mathsf{d}(x,t_{i}\cdot x)}-bf_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))\Big|\to 0\,.

Arguing as in the proof of item
1
, we obtain that

\Big|\frac{t_{i}}{\mathsf{d}(x,t_{i}\cdot x)}-f_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))\Big|\to 0,\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\infty}(\psi_{i}(t_{i}\cdot x))\to f_{\infty}(\pm 1,0)\neq 0\,.

Since
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
is differentiable in
f(x)
by assumption, (
3.29
) implies that
b=\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
, as claimed.

Proof of
3
:
For every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the map
\widetilde{\varphi}(t,\cdot)
is Lipschitz and hence differentiable
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e. in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By Lemma
3.1.1
, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
, the map
\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x),\cdot)
is differentiable in
\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x)
. The identity (
3.20
) follows repeating the argument used for (
3.19
) and taking into account that
|\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}|\equiv 1
.

Finally, for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. point
x\in X
where (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) hold, we have

(3.30)

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi|^{2}

\displaystyle=|\nabla\varphi_{\infty}|^{2}=|\nabla\varphi_{\infty}\cdot\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\infty}|^{2}+\Big|\nabla\varphi_{\infty}\cdot\frac{\nabla f_{\infty}}{|\nabla f_{\infty}|}\Big|^{2}

(3.31)

\displaystyle=|\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}|^{2}(x)+|\nabla f|^{-2}|\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla f|^{2}(x)

(3.32)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{2}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))+\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,.

∎

Remark 3.26
.

Item
3
in Lemma
3.1.1
is well known, as it follows for instance from
[
22
, Theorem 4.5]
. The computation of the directional derivative in the other direction is more subtle instead.

The next lemma and the subsequent corollary contain the main regularity result concerning the map
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
.

Lemma 3.27
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
, the function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
is harmonic.

Proof.

Let
\Psi:X\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the map given by Lemma
3.1.1
(with the usual identification
H\cong\mathbb{R}
), and let
h\in\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
be the density from Lemma
3.1.1
.
Let
\varphi\in\Lip_{c}(X)
, and define
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\widetilde{\varphi}:=\varphi\circ\Psi^{-1}
, so that
\varphi(x)=\widetilde{\varphi}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))
. To prove that
f
is harmonic we need to check that

\int_{X}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla f\,d\mathfrak{m}=0\,.

By item
2
of Lemma
3.1.1
, we have

\int_{X}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla f\,d\mathfrak{m}=\int_{X}\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)\,.

By Lemma
3.1.1
and the fact that
|\nabla f|
is invariant by translations by
H\cong\mathbb{R}
, which follows from the equivariance of
f
, we deduce

\displaystyle\int_{X}\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))

\displaystyle|\nabla f|^{2}(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}|\nabla f|^{2}(\Psi^{-1}(0,s))h(s)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(t,s)\,dt\,ds\,.

By item
1
of Lemma
3.1.1
, for
h\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have that
\widetilde{\varphi}(\cdot,s)\in\Lip_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{x_{1}}(t,s)\,dt=0
. Combining with (
3.27
), the statement follows.
∎

Corollary 3.28
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
the following hold:

(1)

|\nabla f|^{2}\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)\cap\mathsf{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
, and
|\nabla f|^{2}
has non-negative measure-valued Laplacian.

(2)

|\nabla f|^{2}
admits a continuous representative, which is invariant under the action of
H
and increases along the ray
r:(0,\infty)\to X
. Equivalently, the function canonically induced by
|\nabla f|^{2}
on the quotient space
X/H
is increasing.

(3)

The function induced by
|\nabla f|^{2}
on the quotient
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
belongs to
\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+},hd\mathscr{L}^{1})
, where
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},hd\mathscr{L}^{1})
is the quotient
\RCD(0,N)
structure on
X/H
(see Remark
3.1.1
).

(4)

The continuous representative of
|\nabla f|^{2}
is strictly positive on
X\setminus(\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0))
.

Proof.

Proof of
1
:

By Lemma
3.1.1
,
|\nabla f|^{2}\in\mathsf{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
. By Lemma
3.1.1
and
[
80
, Lemma
3.2
]
,
|\nabla f|^{2}\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)\cap\mathsf{L}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
and has non-negative measure-valued Laplacian.

Proof of
2
and
3
:
We first prove simultaneously that item

3
holds, and that
|\nabla f|^{2}
has a continuous representative in
X\setminus(\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0))
.
As we already observed, by the equivariance of
f
with respect to the
\mathbb{R}
-action, for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

(3.34)

|\nabla f|^{2}(x)=|\nabla f|^{2}(a\cdot x)\quad\text{for }\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e. }x\in X\,.

Fix
R>0
, and let
\varphi\in\Lip(X)
be a cut-off function such that
\varphi(a\cdot x)=\varphi(x)
for every
x\in X
and every
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi\equiv 1
on
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}<R\}
, and
\varphi\equiv 0
on
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}>R+1\}
. Such a function can be obtained as a composition of the form
k\circ\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}
, for some
k\in\Lip_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Since
R>0
is arbitrary, it is enough to show that
\varphi|\nabla f|^{2}
has a continuous representative.
For every
t>0
, let
f_{t}:=P_{t}(\varphi|\nabla f|^{2})\in\Lip(X)
. Given any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, by (
3.34
), the invariance of the cut-off function
\varphi
and of the heat flow,
f_{t}
is invariant under the
H\cong\mathbb{R}
-action as well.
Moreover, for every open set
A\subset\subset X
, we have

(3.35)

\lim_{t\to 0}{\sf Ch}(f_{t},A)={\sf Ch}(\varphi|\nabla f|^{2},A)<+\infty\,.

Consider now the quotient space
X/\mathbb{Z}
with its natural
\RCD(0,N)
structure, and observe that by invariance the functions
f_{t}
naturally induce functions
f_{t}^{\mathbb{Z}}:X/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
. From (
3.35
), for every
A\subset\subset X/\mathbb{Z}
, we have that

(3.36)

\limsup_{t\to 0}{\sf Ch}(f_{t}^{\mathbb{Z}},A)<+\infty\,.

Consider now the space
X/\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
obtained by quotienting
X/\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, endowed with its
\RCD(0,N)
structure
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
.
Consider the functions
f_{t}^{\mathbb{R}}:X/\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
naturally induced by
f_{t}^{\mathbb{Z}}
on
X/\mathbb{R}
. By
[
42
, Corollary
5.5
]
and (
3.36
), for every
A\subset\subset X/\mathbb{R}
, we have

\limsup_{t\to 0}{\sf Ch}(f_{t}^{\mathbb{R}},A)<+\infty\,.

Hence, the functions
f_{t}^{\mathbb{R}}
converge in
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
in
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
to a limiting function
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
.
By
[
21
, Theorem A.2]
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=h\,d\mathscr{L}^{1}
, where
h^{1/(N-1)}:(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
is a positive and concave function. Hence, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the functions
f_{t}^{\mathbb{R}}
have uniformly bounded Cheeger energies in
((\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{-1}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},\mathscr{L}^{1})
, where now we are using the standard Euclidean structure.
Thus the functions
f_{t}^{\mathbb{R}}
converge to
f^{\mathbb{R}}_{0}
in
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
((0,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},\mathscr{L}^{1})
as well. Therefore
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,+\infty)
w.r.t. the standard Euclidean structure. In particular,
f^{\mathbb{R}}_{0}
is continuous in
(0,+\infty)
.
At the same time,
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
induces a continuous
pointwise defined
function
F:\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}>0\}\subset X\to\mathbb{R}
, which is the
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
limit of the functions
f_{t}
in
X
. It follows that
F
is a continuous representative of
\varphi|\nabla f|^{2}
in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}>0\}
. This proves item
3
, and shows that
|\nabla f|^{2}
has a continuous representative in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}>0\}\subset X
, as we claimed.

We now claim that
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is monotone in an interval
(0,\eta)
for
\eta>0
small enough. Since
\Delta(\varphi|\nabla f|^{2})\geq 0
in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}<R\}
, by the strong maximum principle
[
45
, Theorem 2.8]
, the function
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is constant in a neighbourhood of each local maximum
(0,R)
. Hence, if
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is not monotone in
(0,\eta)
, there exists a triplet

0<a<b<c<\eta\,,

such that

(3.37)

f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(b)<\min\{f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(a),f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(c)\}.

Thus, if
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is not monotone in any
(0,\eta)
, there exist two triplets

0<a<b<c<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}<c^{\prime}<R\,,

such that

(3.38)

f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(b)<\min\{f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(a),f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(c)\},\quad f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(b^{\prime})<\min\{f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(a^{\prime}),f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}(c^{\prime})\}\,.

It follows that
{f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}}_{|[b,b^{\prime}]}
has a global maximum point
m\in(b,b^{\prime})
. Thus, by the strong maximum principle again,
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
must be constant in
[b,b^{\prime}]
, a contradiction.
This shows that there exists
\eta>0
such that
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is monotone in
(0,\eta)
.
Since
\varphi|\nabla f|^{2}
is essentially bounded,
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is bounded. Thus
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
extends to a continuous monotone function on
[0,R)
. Hence,
\varphi|\nabla f|^{2}
has a continuous representative on
X
. Using again that
\Delta(\varphi|\nabla f|^{2})\geq 0
on
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}<R\}\subset X
, it follows that
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is increasing in
[0,R)
.
