Since by construction
f_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
is the function induced on
X/\mathbb{R}
by the continuous representative of
\varphi|\nabla f|^{2}
,
|\nabla f|^{2}
is increasing on the quotient
X/\mathbb{R}
or, equivalently, on
r
.

Proof of
4
:

If
|\nabla f|^{2}
vanishes on
r(s)
for some
s>0
, then it vanishes on the whole
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}<s\}
by invariance and monotonicity. If this is the case, then
f
must be constant in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}<s\}
, a contradiction.
∎

Roughly speaking, we proved in Lemma
3.1.1
that gradients in
(X,\mathsf{d})
can be computed as on a manifold with a warped product metric of the form
dr^{2}+|\nabla f(r)|^{-2}dx^{2}
. By Lemma
3.1.1
, the measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
(X,\mathsf{d})
has a similar warped product structure. By Corollary
3.1.1
, the warping function has a reasonable amount of regularity.
Making rigorous the isomorphic identification of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with a measured warped product is still quite technical at this stage, but the proof below follows a by now well established strategy essentially introduced in the proof of the splitting theorem for
\RCD(0,N)
spaces in
[
43
]
.

Lemma 3.29
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
the restriction

\Psi^{-1}_{|\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)}:\big(\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}}\big)\to\big(\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}>0\},\mathsf{d}\big)\subset(X,\mathsf{d})

is locally Lipschitz.

Proof.

We denote by
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
the map introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
under the usual identification
H\cong\mathbb{R}
. We recall that
\Psi^{-1}(a,b)=a\cdot r(b)
. Let
K\subset\subset\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
be open.
We claim that there exists
C>1
such that

(3.39)

|f(a\cdot y)-f(y)|=|a|\geq C\mathsf{d}(y,a\cdot y)\quad\text{for every }y,a\cdot y\in\Psi^{-1}(K)\,.

If the claim holds, given pairs
(a,b),(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b})\in K
, we deduce

(3.40)

\mathsf{d}(\Psi^{-1}(a,b),\Psi^{-1}(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}))\leq|b-\widetilde{b}|+\mathsf{d}(a\cdot r(b),[(\widetilde{a}-a)+a]\cdot r(b))\\
\leq|b-\widetilde{b}|+C^{-1}|a-\widetilde{a}|\leq\sqrt{2}C^{-1}\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}}((a,b),(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}))\,,

which implies the statement.
To finish the proof we are left to prove (
3.39
). By item
4
of Corollary
3.1.1
, there exists
0<\widetilde{C}<1
such that
|\nabla f|\geq\widetilde{C}
on
\Psi^{-1}(K)
. Let
y\in\Psi^{-1}(K)
and let
a>0
be such that
a\cdot y\in\Psi^{-1}(K)
. Let
y_{i}\to y
be differentiability points of
f
in
\Psi^{-1}(K)
. Since
|\nabla f|\geq\widetilde{C}>0
on
\Psi^{-1}(K)
, using Lemma
3.1.1
, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\frac{a/j}{\mathsf{d}(a/j\cdot y_{i},y_{i})}=\frac{f(a/j\cdot y_{i})-f(y_{i})}{\mathsf{d}(a/j\cdot y_{i},y_{i})}\geq\widetilde{C}/2\quad\text{for every }j\in\mathbb{N}\text{ large enough.}

Hence

(3.41)

\displaystyle a=

\displaystyle j(a/j)\geq\frac{\widetilde{C}}{2}\Big(\mathsf{d}(y_{i},a/j\cdot y_{i})+\cdots+\mathsf{d}\big([(j-1)a/j]\cdot y_{i},a\cdot y_{i}\big)\Big)

(3.42)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{C}}{2}\mathsf{d}(y_{i},a\cdot y_{i})\,.

Passing to the limit as
y_{i}\to y
, we obtain (
3.39
) when
a>0
. The case
a<0
is analogous.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

The examples discussed in
[
76
,
32
]
show that, in general, the map
\Psi:(X,\mathsf{d})\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
need not be globally biLipschitz.

By the invariance of
|\nabla f|
with respect to the
H\cong\mathbb{R}
-action we can identify it with a function on
X/\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We will tacitly make such identification from now on.
On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we consider the
C^{0}
Riemannian metric (with extended values) given by
g(x_{1},x_{2}):=|\nabla f|^{-2}(x_{2})\,dx_{1}^{2}+dx_{2}^{2}
.
Since the continuous representative of
|\nabla f|
is strictly positive on
(0,+\infty)
by Corollary
3.1.1
, every two points in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be joined by a curve of finite length w.r.t.
g
. Recall that the length of an absolutely continuous curve
\gamma\in AC([0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
with respect to
g
is defined as

(3.43)

\mathrm{length}_{g}(\gamma):=\int_{0}^{1}g({\gamma}^{\prime},{\gamma}^{\prime})^{1/2}\,dt\,.

Thus, the metric
g
induces a real-valued distance
\mathsf{d}_{g}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
by setting

(3.44)

\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b):=\inf\big\{\mathrm{length}_{g}(\gamma):\gamma\in AC([0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})\text{ and }\gamma(0)=a,\gamma(1)=b\big\}\,.

The next two lemmas establish some technical variants of the Sobolev-to-Lipschitz property on
(X,\mathsf{d})
and
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
which are going to helpful later to complete the proof of Proposition
3.1.1
.

Lemma 3.31
.

Consider the
C^{0}
Riemannian metric (with extended values)
g(x_{1},x_{2}):=|\nabla f|^{-2}(x_{2})\,dx_{1}^{2}+dx_{2}^{2}
and the induced real-valued distance
\mathsf{d}_{g}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
If
\varphi\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{g})
has
\lip(\varphi)\leq 1
at
\mathscr{L}^{2}
-a.e. point, then
\varphi
admits a
1
-Lipschitz extension to
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
.

Proof.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
. We need to show that
|\varphi(a)-\varphi(b)|\leq\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)
. Without loss of generality we assume that
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\times(1,+\infty)
. By the monotonicity of
|\nabla f|^{2}
obtained in Corollary
3.1.1
, the set
\mathbb{R}\times(1,+\infty)
is geodesically convex in
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
. Hence, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a smooth curve
\gamma_{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}\times(1,+\infty)
such that
\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)\geq\mathrm{length}(\gamma_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon
. By perturbing
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
, one can construct a curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
such that
\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)\geq\mathrm{length}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})-2\varepsilon
and, for
\mathscr{L}^{1}
-a.e.
t\in[0,1]
,
\lip(\varphi)(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq 1
.
Therefore

\displaystyle|\varphi(a)-\varphi(b)|\leq\int_{0}^{1}(\varphi\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{1}g(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})^{1/2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\mathrm{length}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})\leq\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)+2\varepsilon\,,

where for the second inequality we used
[
71
, Proposition 4.22]
which guarantees that

(3.45)

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathsf{d}_{g}\big(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}(t+s),\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)}{s}=g(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s),\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(s))^{1/2}\quad\text{ for }\mathscr{L}^{1}\text{-a.e. }s\in(0,1)\,.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, the statement follows.
∎

Lemma 3.32
.

Under the same assumptions and with the same notation as in Lemma
3.1.1
,
let
\varphi\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(X\setminus\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\})\cap C(X)
be such that
|\nabla\varphi|\leq 1
at
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. point. Then,
\varphi
is
1
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Our first claim is that
\lip(\varphi)\leq 1
everywhere on
X\setminus\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
. To this aim, fix
x\in X\setminus\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
, and let
\eta\in\Lip_{c}(X\setminus\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\})
be a function which is equal to
1
on a neighbourhood of
x
. By the Bakry-Émery contraction estimate from
[
2
, Theorem 6.2]
,

(3.46)

\lip(P_{t}(\eta\varphi))\leq P_{t}(\lip(\eta\varphi))\quad\text{pointwise}\,.

On a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
x
,
P_{t}(\lip(\eta\varphi))\leq 1+o(1)
and
P_{t}(\eta\varphi)
converges uniformly to
\varphi
as
t\to 0
, by the well-known properties of the heat flow on
\RCD(K,N)
spaces. It follows that, possibly considering a smaller neighbourhood of
x
,
\varphi
is a uniform limit of
(1+o(1))
-Lipschitz functions. The claim follows.

Let
a,b\in X
and let
\gamma\subset X
be any geodesic connecting them. By Lemma
3.1.1
, either
\gamma
has at most two points in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
, or it is fully contained in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
. In the former case,
\varphi\circ\gamma
is absolutely continuous and

(3.47)

|\varphi(a)-\varphi(b)|\leq\int_{0}^{\mathrm{length}(\gamma)}(\varphi\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(t)\,dt\leq\mathrm{length}(\gamma)\leq\mathsf{d}(a,b)\,.

So, assume that
\gamma\subset\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Let
x^{\prime}
be the midpoint of
\gamma
. Let
x^{\prime\prime}
be the point at distance
\varepsilon
from
x^{\prime}
on the ray starting from
x^{\prime}
which realizes the distance from
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
.
Let
\gamma_{a}
and
\gamma_{b}
be geodesics from
a
to
x^{\prime\prime}
, and from
x^{\prime\prime}
to
b
. These geodesics have at most two points each in
\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\}
, and they have length at most
\mathsf{d}(a,b)/2+\varepsilon
. Let
\eta
be the curve (parameterized by arc length) obtained by joining these two geodesics, and observe that

(3.48)

|\varphi(a)-\varphi(b)|\leq\int_{0}^{\mathrm{length}(\eta)}(\varphi\circ\eta)^{\prime}(t)\,dt\leq\mathrm{length}(\eta)\leq\mathsf{d}(a,b)+2\varepsilon\,.

The conclusion follows by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
.
∎

We are ready to complete the proof of the measured warped product structure.

Proof of Proposition
3.1.1
.

Thanks to Lemma
3.1.1
it suffices to prove the following statement:

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space such that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
contains a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
and
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
\Psi:X\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
and
r:[0,+\infty)\to X
be as in Lemma
3.1.1
, under the usual identification
H\cong\mathbb{R}
. On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, consider the
C^{0}
Riemannian metric (with extended values) given by
g(x_{1},x_{2}):=|\nabla f|^{-2}(x_{2})\,dx_{1}^{2}+dx_{2}^{2}
and the induced real-valued distance
\mathsf{d}_{g}
, and let
h
be the density from Lemma
3.1.1
.
Then

\Psi:(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})\to(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g},h(x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2})

is a measure preserving isometry.

The fact that
\Psi
is measure preserving readily follows from Lemma
3.1.1
. In the remaining part of the proof we prove that it is an isometry.
We denote by
\nabla_{g}
gradients w.r.t.
g
, and by
|\cdot|_{g}
the norm of a vector w.r.t.
g
.
Let
\varphi\in\Lip(X)
be such that
\mathsf{L}(\varphi)\leq 1
and define
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
as
\widetilde{\varphi}:=\varphi\circ\Psi^{-1}
. We claim that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
1
-Lipschitz w.r.t.
\mathsf{d}_{g}
.
By Lemma
3.1.1
and Corollary
3.1.1
,
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{g})
.
By Lemma
3.1.1
, combined with the fact that
|\nabla f|^{2}\neq 0
on
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
by Corollary
3.1.1
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{g}\widetilde{\varphi}|^{2}_{g}(\Psi(x))=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{2}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))+\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}_{x_{1}}(f(x),\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}(x))|\nabla f|^{2}(x)

(3.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi|^{2}(x)\quad\text{for }\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e. }x\in X\,.

By
[
71
, Proposition 4.25]
,
\lip_{g}(\widetilde{\varphi})=|\nabla_{g}\widetilde{\varphi}|_{g}
on
\mathscr{L}^{2}
-a.e. point of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
\lip_{g}(\widetilde{\varphi})
denotes the local Lipschitz constant of
\widetilde{\varphi}
w.r.t.
\mathsf{d}_{g}
. By Corollary
3.1.1
and (
3.1.1
), we infer that
\lip_{g}(\widetilde{\varphi})\leq 1
on
\mathscr{L}^{2}
-a.e. point of
\mathbb{R}\times(0,+\infty)
. Hence
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
1
-Lipschitz in
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
by Lemma
3.1.1
.

Fix any
x\in X
and let
\mathsf{d}_{x}:X\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as
\mathsf{d}_{x}(y):=\mathsf{d}(x,y)
. The function
\mathsf{d}_{x}\circ\Psi^{-1}
is
1
-Lipschitz in
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
by the previous part of the proof. Hence

(3.50)

\mathsf{d}(x,y)=|\mathsf{d}_{x}\circ\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(x))-\mathsf{d}_{x}\circ\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(y))|\leq\mathsf{d}_{g}(\Psi(x),\Psi(y))\,.

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\Lip(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{g})
be a
1
-Lipschitz function, and let
\varphi:X\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
be defined as
\varphi:=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\Psi
. We claim that
\varphi
is
1
-Lipschitz.
By Lemma
3.1.1
,
\varphi\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}(X\setminus\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{R}\cdot r(0)}=0\})
.
Arguing as before, we deduce that
|\nabla\varphi|\leq 1
on
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. point of
X
. Thus
\varphi
is
1
-Lipschitz on
X
by Lemma
3.1.1
.
Consider any point
a\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and let
\mathsf{d}_{g,a}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as
\mathsf{d}_{g,a}(b):=\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)
. By the previous considerations
\mathsf{d}_{g,a}\circ\Psi
is
1
-Lipschitz on
X
. Hence,

(3.51)

\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,b)=|\mathsf{d}_{g,a}\circ\Psi(\Psi^{-1}(a))-\mathsf{d}_{g,a}\circ\Psi(\Psi^{-1}(b))|\leq\mathsf{d}(\Psi^{-1}(a),\Psi^{-1}(b))\,.

The combination of (
3.50
) and (
3.51
) shows that
\Psi
is an isometry, thus completing the proof.
∎

3.1.2.
Proof of Theorem
3.5
, base step: splitting

Theorem
3.33
below corresponds to the base case
k=1
in Theorem
3.5
. Proving it is the goal of this section.

Theorem 3.33
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space such that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
contains a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
,
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq 2
, and

(3.52)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))}\,dt=+\infty\,.

Then
X\cong Y\times\mathbb{R}
as metric measure spaces for some
\RCD(0,N-1)
space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y},\mathfrak{m}_{Y})
.

The proof of Theorem
3.33
relies on the measured warped product rigidity obtained in Proposition
3.1.1
, and on the equivalence between synthetic and distributional lower bounds on the weighted Ricci curvature valid for sufficiently regular weighted Riemannian metrics (Theorem
3.34
below).

Given a Riemannian metric
g
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
g_{ij}\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, one can compute the distributional Christoffel symbols, defined as

(3.53)

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\,g^{k\ell}\left(\partial_{i}g_{j\ell}+\partial_{j}g_{i\ell}-\partial_{\ell}g_{ij}\right)\,.

Thanks to the regularity of
g
, the Christoffel symbols belong to
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Hence, one can define the distributional Ricci curvature as

(3.54)

(\Ric_{g})_{ij}:=\partial_{k}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{k}_{ik}+\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\Gamma^{\ell}_{k\ell}-\Gamma^{k}_{i\ell}\Gamma^{\ell}_{jk}\,.

Given a function
V\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, its distributional Hessian is defined as

(3.55)

(\Hess_{g}(V))_{ij}:={\partial^{2}_{i,j}V}-\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\,{\partial_{k}V}\,.

The next result is proved in
[
71
]
.

Theorem 3.34
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a Riemannian manifold with boundary with
g
a continuous Riemannian metric with Christoffel symbols in
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{loc}(M)
. Let
V\in C^{0}(M)\cap\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{loc}(M)
, and assume that
(M,\mathsf{d}_{g},e^{-V}d\mathsf{Vol}_{g})
is an
\RCD(0,\infty)
space. Then,

\Ric_{M}+\Hess_{V}\geq 0\quad\text{on }M\setminus\partial M\text{ in distributional sense}.

Consider now a function
\varphi\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap C((0,+\infty))
, and the manifold
(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
equipped with the warped product metric
g(s,t):=ds^{2}+\varphi^{2}(s)dt^{2}
. We will denote as
(0,+\infty)\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}
the resulting Riemannian manifold. Specializing (
3.53
) to this simplified setting, we deduce

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s}_{t,t}=-{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}_{t,s}=\Gamma^{t}_{s,t}=\frac{\varphi^{\prime}}{\varphi},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s}_{s,s}=\Gamma^{t}_{t,t}=\Gamma^{t}_{s,s}=0\,.

Hence, using (
3.54
), we obtain

(3.56)

(\Ric_{(0,+\infty)\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}})_{t,t}:=-(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{\prime}+(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}\,.

Similarly, using (
3.55
), we deduce

(3.57)

(\Hess_{(0,+\infty)\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}}(V))_{t,t}:=\varphi\varphi^{\prime}V^{\prime}\,.

Proof of Theorem
3.33
.

Thanks to Proposition
3.1.1
, we can assume that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is equivariantly isomorphic to a measured warped product
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}
, with metric and measure given respectively by

(3.58)

g(r,x):=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\varphi(r)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{2}\,,\quad\quad\mathfrak{m}:=h(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

for some increasing function
h\in\Lip_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty))\cap\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and some continuous and strictly positive function
\varphi:(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
, with the
\mathbb{R}
-action being by translation on the
x
component and
\mathsf{d}
the length distance induced by
g
. Under such identification, the equivariant function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
introduced in Lemma
3.1.1
admits the clear expression
f(r,x)=x
and we have
\varphi=|\nabla f|^{-1}
, by direct computation.

The
2
-dimensional Hausdorff measure with respect to
g
on
X
can be written in coordinates as
\varphi(r)drdx
. By (
3.58
), there exists a function
F:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
F\varphi\in\mathsf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathscr{L}^{1})
and

(3.59)

\mathfrak{m}=F(r)\varphi(r)drdx\,.

Let
\mathsf{d}_{g,0}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be defined by
\mathsf{d}_{g,0}(a):=\mathsf{d}_{g}(a,\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})
. According to
[
21
, Section 7]
, consider the disintegration of
\mathfrak{m}
induced by the
1
-Lipschitz function
\mathsf{d}_{g,0}
. By
[
21
, Main Theorem 1.1]
and
[
21
, Theorem A.2]
,

(3.60)

(F\varphi)^{\frac{1}{N-1}}:(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)

is a concave function. Since

\varphi\in C^{0}(0,+\infty)\cap\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,+\infty)

and it is non-increasing and strictly positive by Corollary
3.1.1
,
F\in C^{0}[0,+\infty)\cap\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,+\infty)
.
Using again that

\varphi\in C^{0}(0,+\infty)\cap\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,+\infty)
we deduce that the distributional Christoffel symbols of
g
are in
\mathsf{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
. Note that this condition is independent of whether we consider as reference measure
\mathfrak{m}
or
\mathscr{L}^{2}
. The remaining part of the proof is divided into three steps. In Step 1 we use the equivalence between synthetic and distributional lower bound on the Ricci curvature to deduce that
\varphi
is super-harmonic on the weighted half-lines
([\delta,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},F\mathscr{L}^{1})
for every
\delta>0
. In Step 2 we reformulate the growth condition (
3.52
) into a growth condition for
F
. In Step 3 we combine the results of the previous two steps to show that
\varphi
is constant. This is clearly enough to complete the proof of the splitting of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
.

Step 1:
We claim that for every
\delta>0
, and every non-negative function
\psi\in\Lip_{c}([\delta,+\infty)])
, there holds

(3.61)

\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}\geq 0\,.

Note that the integral in (
3.61
) is well defined and finite thanks to Corollary
3.1.1
.
Set
V:=-\log(F):\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
. By Theorem
3.34
, since
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is an
\RCD(0,N)
space, and thus in particular it is an
\RCD(0,\infty)
space, we have that

(3.62)

\Ric_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}}+\Hess_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{R}}(V)\geq 0\quad\text{distributionally on }(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\,.

Using (
3.56
) and (
3.57
), we obtain that

(3.63)

-(\varphi\varphi^{\prime})^{\prime}+(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}+\varphi\varphi^{\prime}V^{\prime}\geq 0\quad\text{in distributional sense in }(\delta,+\infty)\,.

By a straightforward manipulation, (
3.63
) is equivalent to

\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\varphi^{\prime}V^{\prime}\leq 0\quad\text{in distributional sense on }(\delta,+\infty)\,,

which in turn is equivalent to

(3.64)

\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}\varphi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}\geq 0\quad\text{for every non-negative }\psi\in\Lip_{c}((\delta,+\infty))\,.

We continue proving (
3.61
) for any non-negative function
\psi\in\Lip_{c}([\delta,+\infty))
. For every
\varepsilon>0
, consider the function
\eta_{\varepsilon}
such that
(1-\eta_{\varepsilon})\in\Lip_{c}([\delta,+\infty))
defined by

(3.65)

\eta_{\varepsilon}(t):=1\wedge(1-\varepsilon^{-1}(t-\delta-\varepsilon))\vee 0\,.

Using the Leibniz rule for the second equality, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}(\psi\eta_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}+\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}(\psi(1-\eta_{\varepsilon}))^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}(\psi\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+\eta_{\varepsilon}\psi^{\prime})\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}+\int_{[\delta,+\infty)}\varphi^{\prime}(\psi(1-\eta_{\varepsilon}))^{\prime}\,Fd\mathscr{L}^{1}.

Since
\varphi^{\prime}\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\geq 0
in
(0,+\infty)
by Corollary
3.1.1
, passing to the limit in the previous equation as
\varepsilon\to 0
and taking into account (
3.64
), we deduce (
3.61
).

Step 2:

Let
A:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be defined by
A(r):=\int_{0}^{r}F(t)\,dt
. Note that
A(r)
is the measure of the
r
-ball centered at
0
in the weighted half-line
([0,+\infty),\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},F\mathscr{L}^{1})
.
We claim that

(3.66)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t}{A(t)}\,dt=+\infty\,.

By triangle inequality, there holds

B^{g}_{r}((0,0))\supset\Big\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}:b\in(0,r/2),\,a\in\Big(-r\frac{\varphi(b)^{-1}}{2},r\frac{\varphi(b)^{-1}}{2}\Big)\Big\}\,.

Hence,

\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}^{g}(0,0))\geq\int_{0}^{r}\int_{-r\frac{\varphi(s)^{-1}}{2}}^{r\frac{\varphi(s)^{-1}}{2}}\varphi(s)F(s)\,dxds=r\int_{0}^{r}F(t)\,dt=rA(r)\,.

Therefore, using (
3.52
), we conclude that

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t}{A(t)}\,dt\geq\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{2}}{\mathfrak{m}\big(B^{g}_{t}((0,0))\big)}\,dt=+\infty\,,

thus proving (
3.66
).

Step 3:
We claim that
\varphi
is constant. As noted before, this is clearly enough to prove that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
splits a line.

By Lemma
3.1.2
below and Step 1, for every
\delta>0
we have that

(3.67)

\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}\psi^{2}\frac{{\varphi^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1+\varphi)^{2}}\,Fdt\leq 16\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}{\psi^{\prime}}^{2}\,Fdt\,,

for every non-negative function
\psi\in\Lip_{c}([\delta,+\infty))
.
Fix any
R>\delta
. We claim that
\varphi
is constant on
[\delta,R]
. Using the notation of Step 2, consider the functions
\psi_{i}\in\Lip_{c}([\delta,+\infty))
defined by

\psi_{i}(t):=\Big(\int_{R\vee t\wedge i}^{i}\frac{s}{A(s)}\,ds\Big)\Big/\Big(\int_{R}^{i}\frac{s}{A(s)}\,ds\Big)\,.

We claim that

(3.68)

\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}{\psi^{\prime}_{i}}^{2}\,Fdt\to 0\quad\text{as }i\to+\infty\,,

which, combined with (
3.67
), would imply that
\varphi
is constant on
[\delta,R]
.
To prove the claim, we can compute

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{+\infty}{\psi^{\prime}_{i}}^{2}\,Fdt

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{R}^{i}t^{2}F(t)/A^{2}(t)\,dt\Big)\Big/\Big(\int_{R}^{i}\frac{s}{A(s)}\,ds\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(-\frac{i^{2}}{A(i)}+\frac{R^{2}}{A(R)}+2\int_{R}^{i}\frac{s}{A(s)}\,ds\Big)\Big/\Big(\int_{R}^{i}\frac{s}{A(s)}\,ds\Big)^{2}\,.

The right-hand side of the previous equality converges to zero as
i\to+\infty
thanks to Step 2, thus proving (
3.68
). It follows from (
3.67
) that
\varphi
is constant on
[\delta,R]
. By the arbitrariness of
\delta
and
R
,
\varphi
is constant on
(0,+\infty)
, as we claimed.
∎

The next lemma follows by adapting Yau’s result from
[
91
]
(see also
[
61
, Lemma
7.1
]
).

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
f,u\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be positive functions such that

(3.69)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\geq 0\quad\text{for every non-negative }\varphi\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\,.

Then,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi^{2}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{(1+u)^{2}}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\leq 16\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\,,

for every non-negative
\varphi\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

We define
v\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
v:=(1+u)^{-1}
. Using the chain rule and the Leibniz rule, for every
\varphi\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we get

0\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla(\varphi^{2}v)\cdot\nabla v\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi v\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla v\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\,,

so that

(3.70)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\varphi v\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla v|\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\,.

Applying Young’s inequality we get

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi v\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla v\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}+8\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}v^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\,,

which combined with (
3.70
) gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\leq 16\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}v^{2}\,fd\mathscr{L}^{n}\,.

Recalling the definition of
v
, the statement follows.
∎

3.1.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.5
, inductive step

The goal of this section is complete the inductive proof of Theorem
3.5
by proving the inductive step.
The next three lemmas are needed to carry out such inductive step.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X)\leq k+1
;

(2)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{k+1}}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))}\,dt=+\infty
for some
p\in X
;

(3)

There exists a closed subgroup
\mathbb{R}^{k}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
.

Let
H^{\prime\prime}\leq H
be a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Consider the quotient space
(X/H^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime})
equipped with the measure
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
provided by Remark
3.1.1
. Then, denoting by
B^{\prime\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime\prime})
balls in
(X/H^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime})
centered in
p^{\prime\prime}:=H^{\prime\prime}\cdot p
, we have that:

(1)

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(X/H^{\prime\prime})\leq k
.

(2)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}(B^{\prime\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime\prime}))}\,dt=+\infty
.

(3)

\mathrm{Iso}(X/H^{\prime\prime})
contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
.

Proof.

Item
1
immediately follows from Lemma
3.1.1
. To prove item
3
, it suffices to observe that the quotient
\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\cong H/H^{\prime\prime}
is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Iso}(X/H^{\prime\prime})
.
Hence, we only need to show item
2
, i.e. that

(3.71)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}(B^{\prime\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime\prime}))}\,dt=+\infty\,.

Let
H^{\prime}\leq H^{\prime\prime}
be the subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
1\in H^{\prime\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}
, and consider the natural
\RCD(0,N)
structure
(X/H^{\prime},\mathsf{d}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
. We denote by
B^{\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime})
balls in
(X/H^{\prime},\mathsf{d}^{\prime})
centered in
p^{\prime}:=p+H^{\prime}
. As an intermediate step, we show that there exists
c>0
such that

(3.72)

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}(B^{\prime\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime\prime}))\leq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(B^{\prime}_{t+c}(p^{\prime}))\quad\text{for every }t>0\,.

To this aim, we observe that by definition of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
,

(3.73)

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}(B^{\prime\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime\prime}))=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\{x\in X/H^{\prime}:\mathsf{d}^{\prime}(x+H^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime},p^{\prime})\leq t\}\,.

Since
H^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime}
is a compact group, there exists
c>0
, such that
p^{\prime}+H^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime}\subset B^{\prime}_{c}(p^{\prime})
. Combining with (
3.73
), (
3.72
) follows.
Our next claim is that there exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that

(3.74)

t\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(B^{\prime}_{t/2}(p^{\prime}))\leq c^{\prime}\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))\quad\text{for every }t\geq 0\text{ large enough}\,.

Note that the combination of (
3.74
) and (
3.72
) would give (
3.71
). To prove the claim, consider the projection
\pi:X\to X/H^{\prime}
, and observe that it is a covering map.
Let
F\subset X
be the fundamental domain containing
p
provided by Lemma
A
. By construction,

(3.75)

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(B^{\prime}_{t}(p^{\prime}))\leq\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p)\cap F)\,,\quad\forall t>0\,.

Assume w.l.o.g. that the generator
h
of
H^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
\mathsf{d}(p,p+h)=1
. By the triangle inequality,

(3.76)

\displaystyle B_{t}(p)\supset\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}h^{i}+(B_{t/2}(p)\cap F)\,.

Since
\mathfrak{m}((h^{i}+F)\cap F)=0
by Lemma
A
again, we deduce that

(3.77)

\mathfrak{m}(B_{t}(p))\geq\lfloor t/2\rfloor\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(B^{\prime}_{t/2}(p^{\prime}))\,,

thus yielding (
3.74
).
∎

The following lemma is well known, thus its elementary proof will be omitted.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a metric space, and let
H<\mathrm{Iso}(X\times\mathbb{R})
be a closed subgroup. Consider the resulting quotient space
(X\times\mathbb{R})/H
, and assume that it is isometric to
Y\times\mathbb{R}
for some metric space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{y})
. Let
\pi:X\times\mathbb{R}\to Y\times\mathbb{R}
be the projection on the quotient, and assume that
\pi((t,x))=(t,y)
for some
x\in X
,
y\in Y
and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(3.78)

H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)\times\{\mathrm{Id}\}<\mathrm{Iso}(X\times\mathbb{R})\,.

Applying iteratively Lemma
3.1.3
and the splitting theorem for
\RCD(0,N)
spaces from
[
43
]
, and using that lines can be lifted under metric submetries, we obtain the following:

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(0,N)
space, and let

H<\mathrm{Iso}(X)
be a closed subgroup.
Assume that
X/H\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
isometrically. Then,
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y^{\prime}
isometrically, and

(3.79)

H<\mathrm{Iso}(X^{\prime})\times\{\mathrm{Id}\}<\mathrm{Iso}(Y^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k})\,.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

We argue by induction. The base step corresponds to Theorem
3.33
.
For the inductive step, we assume that the statement holds for every
j
such that
1\leq j\leq k-1
, and we show that it holds for
k
. Let
H^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}
be a closed subgroup of
H
, and consider the quotient space
(X/H^{\prime},\mathsf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime})
with the induced
\RCD(0,N)
structure, as in Remark
3.1.1
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
the space
X/H^{\prime}
satisfies the hypotheses of the theorem for
k-1
. Thus, by the inductive hypothesis,
X/H^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times Y
as metric measure spaces, for some
\RCD(0,N-k+1)
space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y},\mathfrak{m}_{Y})
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
, we also have that
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times Y^{\prime}
isometrically, and that

(3.80)

H^{\prime}<\{\mathrm{Id}\}\times\mathrm{Iso}(Y^{\prime})<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k-1})\times\mathrm{Iso}(Y^{\prime})\,.

Hence,
Y^{\prime}
splits off a line isometrically thanks to the base case
k=1
.
∎

3.1.4.
Proof of Corollary
3.1

We distinguish two cases, according to the volume growth rate at infinity. The case of Euclidean volume growth is much easier and does not require Theorem
3.5
.

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
and such that

(3.81)

\lim_{s\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{s}(p))}{s^{n}}=v>0\quad\text{for some }p\in X\,.

Then, either
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times Y
isometrically, or the blow-down
X_{\infty}
of
X
is unique and isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
, for some
Z\in\{C(S^{1}_{r}),C([0,a])\}
with
r\in(0,1)
,
a\in(0,\pi)
.
In the latter case, if
(X_{\infty},H_{\infty})
is an equivariant blow-down of
(X,H)
, then
H_{\infty}<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{n-2})\times\mathrm{Iso}(Z)
and
\pi_{\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{n-2})}(H_{\infty})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
acts by translations on
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.

Proof.

By (
3.81
) and
[
33
,
34
]
after
[
24
, Theorem 7.6]
, every blow-down
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},p_{\infty})
of
X
is a
n
-dimensional metric cone centered at its tip
p_{\infty}
. Fix one such
X_{\infty}
. By Lemma
2.1
, there exists a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Iso}(X_{\infty})
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.
By cone-splitting,

X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times C(Z)
isometrically, where
Z\in\{S^{1}_{r},[0,a]\}
. If
Z=S^{1}_{1}
, then
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
[
34
, Theorem 1.6]
. If
Z=[0,\pi]
, then
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
by
[
17
, Theorem 8.1]
. Thus, we reduce to the case when
Z\in\{C(S^{1}_{r}),C([0,a])\}
and
r\in(0,1),a\in(0,\pi)
.
By volume convergence, see
[
34
, Theorem 1.3]
after
[
30
,
25
]
,

\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{1}(p_{\infty}))=v
.
Thus, the radius
r\in(0,1)
or the parameter
a\in(0,\pi)
such that
X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times C(S^{1}_{r})
or
X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times C([0,a])
are determined by the asymptotic volume ratio of
X
. In particular, there are at most two non-isometric choices for the blow-down of
X
. By Lemma
2.2
, the blow-down of
X
must be unique.

The second part of the statement now readily follows from
[
75
, Theorem 3.2]
.

∎

In the case where the volume growth is non-Euclidean, we begin by studying the blow-down of the quotient space with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
-action.

Lemma 3.40
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
and such that

(3.82)

\lim_{s\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{s}(p))}{s^{n}}=0\quad\text{for some }p\in X\,.

Then, either
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
as metric measure spaces, or the blow-down of
X/H
is unique up to pointed isometry and pointed isometric to
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},0)
.

Proof.

Set
Y:=X/H
, and consider its induced
\RCD(0,n)
structure
(Y,\overline{\mathsf{d}},\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
. By Theorem
2.13
, we can assume without loss of generality that
Y
is non-compact.
Consider any blow-down
(Y_{\infty},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty},\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{\infty},y_{\infty})
of
Y
. Up to taking a subsequence, the corresponding rescalings of
X
converge to an equivariant blow-down
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},H_{\infty},x_{\infty})
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},H)
.
By Lemma
2.1
,
H_{\infty}
contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.
By Theorem
2.3
,
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}\cong Y_{\infty}
isometrically. Thus, by Lemma
3.1.1
,
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(Y_{\infty})\leq 1
.
Since
Y
is non-compact,
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(Y_{\infty})=1
and
Y_{\infty}
is non-compact. Thus, by the classification of
1
-dimensional
\RCD
spaces from
[
59
]
,
Y_{\infty}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

If
Y
has a blow-down isometric to
\mathbb{R}
, then
Y\cong\mathbb{R}\times Y^{\prime}
, for some compact
\RCD(0,n-1)
space
Y^{\prime}
by Lemma
2.2
.
By Lemma
3.1.3
,
X\cong\mathbb{R}\times X^{\prime}
,
H<\{\mathrm{Id}\}\times\mathrm{Iso}(X^{\prime})<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{Iso}(X^{\prime})
.
Hence, with a slight abuse of notation,
X^{\prime}/H\cong Y^{\prime}
. By Theorem
2.13
again,
X^{\prime}
splits off
n-2
Euclidean factors.

Assume that every blow-down
(Y_{\infty},y_{\infty})
of
Y
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In this case, we claim that
y_{\infty}=0
. If not, by taking a blow-up of
Y_{\infty}
at
y_{\infty}
and using that the blow-up of a blow-down is still a blow-down, we obtain a blow-down of
Y
isometric to
\mathbb{R}
, thus reaching a contradiction.
∎

The combination of Lemma
3.1.4
below with Lemma
3.1.4
is clearly sufficient to prove Corollary
3.1
.

Lemma 3.41
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
and such that

(3.83)

\lim_{s\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{s}(p))}{s^{n}}=0\quad\text{for some }p\in X\,.

Then, either
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Y
isometrically, or the blow-down
X_{\infty}
of
X
is unique up to pointed isometry and isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
, where
Z\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
.
Moreover, if
(X_{\infty},H_{\infty})
is an equivariant blow-down of
(X,H)
, then
H_{\infty}^{2}:=\{h^{2}:h\in H_{\infty}\}
acts freely on
X_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Set
Y:=X/H
. By Lemma
3.1.4
, we can assume without loss of generality that the blow-down of
Y
is unique (up to pointed isometry) and pointed isometric to
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}},0)
.
Let
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},H_{\infty},x_{\infty})
be an equivariant blow-down of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},H)
. By Theorem
2.3
,
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}=\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with the orbit
x_{\infty}\cdot H_{\infty}
corresponding to
0\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
By Lemma
2.1
,
H_{\infty}
contains a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.
By (
3.83
),
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
is an
\RCD(0,n)
space with
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(X_{\infty})\leq n-1
. Thus, by Theorem
3.5
,
X_{\infty}:=\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z^{\prime}
, where
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{e}}(Z^{\prime})\leq 1
. Since
X_{\infty}/H_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
, by Lemma
3.1.1
we infer that
\dim_{\mathrm{e}}(Z^{\prime})=1
.
If a blow-down
X
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times K
where
K
is compact then by taking another blow-down we obtain a contradiction.
Therefore, all blow-downs of
X
are isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
with
Z\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
. Moreover, if
X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then
x_{\infty}\in\partial X_{\infty}
. Hence, the space of
pointed
blow-downs of
X
contains at most two elements. Since such space is connected by Lemma
2.2
, it follows that blow-down of
X
is unique up to pointed isometries.

To prove the second part of the statement,
let
G_{\infty}
denote a closed subgroup of
H_{\infty}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
obtained from Lemma
2.1
. Observe that
H_{\infty}
is abelian, since all the approximating groups along the blow-down sequence are.
The quotient group
H_{\infty}/G_{\infty}
acts effectively by measure preserving isometries on
X_{\infty}/G_{\infty}
. By Lemma
3.1.1
,
X_{\infty}/G_{\infty}
has essential dimension at most
1
, and
(X_{\infty}/G_{\infty})/(H_{\infty}/G_{\infty})\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
. It follows that
X_{\infty}/G_{\infty}
is isometric to either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and that
H_{\infty}/G_{\infty}
is either generated by a reflection of
\mathbb{R}
, or the identity on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In both cases, if
h\in H_{\infty}
, then
h^{2}\in G_{\infty}
. Since
G_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
,
h^{2}
acts freely on
X_{\infty}
.
∎

Corollary 3.42
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
, and assume that
X
does not split a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
.
Then there is
k\in\{n-2,n-1\}
such that the blow-down of
X
is unique (up to pointed isometry), pointed isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y,(0,y))
for some pointed metric space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y},y)
, and
\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y)\cdot(0,y)\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{y\}
.

Proof.

The conclusion follows combining Lemmas
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
with Lemma
2.2
.
∎

3.2.
Equivariant harmonic functions with almost linear growth

We continue to work under the assumption that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
is an
\RCD(0,n)
space such that
\mathrm{Iso}(X)
contains a closed subgroup
H\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
.
Our first goal in this section is to construct an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued harmonic map
u:X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with
k\in\{n-2,n-1\}
determined by the dimension of the maximal Euclidean factor split-off by the blow-down of
X
which is:

i)

equivariant with respect to the
H^{2}<H
-action;

ii)

almost-splitting up to linear transformation at every sufficiently large scale;

iii)

non-degenerate
along the
H^{2}
-orbit of the base-point, after the linear corrections appearing in ii).

For the precise meaning of the non-degeneracy condition in iii) we address the reader to the statement of Proposition
3.2
.

Proposition
3.2
below addresses the first two items of the plan.

Proposition 3.43
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
. Let
H^{2}:=\{h^{2}:h\in H\}
.
Then there exist
k\in\{n-2,n-1,n\}
and a harmonic map
u:X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfying the following:

(1)

For every
\gamma\in H
we have
u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)+u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
, and the map
\varphi:H^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
given by
\varphi(\gamma^{2}):=u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
is an injective group homomorphism.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
R_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
R>R_{\varepsilon}
there exists a positive definite lower triangular matrix
T_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that
T_{R}u:B_{R}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an
\varepsilon
-splitting map.

Proof.

The statement holds trivially if
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Z
. Thus we assume without loss of generality that this is not the case. By Lemmas
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
, the blow-down
X_{\infty}
of
X
is unique (up to isometry) and isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
for some
k\geq n-2
and some metric space
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y})
that does not split a line.
By Theorem
2.16
, there exists a harmonic map
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}):X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
vanishing in
p\in X
such that:

(1)

\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}\}
is a basis for

(3.84)

\Big\{v\in\mathsf{W}^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X):\Delta v=0,\text{and for every }\varepsilon>0\text{ there exist }R_{\varepsilon},C_{\varepsilon}>0:\\
v(x)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathsf{d}(x,p)^{1+\varepsilon}+C_{\varepsilon}\text{ for every }x\in X\Big\}\,.

(2)

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
R>R_{\varepsilon}
there exists a positive definite lower triangular matrix
T_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, such that
T_{R}u:B_{R}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an
\varepsilon
-splitting map.

Let
\gamma\in H
. We claim that
u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)+u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
. To this aim observe that along any sequence of rescalings converging to the equivariant blow-down
(X_{\infty},H_{\infty},p_{\infty})
of
(X,H)
,
\gamma
converges to a limiting element
\gamma_{\infty}\in H_{\infty}
by Lemma
2.1
and
\gamma^{2}
converges to
\gamma_{\infty}^{2}
. Since
\gamma_{\infty}
fixes
p_{\infty}
, by Lemmas
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
, we get that
\pi_{\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}(\gamma_{\infty}^{2})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
.

Consider a sequence
\varepsilon_{i}\downarrow 0
, and corresponding
R_{i}\uparrow+\infty
and
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that
T_{i}u:B_{R_{i}}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an
\varepsilon_{i}
-splitting map for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
By
1
, there exist matrices
A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, such that

(3.85)

\frac{T_{i}u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)}{R_{i}}=\frac{A_{i}T_{i}u(\cdot)+T_{i}u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)}{R_{i}}\,.

To conclude the proof of the claim, it suffices to show that
A_{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Since the sequence of maps
T_{i}u/R_{i}
converges to
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
in the blow-down and
\gamma^{2}
converges to an element
\gamma_{\infty}^{2}
such that
\pi_{\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}(\gamma_{\infty}^{2})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
, passing to the limit in (
3.85
) we obtain that
A_{i}\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
. Note that the matrices
A_{i}
are all similar to each other by (
3.85
). Thus, all their (complex) eigenvalues are equal to
1
. To conclude the proof it suffices to show that all the matrices
A_{i}
are diagonalizable. To this aim, let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
be such that

(3.86)

u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=Bu(\cdot)+u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)\,.

The matrix
B
is easily seen to be similar to the matrices
A_{i}
, so that it suffices to show that
B
is diagonalizable.
Iterating (
3.86
), we deduce

(3.87)

u\circ i\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=B^{i}u(\cdot)+u(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p)\quad\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\,,

where
i\gamma^{2}
denotes the sum in
H^{2}
of
i
copies of
\gamma^{2}
.
Let
M\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times k}
be the invertible matrix such that
MBM^{-1}=J
is the Jordan form of
B
.
From (
3.87
) we deduce that

(3.88)

Mu\circ i\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=J^{i}Mu(\cdot)+Mu(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p)\quad\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\,.

By the triangle inequality there exists
c>0
such that
\mathsf{d}(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p,p)\leq ci
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix now
R>0
. By the Cheng-Yau gradient estimate
[
51
, Theorem 1.2]
and the almost linear growth assumption in item
1
, there exists a constant
c(R,n)>0
such that

(3.89)

\sup_{B_{R}(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p)}|\nabla u|\leq c(R,n)i^{1/2}\quad\text{for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\,.

Hence, from (
3.88
) and (
3.89
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B_{R}(p)}|J^{i}Mu|=\sup_{x\in B_{R}(p)}|Mu(i\gamma^{2}\cdot x)-Mu(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p)|

(3.90)

\displaystyle\leq|M|R\,\sup_{B_{R}(i\gamma^{2}\cdot p)}|\nabla u|\leq Ci^{1/2}\,,

for some constant
C>0
.
Our next claim is that if
R>0
is sufficiently large then

(3.91)

\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\big(u(B_{R}(p))\big)>0\,.

Note that the claim is almost straightforward in the smooth Riemannian case. Indeed, if
R>0
is sufficiently large then
T_{R}u
is an almost splitting map. Thus, by a standard propagation of regularity argument originally due to
[
25
]
, there exists a point
x\in B_{R}(p)
such that
\{\nabla u_{1}(x),\dots,\nabla u_{k}(x)\}
are linearly independent. In the
\RCD
case one can argue in a similar way to find a regular point
x\in B_{R}(p)
and
0<r<R
such that
T_{R}u:B_{r}(x)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
is still an almost splitting map. Then one can notice that
T_{R}u:B_{r}(x)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
can be extended to an almost splitting map
\overline{T_{R}u}:B_{r/2}(x)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with the first
n-2
components given by those of
T_{R}u
). From
[
18
]
we have that
\mathscr{L}^{n}(\overline{T_{R}u}(B_{r}(x)))>0
. Thus, from Fubini we deduce that
\mathscr{L}^{n-2}(B_{r}(x))>0
and (
3.91
) follows.
By (
3.91
) there exist linearly independent vectors
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-2}\subset Mu(B_{R}(p))\subset\mathbb{C}^{n-2}
. Thus, possibly increasing
C>0
, (
3.43
) implies that

(3.92)

|J^{i}|\leq Ci^{1/2}\,.

If
J
is not diagonal, using that it has all eigenvalues equal to
1
, then at least one entry of
J^{i}
has modulus greater than
i
, a contradiction. This shows that
u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)+u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
, as we claimed.

Consider now the map
\varphi:H^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined by
\varphi(\gamma^{2}):=u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
. The map is a group homomorphism thanks to the identity
u\circ\gamma^{2}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)+u(\gamma^{2}\cdot p)
. Indeed, given any
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in H^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle u\circ\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}(\cdot)=

\displaystyle[u(\cdot)+u(\gamma_{1}\cdot p)]\circ\gamma_{2}=u\circ\gamma_{2}(\cdot)+u(\gamma_{1}\cdot p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u(\cdot)+u(\gamma_{1}\cdot p)+u(\gamma_{2}\cdot p)\,.

Therefore
\varphi(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})=\varphi(\gamma_{1})+\varphi(\gamma_{2})
.

To complete the proof, assume by contradiction that
\varphi
is not injective. Under such assumption there exists a subgroup
H^{\prime}<H
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
such that
u(H^{\prime}\cdot p)=\{0\}
. Since we assumed at the beginning of the proof that
X
does not split off
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
isometrically, by Corollary
3.1.4
the blow-down of
X
is pointed isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y,(0,y))
, with
Y
containing no lines, and it satisfies
\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y)\cdot(0,y)\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{y\}
.
Moreover, the orbit
H^{\prime}\cdot p
pointed Hausdorff converges along a blow down sequence to an orbit
H^{\prime}_{\infty}\cdot(0,y)
for some
H^{\prime}_{\infty}<\mathrm{Iso}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y)
. By Lemma
2.1
,
H_{\infty}
has a closed subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(H^{\prime}_{\infty}\cdot(0,y))
contains a set homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.
At the same time, by item
2
, the map
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}
is approximated, along a chosen blow down sequence, by maps of the form
T_{i}u/R_{i}
for some
R_{i}\uparrow+\infty
and some matrices
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. Since all these maps vanish on
H^{\prime}\cdot p
,
we deduce that
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(H^{\prime}_{\infty}\cdot(0,y))=\{0\}
, a contradiction.
∎

Th next lemma will be needed for the proof of Proposition
3.2
. The result holds for actions of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, but we only state it for
k=n-2
, as this is the case that we will use later on.

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a proper separable metric space with a closed subgroup
H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
and generated by
\{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n-2}\}
. Let
R>0
. Then, there are natural numbers
\{k_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n-2}
and
C>0
such that

(3.93)

R/2n\leq\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,k_{j}\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)\leq R/2n+C\quad\text{for }j=1,\dots,n-2\,,

and for every natural number
t<k_{j}
we have

(3.94)

\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,t\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)\leq R/2n\,.

Proof.

Let
k_{1}
be the smallest natural number such that

(3.95)

\mathsf{d}\Big(p,k_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdot p\Big)\geq R/2n\,.

Observe that such
k_{1}
exists since
\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1})\cdot p
is non-compact.
We claim that for every
j\in\{1,\cdots,n-3\}
we have

(3.96)

\limsup_{s\to+\infty}\mathsf{d}(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j})\cdot p,s\gamma_{j+1}\cdot p)=+\infty\,.

Assume by contradiction that this is not the case, i.e.,

(3.97)

\limsup_{s\to+\infty}\mathsf{d}(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j})\cdot p,s\gamma_{j+1}\cdot p)<c<+\infty\,.

Then for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
h_{s}\in\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j})
such that
\mathsf{d}(p,(h_{s}+s\gamma_{j+1})\cdot p)\leq c
. Up to a not relabelled subsequence,
h_{s}+s\gamma_{j+1}\to h_{\infty}\in H
, thus contradicting the assumption that
H\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
as a topological group. Hence (
3.96
) holds.

For every
j\in\{1,\cdots,n-3\}
, we let
k_{j}
be the smallest natural number such that

(3.98)

\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j})\cdot p,k_{j}\gamma_{j+1}\cdot p\Big)\geq R/2n\,.

These numbers exist by (
3.96
). At the same time, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(3.99)

\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma_{j}\cdot p)\leq C\quad\text{for every }j\in\{1,\cdots n-2\}\,.

Therefore

(3.100)

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,k_{j}\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\,\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,(k_{j}-1)\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)

(3.101)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{d}\Big((k_{j}-1)\gamma_{j}\cdot p,k_{j}\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)

(3.102)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,R/2n+C\,.

Combining with (
3.98
), we obtain

(3.103)

R/2n\leq\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,k_{j}\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)\leq R/2n+C\,,

thus concluding the proof.
∎

Proposition
3.2
formulates and establishes a precise version of the non-degeneracy condition referred to in item iii) of the plan discussed at the beginning of the section.

Proposition 3.45
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}\{X\}
. Assume that
X
does not split a factor
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
. Let
k\in\{n-2,n-1\}
be given by Corollary
3.1.4
such that the blow-down of
X
is unique and isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
, where
Y
does not split any line.
Let
u:X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the harmonic map given by Proposition
3.2
and let
\varepsilon_{i}\downarrow 0
,
R_{i}\uparrow+\infty
, and
T_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
be such that
T_{i}u:B_{R_{i}}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an
\varepsilon_{i}
-splitting map for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

If
k=n-1
, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
we let
\Sigma_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
be the hyperplane containing
T_{i}u(H^{2}\cdot p)
and let
\pi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\Sigma_{i}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
be the orthogonal projection on
\Sigma_{i}
.
Define
v_{i}:B_{R_{i}}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
by
v_{i}:=T_{i}u
if
k=n-2
, and
v_{i}:=\pi_{i}\circ T_{i}u
if
k=n-1
.
Then
v_{i}
is a
\varepsilon_{i}
-splitting map for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for every
i
sufficiently large

(3.104)

\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(v_{i}(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\})\Big)\geq C_{2}R_{i}^{n-2}.

Proof.

By Lemmas
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
, either
X\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Y

isometrically, or the blow-down of
X
is unique and isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y^{\prime}
for some

k\in\{n-2,n-1\}
.
Let
\{\gamma_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n-2}
be generators of
H^{2}
and, for every
i
, let
\{k_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{n-2}
be the natural numbers given by Lemma
3.2
relative to the scale
R_{i}
.
We divide the rest of the proof in three steps.

Step 1
: Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the
(n-2)
-dimensional vector space containing
u(H\cdot p)
and consider the map
{T_{i}}_{|\Sigma}:\Sigma\to\Sigma_{i}
. We claim that

(3.105)

\liminf_{i\to+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{det}({T_{i}}_{|\Sigma})\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}}{R^{n-2}_{i}}>0\,.

By construction of the
\{k_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{n-2}
(see Lemma
3.2
), for every
j\in\{2,\cdots,n-2\}
there exists
h_{i,j}\in\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{d}\Big(p,(h_{i,j}+k_{i,j}\gamma_{j})\cdot p\Big)}{R_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{d}\Big(\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j-1})\cdot p,k_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\cdot p\Big)}{R_{i}}

(3.106)

\displaystyle\to 1/2n\quad\text{as }i\uparrow+\infty\,.

Let
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},H^{2}_{\infty},p_{\infty})
be an equivariant blow-down obtained as limit of the sequence
(X,\mathsf{d}/R_{i},H^{2})
. Recall that
X_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y
, and set
p_{\infty}=(0,y_{\infty})
.
The groups
H^{2}_{j}:=\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{j})
converge, up to not relabelled subsequences, to groups
H^{2}_{\infty,j}<H^{2}_{\infty}
, and the orbits
H^{2}_{j}\cdot p
converge to
H^{2}_{\infty,j}\cdot p_{\infty}
. Thanks to (
3.45
) and Lemma
3.1.4
, the points
q_{i,j}:=(h_{i,j}+k_{i,j}\gamma_{j})\cdot p
converge to limit points

(3.107)

q_{\infty,j}\in H^{2}_{\infty,j}\cdot p_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{y_{\infty}\}\subset X_{\infty}

such that

(3.108)

\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(H^{2}_{\infty,j-1}\cdot p_{\infty},q_{\infty,j})=\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(p_{\infty},q_{\infty,j})=1/2n\,.

Thanks to (
3.107
) and (
3.108
) we have

(3.109)

\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{eucl}}(\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(H^{2}_{\infty,j-1}\cdot p_{\infty}),\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,j}))=1/2n\,.

The maps
T_{i}u/R_{i}
converge to the projection
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}:X_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
along the blow-down sequence, so that

(3.110)

T_{i}u/R_{i}(q_{i,j})\to\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,j})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\quad\text{as }i\uparrow+\infty\,.

The set
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(H^{2}_{\infty,j-1}\cdot p_{\infty})
is contained in a
(j-1)
-dimensional vector space since it is the limit of the nets
T_{i}u/R_{i}(H^{2}_{j-1}\cdot p)
. Moreover, it contains the set
\{\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,s})\}_{s=1}^{j-1}
. At the same time, by Lemma
2.1
,
H^{2}_{\infty,j-1}
contains a closed group isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{j-1}
. Thus, by invariance of the domain,
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(H^{2}_{\infty,j-1}\cdot p_{\infty})
coincides with the aforementioned
(j-1)
-dimensional vector space.
It follows from (
3.109
) that
\{\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,j})\}_{j=1}^{n-2}
are linearly independent. Hence we obtain that

(3.111)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{det}({T_{i}}_{|\Sigma})\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}}{R^{n-2}_{i}}\Big(u(\gamma_{1}\cdot p)\wedge\cdots\wedge u(\gamma_{n-2}\cdot p)\Big)

(3.112)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R^{n-2}_{i}}\Big([T_{i}u(q_{i,1})]\wedge\cdots\wedge[T_{i}u(q_{i,n-2})]\Big)

(3.113)

\displaystyle\to\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(q_{\infty,n-2})\neq 0\,,

thus completing the proof of (
3.105
).

Step 2
:
Arguing as we did in the proof of (
3.91
), we can show that
there exists
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the
\varepsilon_{i_{0}}
-splitting map
T_{i_{0}}u:B_{R_{i_{0}}}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies

(3.114)

\mathscr{L}^{k}(T_{i_{0}}(u(B_{R_{i_{0}}}(p))))>0\,.

Thus, setting
C_{1}:=R_{i_{0}}
, we have

(3.115)

\mathscr{L}^{k}(u(B_{C_{1}}(p)))>0\,.

Moreover, modulo passing to a sublattice of
H^{2}
, we may suppose that the sets
u(h\cdot B_{C_{1}}(p))
are all disjoint as
h
varies in
H^{2}
.
We claim that

(3.116)

\#\big((H^{2}\cdot p)\cap B_{R_{i}-C_{1}}(p)\big)\geq\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}\,.

To prove (
3.116
), fix
j\in\{2,\cdots,n-2\}
. For every natural number
m_{i,j}\leq k_{i,j}
there exists
h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}\in H^{2}_{j-1}
such that

(3.117)

\mathsf{d}(p,(h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}+m_{i,j}\gamma_{j})\cdot p)\leq R_{i}/2n+C<\frac{R_{i}-C_{1}}{n}\,,

where
C>0
is the constant of Lemma
3.2
and the last inequality holds for
i
large enough.
Hence by triangle inequality we have

(3.118)

\mathsf{d}\Big(p,\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}+m_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)\bigg]\cdot p\Big)<R_{i}-C_{1}\,,

for every

(3.119)

(m_{i,1},\cdots,m_{i,n-2})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-2}\cap\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}[1,k_{i,j}]\,.

Given (
3.118
) and (
3.119
), to prove (
3.116
) it suffices to establish the following claim: if

(3.120)

(m_{i,1},\cdots,m_{i,n-2})\neq(m^{\prime}_{i,1},\cdots,m^{\prime}_{i,n-2})\,,

then

(3.121)

\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}+m_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)\neq\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(h_{i,j}^{m^{\prime}_{i,j}}+m^{\prime}_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)\,.

To prove the claim, assume by contradiction that (
3.120
) holds but

(3.122)

\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}+m_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(h_{i,j}^{m^{\prime}_{i,j}}+m^{\prime}_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)\,.

Then
m_{i,n-2}=m^{\prime}_{i,n-2}
since
\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n-2}\}
is a basis of
H^{2}
and

(3.123)

\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}\,,\,\,\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}h_{i,j}^{m^{\prime}_{i,j}}\in\mathrm{span}(\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n-3})\,.

Hence
h_{i,n-2}^{m_{i,n-2}}=h_{i,n-2}^{m^{\prime}_{i,n-2}}
and thus

(3.124)

\sum_{j=1}^{n-3}\big(h_{i,j}^{m_{i,j}}+m_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-3}\big(h_{i,j}^{m^{\prime}_{i,j}}+m^{\prime}_{i,j}\gamma_{j}\big)\,.

Iterating this procedure, we deduce
(m_{i,1},\cdots,m_{i,n-2})=(m^{\prime}_{i,1},\cdots,m^{\prime}_{i,n-2})
, a contradiction.

Step 3
: To conclude the proof we treat the case
k=n-2
and
k=n-1
separately.

Assume first that
k=n-2
.
Using (
3.116
) and the equivariance of
u
, we obtain that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there holds

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(\frac{T_{i}}{R_{i}}\left(u\big(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\}\big)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{det}(T_{i})}{R_{i}^{n-2}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(u\big(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathrm{det}(T_{i})\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}}{R^{n-2}_{i}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(u(B_{C_{1}}(p))\Big)\,.

Combining with (
3.105
) and (
3.115
), we obtain the result for
k=n-2
.

Assume now that
k=n-1
.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{i}
be the set of affine hyperplanes of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
parallel to
\Sigma_{i}
. Observe that for every
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{n-2}(\pi_{i}\circ T_{i}(A))\geq

\displaystyle\,\sup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{i}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}(T_{i}(A)\cap H)

(3.125)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{i}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(T_{i}(A\cap T_{i}^{-1}(H))\Big)\,.

Let
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
be a unit vector normal to
\Sigma:=T_{i}^{-1}(\Sigma_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. We have that

\displaystyle\sup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{i}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(T_{i}(A\cap T_{i}^{-1}(H))\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(T_{i}\big((A+tv)\cap T_{i}^{-1}(\Sigma_{i})\big)\Big)

(3.126)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}({T_{i}}_{|\Sigma})\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\big((A+tv)\cap\Sigma\big)\,.

Applying (
3.45
) and (
3.45
) with

(3.127)

A:=u(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\})\,,

we deduce that

(3.128)

\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(\pi_{i}\circ\frac{T_{i}}{R_{i}}\left(u(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,p+H^{2})\leq C_{1}\}\right)\Big)\\
\geq\frac{\mathrm{det}({T_{i}}_{|\Sigma})}{R^{n-2}_{i}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\big([u(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\})+tv]\cap\Sigma\big)\,.

By (
3.116
),
[u(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\})+tv]\cap\Sigma
contains
\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}
disjoint copies of
[u(B_{C_{1}}(p))+tv]\cap\Sigma
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{n-2}\Big(\pi_{i}\circ\frac{T_{i}}{R_{i}}u(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,p+H^{2})\leq C_{1}\})\Big)

(3.129)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathrm{det}({T_{i}}_{|\Sigma})\Pi_{j=1}^{n-2}k_{i,j}}{R^{n-2}_{i}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{L}^{n-2}([u(B_{C_{1}}(p))+tv]\cap\Sigma)\,.

The statement then follows combining (
3.45
) with (
3.105
) and (
3.115
).
∎

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Let
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
denote the universal cover of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
. Since
\pi_{1}(X)
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}
there is a closed subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n-2}\cong H<\mathrm{Iso}(\overline{X})
.
By Corollary
3.1.4
, either
\overline{X}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Y

isometrically, or the blow-down of
\overline{X}
is unique up to isometry and isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times Y^{\prime}
for some

k\in\{n-2,n-1\}
.

Let
\varepsilon_{i}\downarrow 0
,

R_{i}\uparrow+\infty
,
and
v_{i}:B_{R_{i}}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
be the
numbers and the
\varepsilon_{i}
-splitting maps given by Proposition
3.2
.
Fix
\widetilde{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small.
By the slicing theorem, see
[
15
]
after
[
28
, Theorem 1.23]
, for every
i
large enough
there exists
A_{i,\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\subset B^{\mathbb{R}^{n-2}}_{R_{i}/2}(0)
with
\mathscr{L}^{n-2}(B^{\mathbb{R}^{n-2}}_{R_{i}/2}(0)\setminus A_{i,\widetilde{\varepsilon}})<\widetilde{\varepsilon}R_{i}^{n-2}
such that the following holds:
For every
x\in v_{i}^{-1}(A_{i,\widetilde{\varepsilon}})\cap B_{R_{i}}(p)
, and for every
r\in(0,R_{i}/2]
, there exists a matrix
A_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-2)\times(n-2)}
such that
A_{r}{v_{i}}_{|B_{r}(x)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
is an
\widetilde{\varepsilon}
-splitting map.

By (
3.104
) in Proposition
3.2
, there exists
C_{1}>0
such that for
i
large enough we have

(3.130)

A_{i,\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\cap v_{i}\big(\{x\in B_{R_{i}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\}\big)\neq\emptyset\,.

Fix now a sequence
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{j}\downarrow 0
. For every
j\in\mathbb{N}
let
i(j)
be such that

(3.131)

A_{i(j),\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{j}}\cap v_{i(j)}\big(\{x\in B_{R_{i(j)}}(p):\mathsf{d}(x,H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}\}\big)\neq\emptyset\,.

Accordingly, for every
j
we take
x_{j}\in B_{R_{i(j)}}(p)
such that

i)

v_{i(j)}(x_{j})\in A_{i(j),\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{j}}
;

ii)

\mathsf{d}(x_{j},H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}
.

Fix any
R\gg 1
. Applying
[
17
, Theorem 3.8]
(after
[
28
, Lemma 1.21]
) we obtain a sequence
\delta_{j}\downarrow 0
such that the balls
B_{R}(x_{j})
are
\delta_{j}R
-close in GH sense to balls

(3.132)

B_{R}^{\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Y_{j}}((0,y_{i}))\,,

for some pointed metric spaces
(Y_{j},y_{j})
. Since
\mathsf{d}(x_{j},H^{2}\cdot p)\leq C_{1}
, modulo applying an isometry of
H^{2}
, we can assume that
x_{j}\in\overline{B}_{C_{1}}(p)
for every
j
.
Up to another subsequence,
x_{j}\to x_{\infty}\in\overline{B}_{C_{1}}(p)
, so that
B_{R}(x_{\infty})
is isometric to
B_{R}^{\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times Y}((0,y))
for some pointed metric space
(Y,y)
. Hence, the same holds for
B_{R-C_{1}}(p)
. Since
R\gg 1
is arbitrary, the statement follows.

4.
A vanishing theorem for the simplicial volume

The goal of this section is to prove the following vanishing result for the simplicial volume of
\RCD(0,n)
manifolds:

Theorem 4.1
.

For every
n\geq 2
, the following holds. If
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
is an
\RCD(0,n)
space and
X
is an oriented triangulable topological
n
-manifold, then
||X||=0
.

Theorem
1.7
clearly follows from Theorem
4.1
, since smooth manifolds are triangulable by
[
90
]
.

Remark 4.2
.

The assumption that
X
is triangulable is related to the presence of the same assumption in
[
39
, Corollary 11]
on which our proof depends. As remarked in
[
39
]
it seems possible that such assumption is not necessary.

The closest available result in the literature is due to J. Lott and Z. Shen
[
65
, Corollary 1.1]
. They show that a complete Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)
with quadratic curvature decay and such that

(4.1)

\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}=0

for some
p\in M
satisfies
||M||=0
.

For the proof of Theorem
4.1
we are going to distinguish two cases: the Euclidean volume growth case, i.e., the case when

(4.2)

\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}>0\,,

and the non-Euclidean volume growth case, when

(4.3)

\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}=0\,,

discussed in Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
respectively.
In both cases, the key technical tool is provided by
[
39
, Corollary 11]
, which we state below as Theorem
4.6
. We will need some background and terminology which we proceed to recall.

Definition 4.3

(Amenable subsets of a topological space)
.

Let
X
be a path-connected topological space,
Y
be a subset of
X
and
i:Y\to X
be the inclusion map. We say that
Y
is amenable (in
X
) if for every path-connected component
Y^{\prime}
of
Y
the image of
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})\to\pi_{1}(X)
is an amenable subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X)
.

Definition 4.4

(Covers and multiplicity)
.

Let
X
be a path-connected topological space, and
let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a cover of
X
, i.e.
U_{i}\subset X
for every
i\in I
and
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
. We say that the cover is open if
U_{i}
is open in
X
for every
i\in I
, and amenable if each if
U_{i}
is amenable in
X
for every
i\in I
. The multiplicity of
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{mult}\big(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}\big):=\sup\{n\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\exists\,i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in I\,,\,i_{h}\neq i_{k}

\displaystyle\text{ for }h\neq k,\,U_{i_{1}}\cap\dots\cap U_{i_{n}}\neq\emptyset\}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\,.

Definition 4.5
.

A sequence of subsets
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of a path-connected non-compact topological space
X
is said to be amenable at infinity if there exists a sequence
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of open subsets of
X
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

each
W_{i}
is large, i.e. its complement in
X
is relatively compact;

(ii)

the family
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is locally finite;

(iii)

U_{i}\subset W_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
;

(iv)

there exists
\overline{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
U_{i}
is an amenable subset of
W_{i}
for every
i\geq\overline{i}
.

The following statement corresponds to
[
39
, Corollary 11]
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
M^{n}
be an oriented, open, and triangulable manifold of dimension
n
. Suppose that
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an amenable open cover of
M
such that each
U_{i}
is relatively compact in
M
, the sequence
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is amenable at infinity and
\mathrm{mult}\big(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\leq n
. Then
\left\lVert M\right\rVert=0
.

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.1
: Euclidean volume growth case

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4.1
under the assumption that the space has maximal volume growth. Thanks to Theorem
4.6
, it is sufficient to prove Theorem
4.7
below.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space. If
X
has maximal volume growth,
then it admits a cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
which is amenable, open, amenable at infinity and has
\mathrm{mult}\big(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\leq 2
.

Theorem
4.7
, in turn, is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.8
below, which is the focus of this section.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with maximal volume growth

(4.4)

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}\geq v>0.

Then, for every
r
sufficiently large,
B_{4r}(p)\setminus B_{3r}(p)
is contained in a path-connected component
C_{r}
of
B_{5r}(p)\setminus B_{2r}(p)
. Moreover, there exists a constant
C(n,v)>0
such that
the image through the push-forward of the inclusion map

(4.5)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(C_{r},c)\to\pi_{1}(B_{6r}(p)\setminus B_{r}(p),c)

has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
for every sufficiently large
r>0
and every
c\in C_{r}
.

The proof of the first part of the statement of Theorem
4.8
is straightforward.
To prove (
4.5
) we argue by contradiction. Hence, we find a sequence of annuli
A_{i}:=B_{6r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{r_{i}}(p)
such that the images of the maps as in (
4.5
) have large cardinality.
Then we consider their universal coverings
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, and suitable subsets
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}\subset\widetilde{A}_{i}
which are coverings of the corresponding path-connected components
C_{r_{i}}
.
Thanks to our assumption by contradiction, the coverings
\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\prime}
are non-compact. At the same time, scaling down distances, the sets
C_{r_{i}}
converge to a metric space
Y
which is isometric to an annulus in a metric cone over an
\RCD(n-2,n-1)
space with volume bounded below by a constant depending on
v
. Such annulus is homotopically equivalent to the cross-section. Thus, in particular, if
n\geq 3
then
\#\pi_{1}(Y)<C(n,v)
as an application of
[
72
, Theorem 1.2]
.
Similarly, the coverings
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}
equipped with their deck transformations converge in equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff sense to a pair
(\widetilde{Y},H)
with
H<\mathrm{Iso}(\widetilde{Y})
such that
\widetilde{Y}/H\cong Y
isometrically.

To finish the proof, we are going to combine the statement that
\#\pi_{1}(Y)<C(n,v)
with some ideas originally developed in
[
5
,
6
,
95
]
to deduce that
H
has also cardinality bounded by
C(n,v)
. This then leads to a contradiction.

Making completely rigorous the first part of the plan sketched above involves a number of steps. The proofs of the next two lemmas follow the lines of
[
95
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(K,n)
space with

(4.6)

\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{1}(p))\geq v>0.

Then there exists
k(v,n,K)\in\mathbb{N}
such that the image through the push-forward of the inclusion map

(4.7)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{1}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{4}(p),p)

contains at most
k
elements that can be represented by loops of length less than or equal to
2
centered in
p
.

Proof.

We denote the image of
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{1}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{4}(p),p)
by
G
.
Let
\pi:Y\to B_{4}(p)
be the universal covering of
B_{4}(p)
. Consider on
Y
the distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
obtained by lifting the length structure of
B_{4}(p)
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}
denote
the corresponding Hausdorff measure.
Let
\widetilde{p}\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, and let
F\subset Y
be the set containing
\widetilde{p}
constructed in Lemma
A
.
Call
G_{2}\subset G
the set of elements that can be represented by loops of length at most
2
centered in
p
. Let

(4.8)

B:=\bigcup_{g\in G_{2}}g(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1}(\widetilde{p}))\subset Y\,.

We claim that
B\subset B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{3}(\widetilde{p})
. Indeed, if
g\in G_{2}
, we can join
\widetilde{p}
to
g(\widetilde{p})
with a curve of length at most
2
and we can join
g(\widetilde{p})
to any element of
g(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1}(\widetilde{p}))
by a curve of length at most
1
. Hence,
B\subset B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{3}(\widetilde{p})
by triangle inequality.

Combining such inclusion with Lemma
A
we get

(4.9)

\displaystyle(\#G_{2})\cdot\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{1}(p))=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{g\in G_{2}}\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}\big(g(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1}(\widetilde{p}))\big)

(4.10)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(B)\leq\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{3}(\widetilde{p}))\,.

Thus, by Lemma
A
, it holds

(4.11)

(\#G_{2})\cdot\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{1}(p))\leq v_{K,n}(3),

where
v_{K,N}(r)
denotes the volume of the ball of radius
r
in the model space for the
\CD(K,N)
condition.
Hence, combining with (
4.6
), we obtain that

(4.12)

\#G_{2}\leq v_{K,n}(3)/v\,,

thus concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with

(4.13)

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}\geq v>0\,.

Then there exist
\varepsilon(n,v)>0
,
C(n,v)\in\mathbb{N}
such that the image through the push-forward of the inclusion map

(4.14)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{1}(p),p)

has cardinality at most
C
.

Proof.

Let
m_{0}:=\lceil 1+\omega_{n}3^{n}/v\rceil
, and choose
\varepsilon(n,v):=(10m_{0})^{-1}
. We denote the image of
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{1}(p),p)
by
G
. It is a standard observation that
G
can be generated by loops of length at most
2\varepsilon
. Thus, by Lemma
4.1
,
G
is generated by at most
k(n,v)
elements
\{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}\}
of length at most
2\varepsilon
centered in
p
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
U(m)
be the ball of radius
m
in
G
centered in the identity w.r.t. the word metric and the set of generators considered above.
Let
\pi:Y\to B_{1}(p)
be the universal covering of
B_{1}(p)
and let
\widetilde{p}
be a preimage of
p
.
Endow
Y
with the distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
induced by the lift of the length structure of
B_{1}(p)
(cf. with Remark
A
) and the corresponding Hausdorff measure
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}
. Let
F\subset Y
be the set containing
\widetilde{p}
as in Lemma
A
.

We define

(4.15)

B:=\bigcup_{g\in U(m_{0})}g(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p}))\subset Y\,.

We claim that if
x\in B
then
x
is joined to
\widetilde{p}
by a curve of length at most
3m_{0}\varepsilon
. Indeed, if

(4.16)

x\in g(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p}))\quad\text{for some}\quad g\in U(m_{0})\,,

then there is a curve from
\widetilde{p}
to
g(\widetilde{p})
of length at most
2m_{0}\varepsilon
, and another curve of length at most
m_{0}\varepsilon
joining
g(\widetilde{p})
and
x
.

Since
B\subset B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{3m_{0}\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p})
, Lemma
A
implies that

(4.17)

\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(B)\leq\omega_{n}(3m_{0}\varepsilon)^{n}\,.

We claim that
\#G\leq\#U(m_{0})
. Note that if the claim holds then the statement immediately follows.

Assume by contradiction that

(4.18)

\#G>\#U(m_{0})\,.

Since
\#U(m+1)>\#U(m)
unless
U(m)=G
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
m_{0}\leq\#U(m_{0})
. Hence, we obtain that

(4.19)

m_{0}\cdot\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(p))\leq\#U(m_{0})\cdot\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p}))=\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(B)\,.

Combining with (
4.17
), we deduce that

(4.20)

m_{0}\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(p))\leq\omega_{n}(3m_{0}\varepsilon)^{n}\,.

Exploiting (
4.13
), we obtain that

(4.21)

m_{0}\leq\omega_{n}(3m_{0}\varepsilon)^{n}/\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{m_{0}\varepsilon}(p))\leq\omega_{n}3^{n}/v\,,

thus contradicting our choice of
m_{0}
.
∎

The following lemma is well-known (cf. with
[
94
]
), and it can be obtained arguing as for the proof of the analogous result in the smooth setting, see
[
6
]
. We discuss the proof for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
K>0
and let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be an
\RCD(K,n)
space with

(4.22)

\mathscr{H}^{n}(X)\geq v>0\,.

Then,
\pi_{1}(X)
has cardinality at most
C(n,v,K)
.

Proof.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n})
be the universal covering of
X
, which is simply connected and is an
\RCD(K,n)
space by
[
89
]
and
[
72
]
. By Bonnet-Myers
[
87
, Corollary 2.6]
, it holds
\mathrm{diam}(\widetilde{X})\leq C(n,K)
, so that Bishop-Gromov’s inequality
[
87
, Theorem 2.3]
implies that
{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(\widetilde{X})\leq C(n,K)
. Consider now a fundamental domain
F\subset\widetilde{X}
as given by Lemma
A
. For every
g\in\pi_{1}(X)
, it holds
g\cdot F\cap F=\emptyset
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(g\cdot F)=\mathscr{H}^{n}(X)
. Hence,

(4.23)

\#\pi_{1}(X)\,v\leq\#\pi_{1}(X)\,\mathscr{H}^{n}(X)\leq\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}(\widetilde{X})\leq C(n,K)\,,

concluding the proof.
∎

The next two lemmas contain technical results concerning sequences of metric spaces converging in the pGH sense to metric cones.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
Z=C(Y)
be the metric cone defined over some metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
with
{\rm diam}(Y)\leq\pi
and let
\mathsf{d}
be the associated cone distance. Calling
\overline{p}
its tip, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, we define the annuli

(4.24)

A:=B_{6}(\overline{p})\setminus\overline{B}_{1}(\overline{p})\,,\quad A_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{B}_{6-\varepsilon}(\overline{p})\setminus B_{1+\varepsilon}(\overline{p})\,,\quad A^{\varepsilon}:=\overline{B}_{6+\varepsilon}(\overline{p})\setminus B_{1-\varepsilon}(\overline{p})\,.

Denote by
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}
,
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}
the length distances induced by
\mathsf{d}
on the annuli
A
,
A_{\varepsilon}
and
A^{\varepsilon}
, respectively. Then, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
we have that

(4.25)

\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\leq\widehat{\mathsf{d}}(p,q)\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,,

for every
p,q\in\widetilde{A}:=\overline{B}_{5}(\overline{p})\setminus B_{2}(\overline{p})
.

Proof.

Recall that
Z=C(Y)
is defined as

(4.26)

Z=(Y\times[0,\infty))/\sim\,,

where the equivalence relation
\sim
identifies all points of the form
(y,0)
for all
y\in Y
, and the cone distance
\mathsf{d}
is defined by

(4.27)

\mathsf{d}\big((x,r),(y,s)\big)=\sqrt{r^{2}+s^{2}-2rs\,\cos(d_{Y}(x,y))},\quad\text{where }x,y\in Y,\,r,s\geq 0\,.

We are only going to prove the first inequality in (
4.25
), as the proof of the second is analogous. Given any two points
p,q\in\widetilde{A}
, for every sufficiently small
\delta>0
there exists a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to A
such that
\gamma(0)=p
,
\gamma(1)=q
and
\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma)<\widehat{\mathsf{d}}(p,q)+\delta
. If
\gamma(t)=(y(t),r(t))
, with
y:[0,1]\to Y
and
r:[0,1]\to(1,6)
, we define
\widetilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\to A_{\varepsilon}
as

(4.28)

\widetilde{\gamma}(t):=(y(t),\widetilde{r}(t)):=\big(y(t),(1+\varepsilon)\vee r(t)\wedge(6-\varepsilon)\big)\,.

Since
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
,
\widetilde{\gamma}(0)=p
and
\widetilde{\gamma}(1)=q
.
Then, we observe that, for every
s,t\in[0,1]
, there holds

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s),\widetilde{\gamma}(t))^{2}=

\displaystyle(\widetilde{r}(s)-\widetilde{r}(t))^{2}+2\widetilde{r}(s)\widetilde{r}(t)\,\big(1-\cos(d_{Y}(y(s),y(t)))\big)

(4.30)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(r(s)-r(t))^{2}+2(1+\varepsilon)^{2}r(s)r(t)\,\big(1-\cos(d_{Y}(y(s),y(t))\big)

(4.31)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\mathsf{d}(\gamma(s),\gamma(t))^{2}\,.

Thus, we conclude that

(4.32)

\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\leq\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{\gamma})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma)<(1+\varepsilon)(\widehat{\mathsf{d}}(p,q)+\delta)\,.
