Since (
4.32
) holds for every
\delta>0
, the first inequality in (
4.25
) follows.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},\overline{p})
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with

(4.33)

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(\overline{p}))}{r^{n}}\geq v>0\,.

Let
r_{i}\uparrow+\infty
be a sequence such that
(X,\mathsf{d}_{i}:=\mathsf{d}/r_{i},\overline{p})
converges in the pGH sense to a metric cone
(C(Y),\mathsf{d}_{\infty},p_{\infty})
. For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set
A_{i}:=B_{6r_{i}}(\overline{p})\setminus\overline{B}_{r_{i}}(\overline{p})
and
\widetilde{A}_{i}:=\overline{B}_{5r_{i}}(\overline{p})\setminus B_{2r_{i}}(\overline{p})
. Moreover, we define
A:=B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{6}(p_{\infty})\setminus\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1}(p_{\infty})
and
\widetilde{A}:=\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{5}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{2}(p_{\infty})
. For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we call
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
the length distance induced by
\mathsf{d}_{i}
on
A_{i}
and, similarly, we call
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}
the length distance induced by
\mathsf{d}_{\infty}
on
A
. Then,
(\widetilde{A}_{i},\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i})
converges in the GH sense to
(\widetilde{A},\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty})
.

Proof.

Since
(X,\mathsf{d}_{i},\overline{p})
converges in the pGH sense to
(C(Y),\mathsf{d}_{\infty},p_{\infty})
, there exists a sequence
(\varepsilon_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to 0
such that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
there are
\varepsilon_{i}
-GH maps
f_{i}:B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\to B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(\overline{p})
and
g_{i}:B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(\overline{p})\to B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})
with
f_{i}(\overline{p})=p_{\infty}
and
g_{i}(p_{\infty})=\overline{p}
. In particular,
f_{i}\big(B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\big)
is an
\varepsilon_{i}
-net in
B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(\overline{p})
,
g_{i}\big(B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(\overline{p})\big)
is an
\varepsilon_{i}
-net in
B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})
and

(4.34)

\begin{split}|\mathsf{d}_{i}(f_{i}(x_{\infty}),f_{i}(y_{\infty}))-\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})|<\varepsilon_{i}\,,\quad&\forall\,x_{\infty},y_{\infty}\in B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\,,\\
|\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(g_{i}(x_{i}),g_{i}(y_{i}))-\mathsf{d}_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})|<\varepsilon_{i}\,,\quad&\forall\,x_{i},y_{i}\in B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(\overline{p})\,.\end{split}

Moreover, we can construct such
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
so that

(4.35)

\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(g_{i}(f_{i}(x_{\infty}),x_{\infty}))<2\varepsilon_{i},\quad\forall x_{\infty}\in B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1/\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\,.

In the following we are implicitly going to consider only indexes
i
sufficiently large so that
\varepsilon_{i}<1/7
and thus
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
are defined on
B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{7}(p_{\infty})
and
B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{7}(\overline{p})
, respectively.

Fix any two points
p,q\in\widetilde{A}
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Consider the annulus

(4.36)

A_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{6-\varepsilon}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1+\varepsilon}(p_{\infty})\subset C(Y)\,.

According to Lemma
4.1
, take a constant-speed curve
\gamma_{\varepsilon}:[0,1]\to A_{\varepsilon}
such that
\gamma_{\varepsilon}(0)=p
,
\gamma_{\varepsilon}(1)=q
and
\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}(\gamma_{\varepsilon})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)
. Partition the interval
[0,1]
with times
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{N}=1
such that
|t_{j+1}-t_{j}|<\varepsilon(8\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q))^{-1}
for every
j=0,\dots,N-1
. Setting
x_{j}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(t_{j})
for
j=0,\dots,N
, we have that

(4.37)

\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(x_{j},x_{j+1})<\varepsilon/4\,,\quad\text{for }j=0,\dots,N-1\,.

Moreover,

(4.38)

\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(x_{j},x_{j+1})\leq\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}(\gamma_{\varepsilon})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)\,.

Take any
i\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large such that
\varepsilon_{i}<\varepsilon/8
. Setting
x^{i}_{j}=f_{i}(x_{j})
and recalling that
f_{i}
is a
\varepsilon_{i}
-GH map, on the one hand we have that

(4.39)

\mathsf{d}_{i}(x_{j}^{i},x_{j+1}^{i})<\varepsilon_{i}+\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(x_{j},x_{j+1})<\varepsilon/2\,,\quad\text{for }j=0,\dots,N-1\,.

On the other hand, recalling that
f_{i}(\overline{p})=p_{\infty}
, we get that

(4.40)

x_{j}^{i}\in\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{6-3\varepsilon/4}(\overline{p})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}_{1+3\varepsilon/4}(\overline{p})\subset X_{i}\,,\quad\text{for }j=0,\dots,N\,.

Combining (
4.39
) and (
4.40
), we deduce that
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(x_{j}^{i},x_{j+1}^{i})=\mathsf{d}_{i}(x_{j}^{i},x_{j+1}^{i})
for
j=0,\dots,N-1
. Thus, introducing the simplified notations
p_{i}=f_{i}(p)=f_{i}(x_{0})=x_{0}^{i}
and
q_{i}=f_{i}(q)=f_{i}(x_{N})=x_{N}^{i}
, we obtain that

(4.41)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(x^{i}_{0},x^{i}_{N})\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(x^{i}_{j},x^{i}_{j+1})

(4.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{d}_{i}(x^{i}_{j},x^{i}_{j+1})\leq\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(x_{j},x_{j+1})+\varepsilon_{i}N

(4.43)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\varepsilon)\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)+\varepsilon_{i}N\,.

Consider a constant-speed curve
\eta_{i}:[0,1]\to A_{i}
such that
\eta_{i}(0)=p_{i}
,
\eta_{i}(1)=q_{i}

and
\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}(\eta_{i})=\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})
. Partition the interval
[0,1]
with times
s_{0}=0<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{M}=1
such that
|s_{j+1}-s_{j}|<\varepsilon(16\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)+\varepsilon N)^{-1}
. Then, calling
y^{i}_{j}=\eta_{i}(s_{j})
for
j=0,\dots,M
, the inequality (
4.41
) guarantees that

(4.44)

\mathsf{d}_{i}(y^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j+1})<\varepsilon/8\,.

Define
z^{i}_{j}:=g_{i}(y^{i}_{j})\in C(Y)
for
j=0,\dots,M
, and observe that by construction (see (
4.35
)) we have that

(4.45)

\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{0},p)<2\varepsilon_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{M},q)<2\varepsilon_{i}\,.

On the other hand, since
g_{i}
is a
\varepsilon_{i}
-GH map,

(4.46)

\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})<\varepsilon_{i}+\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(y^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j+1})<\varepsilon/2\,,\quad\text{for }j=0,\dots,M-1\,.

Moreover, recalling that
g_{i}(p_{\infty})=\overline{p}
, we get that

(4.47)

z_{j}^{i}\in\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{6+\varepsilon/4}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1-\varepsilon/4}(p_{\infty})\subset C(Y)\,,\quad\text{for }j=0,\dots,M\,.

Therefore

(4.48)

\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})\geq\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\mathsf{d}_{i}(y^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j+1})\geq\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})-\varepsilon_{i}M\\
\geq\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{0},p)+\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})+\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{M},q)-\varepsilon_{i}(M+4)\,.

Let
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}
denote the length distance on

\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{6+\varepsilon}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{1-\varepsilon}(p_{\infty})
.
Combining (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), we know that
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})=\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})
for
j=0,\dots,M-1
, and similarly
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z^{i}_{0},p)=\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{0},p)
and
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z^{i}_{M},q)=\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(z^{i}_{M},q)
. Then, the right-hand side of (
4.48
) is equal to

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z^{i}_{0},p)+\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z_{j}^{i},z_{j+1}^{i})+\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(z^{i}_{M},q)-\varepsilon_{i}(M+4)

\displaystyle\geq\widehat{\mathsf{d}}^{\varepsilon}(p,q)-\varepsilon_{i}(M+4)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)-\varepsilon_{i}(M+4)\,,

where we applied Lemma
4.1
for the last inequality.
In particular,

(4.49)

\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)-\varepsilon_{i}(M+4)\,.

Since
p
,
q
and
\varepsilon
were chosen arbitrarily and
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}
is clearly bounded on
\widetilde{A}\times\widetilde{A}
, the combination of (
4.41
) and (
4.49
) implies that given any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
N(\varepsilon)
such that for every
i\geq N(\varepsilon)
we have

(4.50)

\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(f_{i}(p),f_{i}(q))-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)\big|\leq\varepsilon/3\,,\quad\forall p,q\in\widetilde{A}\,.

Consider the closest point projection
\pi:C(Y)\to\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{5-\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{2+\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})
and observe that

(4.51)

\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),p)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\,,\quad\forall p\in\widetilde{A}\,.

Thus for every
p,q\in\widetilde{A}
and every
i\geq N(\varepsilon)
we have that

(4.52)

\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(f_{i}(\pi(p)),f_{i}(\pi(q)))-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)\big|\\
\leq\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(f_{i}(\pi(p)),f_{i}(\pi(q)))-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),\pi(q))\big|+\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),\pi(q))-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)\big|\\
<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),\pi(q))-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),q)\big|+\big|\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(\pi(p),q)-\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(p,q)\big|<\varepsilon\,.

On the other hand, since
f_{i}
is an
\varepsilon_{i}
-GH map,

(4.53)

\mathsf{d}_{i}\big(x_{i},f_{i}\big(\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{5+2\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{2-2\varepsilon_{i}}(p_{\infty})\big)\big)\leq\varepsilon_{i}\,,\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\widetilde{A}_{i}\,.

Therefore, noting that

(4.54)

f_{i}\big(\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{5-\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{2+\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})\big)\subset\widetilde{A}_{i}

for every
i
sufficiently large, we have that

(4.55)

f_{i}(\pi(\widetilde{A}))=f_{i}\big(\overline{B}^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{5-\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})\setminus B^{\mathsf{d}_{\infty}}_{2+\varepsilon/3}(p_{\infty})\big)

is a
(4\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon/3)
-net in
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, with respect to
\mathsf{d}_{i}
. Thus, up to taking a bigger
N(\varepsilon)
, for every
i\geq N(\varepsilon)
we have that
f_{i}(\pi(\widetilde{A}))
is a
\varepsilon
-net in
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, with respect to
\mathsf{d}_{i}
. Since the distances
\mathsf{d}_{i}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
coincide when restricted to balls of radius
\frac{1}{2}
centered in
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small we conclude that
f_{i}(\pi(\widetilde{A}))
is an
\varepsilon
-net in
\widetilde{A}_{i}
with respect to
\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
.
This argument proves that for every
i\geq N(\varepsilon)
the map
f_{i}\circ\pi:(\widetilde{A},\widehat{\mathsf{d}})\to(\widetilde{A}_{i},\widehat{\mathsf{d}}_{i})
is an
\varepsilon
-GH map. The conclusion follows from the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.8
.

We first show that, for every
r
sufficiently large,
B_{4r}(p)\setminus B_{3r}(p)
is contained in a path-connected component
C_{r}
of
B_{5r}(p)\setminus B_{2r}(p)
.

Assume that this is not the case, so that there exists a sequence
r_{i}\uparrow+\infty
violating the statement.
Hence, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there are two points
x_{i},y_{i}\in B_{4r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{3r_{i}}(p)
such that no path in
B_{5r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{2r_{i}}(p)
connects them.
By
[
33
,
34
]
after
[
24
, Theorem 7.6]
, up to passing to a subsequence, the spaces
(X,\mathsf{d}/r_{i})
converge in pGH sense to a metric cone
C(Z)
centered in its tip, where
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z})
is a path-connected metric space. Hence, there are limit points
x_{i}\to x_{\infty}
,
y_{i}\to y_{\infty}
in
\overline{B}^{C(Z)}_{4}(p)\setminus{B}^{C(Z)}_{3}(p)
. Consider a collection
\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}\subset\overline{B}^{C(Z)}_{4}(p)\setminus{B}^{C(Z)}_{3}(p)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
z_{1}=x_{\infty}
,
z_{k}=y_{\infty}
and
\mathsf{d}^{C(Z)}(z_{j},z_{j+1})\leq 1/2
, for every
j\in\{1,\cdots,k-1\}
. For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and every
j\in\{2,\cdots,k-1\}
consider points
x^{i}_{j}\in B_{5r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{2r_{i}}(p)
such that
x^{i}_{j}\to z_{j}
, and set
x^{i}_{1}:=x_{i}
and
x^{i}_{k}:=y_{i}
. Consider a curve obtained by concatenating geodesics connecting
z^{i}_{j}
and
z^{i}_{j+1}
as
j
varies between
1
and
k-1
. For
i
large enough, this curve is contained in
B_{5r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{2r_{i}}(p)
, a contradiction.

Let
L>0
be large to be chosen later, and assume by contradiction that the images of the maps in (
4.5
) have cardinality greater than
L
for some sequence
r_{i}\uparrow+\infty
. Let
A_{i}:=B_{6r_{i}}(p)\setminus\overline{B}_{r_{i}}(p)
, and let
\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
be the induced length distance on
A_{i}
.
Since
A_{i}
is an open set of an
\RCD(0,n)
space, it is semi-locally simply connected by
[
89
]
. Hence, there exists a simply connected universal covering
\widetilde{A}_{i}
of
A_{i}
.
On
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, we consider the distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}
obtained by lifting the length structure of
A_{i}
as in Remark
A
. The deck transformations of this covering form of a group of isometries of
(\widetilde{A}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i})
isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(A_{i})
, and the corresponding quotient space is isometric to
(A_{i},\overline{\mathsf{d}_{i}})
.

Let
p_{i}:\widetilde{A}_{i}\to A_{i}
be the covering map, and let
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}
be a path-connected component of
p_{i}^{-1}(C_{r_{i}})
.
Let
G_{i}<\pi_{1}(A_{i})
be the subgroup of deck transformations of
p_{i}:\widetilde{A}_{i}\to A_{i}
sending
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}
into itself.
Then, since
G_{i}
acts freely on
\widetilde{A}_{i}
, it can be naturally identified with a group of isometries of
(\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i})
. Moreover, since
\pi_{1}(A_{i})
acts transitively on the fibers of
p_{i}:\widetilde{A}_{i}\to A_{i}
, it follows that
G_{i}
acts transitively on the fibers of the restriction

(4.56)

{p_{i}}|_{\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}}:\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}\to C_{r_{i}}\,.

Thus,
(\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i})/G_{i}\cong(C_{r_{i}},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i})
isometrically.
In addition, since loops contained in
C_{r_{i}}
can be lifted to paths contained in
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}
, each group
G_{i}
contains an isomorphic copy of the image of
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(C_{r_{i}},\overline{c}_{i})\to\pi_{1}(A_{i},\overline{c}_{i})
where
\overline{c}_{i}\in C_{r_{i}}
.

The spaces
(\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})
are locally uniformly doubling by Lemma
A
. Hence, modulo passing to a subsequence, one has the equivariant GH convergence

(4.57)

(\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i},G_{i})\to(Z,\mathsf{d}_{z},G)\,.

Observe now that by an argument similar to the one used at the beginning of the proof, for every
\varepsilon>0
, each
(C_{r_{i}},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})
is
\varepsilon
-close to
(B_{5r_{i}}(p)\setminus B_{2r_{i}}(p),\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})
in GH sense for
i
large enough. Combining with Lemma
4.1
, we deduce that
the spaces
(C_{r_{i}},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})
converge to an annulus
(C_{\infty},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty})
of a tangent cone at infinity
C(Y)
of
X
, where
Y
is a non-collapsed
\RCD(n-2,n-1)
space, and
\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}
is the length distance induced by
C(Y)
on
C_{\infty}
.
By
[
34
, Theorem 1.2]
(after
[
30
]
),
\mathscr{H}^{n-1}(Y)
is bounded from below by a constant depending on
n
and
v
, so that
\#\pi_{1}(C_{\infty})<C(n,v)
by Lemma
4.1
.
Note that here we are using the assumption that
n\geq 3
. In the remainder of the proof, we denote by
C(n,v)
a generic constant depending on
n
and
v
only, without renaming it at every step.
Since
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}/G_{i}\cong C_{r_{i}}
, by Theorem
2.3
, we have that

(4.58)

(Z,\mathsf{d}_{z})/G\cong(C_{\infty},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty})\,.

We now claim that
\#G\leq C(n,v)
. We divide the proof of this fact in steps.

Step 1
: We claim that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}:=\{g\in G:g\cdot x\in B_{\varepsilon}(x)\text{ for some }x\in Z\}
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
, where
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon^{\prime}/10
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}(n,v)
is given by Lemma
4.1
.

Assume that there are
N>0
distinct elements
\{g_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\subset\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
and (possibly repeated) points
x_{k}\in Z
such that
g_{k}\cdot x_{k}\in B_{\varepsilon}(x_{k})
.
For every
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, choose points
x_{i,k}\in\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i}
converging to
x_{k}
, and isometries
g_{i,k}\in G_{i}
converging to
g_{k}
. If
k\neq k^{\prime}
, since
g_{k}\neq g_{k^{\prime}}
, we have that
g_{i,k}\neq g_{i,k^{\prime}}
for
i
large enough.

Consider the correspondence between deck transformations of
\widetilde{A}_{i}
and
\pi_{1}(A_{i})
. Each
g_{i,k}
corresponds to a loop
\gamma_{i,k}
of
\pi_{1}(A_{i})
centered in
p_{i}(x_{i,k})\in A_{i}
obtained by projecting a curve of
\widetilde{A}_{i}
from
x_{i,k}
to
g_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k}
. In particular, such curve can be chosen to satisfy

(4.59)

\mathrm{length}^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}}(\gamma_{i,k})\leq 2\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(x_{i,k},g_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k})\,.

Since
x_{i,k}\to x_{k}
and
g_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k}\to g_{k}\cdot x_{k}\in B^{\mathsf{d}_{z}}_{\varepsilon}(x_{k})
as

(\widetilde{A}^{\prime}_{i},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})\to(Z,\mathsf{d}_{z})
, it follows that

(4.60)

\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\,\mathrm{length}^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i}}(\gamma_{i,k})\leq 2\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(x_{i,k},g_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k})}{r_{i}}\leq 2\varepsilon\,.

Observe now that
C_{\infty}\cong Z/G
is
C(n)
-doubling and has diameter bounded above by a possibly different
C(n)
. As a consequence, we deduce that there exists
T(N,n)>0
(with the property that
T(N,n)\uparrow+\infty
as
N\uparrow+\infty
) such that, if
C_{\infty}
contains a collection of points
\{c_{l}\}_{l=1}^{N}
, then there are at least
T
points of such collection contained in a ball
B^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}}_{\varepsilon/2}(c)
.
Using this principle with the collection
\{G\cdot x_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\subset C_{\infty}
, we find
T
different indexes
k_{1},\dots,k_{T}
such that
\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty}(G\cdot x_{k_{j}},G\cdot x_{\infty})<\varepsilon/2
for all
j=1,\dots,T
and some
G\cdot x_{\infty}\in Z/G
.
By Theorem
2.3
, the projections
G_{i}\cdot x_{i,k_{j}}\in(C_{r_{i}},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})
converge to
G\cdot x_{k_{j}}
in the space realizing the convergence
(C_{r_{i}},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})\to(C_{\infty},\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{\infty})
, for all
j=1,\dots,T
.
Moreover, in the same space, there are points
a_{i}\in C_{r_{i}}
such that
a_{i}\to G\cdot x_{\infty}
.
Hence, for
i
large enough

(4.61)

G_{i}\cdot x_{i,k_{j}}\in B^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i}}_{\varepsilon}(a_{i})\subset C_{r_{i}}

for all
j=1,\dots,T
.

Observe that for
\varepsilon
small enough we have

B^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i}}_{\varepsilon}(a_{i})=B^{\mathsf{d}/r_{i}}_{\varepsilon}(a_{i})
. Moreover, for every
k
and every
i
, it holds that
G_{i}\cdot x_{i,k}\in C_{r_{i}}
is identified with
p_{i}(x_{i,k})\in A_{i}
via the inclusion
C_{r_{i}}\to A_{i}
.
Hence, combining with (
4.60
), the balls

B^{\mathsf{d}}_{3r_{i}\varepsilon}(a_{i})\subset X
contain
T
loops
\gamma_{i,k}
corresponding to different elements of
\pi_{1}(A_{i})
.
By Lemma
4.1
, it follows that
T\leq C(n,v)
, so that
N\leq C(n,v)
as well.

We record two important consequences of Step 1:

i)

the set
\mathrm{Fix}(G):=\{g\in G:g\cdot x=x\text{ for some }x\in Z\}
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
;

ii)

for every
z\in Z
, there is
\varepsilon>0
such that the almost stabilizer
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(z):=\{g\in G:g\cdot z\in B_{\varepsilon}(z)\}
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
.

Step 2
: We claim that
H:=\langle\mathrm{Fix}(G)\rangle
is normal and has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
.

Let
h\in\mathrm{Fix}(G)
be such that
h\cdot z=z
for some
z\in Z
, and let
g\in G
. Then
g^{-1}hg
fixes
g^{-1}(z)
, so that
g^{-1}hg\in\mathrm{Fix}(G)
.
Let now
h\in H
, so that
h=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}
for some
\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}\subset\mathrm{Fix}(G)
. For every
g\in G
, we have that

(4.62)

g^{-1}hg=g^{-1}g_{1}g\,g^{-1}g_{2}\cdots g_{k-1}g\,g^{-1}g_{k}g\,.

Since
g^{-1}g_{j}g
belongs to
\mathrm{Fix}(G)
for every
j
, it follows that
g^{-1}hg\in H
, proving that
H
is normal.

To prove that
H
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
, we first claim that
H/Z(H)
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
, where
Z(H)<H
is the center of
H
.
Let
T:H\to\mathrm{Sym}(\mathrm{Fix}(G))
be the homomorphism defined so that
T(h)
is the permutation of
\mathrm{Fix}(G)
given by
T(h)(g):=h^{-1}gh
. By Step 1,
H/\mathrm{Ker}(T)
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
. Since
\mathrm{Ker}(T)
is in the center of
H
it follows that
H/Z(H)
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
, as claimed.

By Schur’s Theorem, the derived group
H^{\prime}\leq H
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
. Therefore it suffices to show that
H/H^{\prime}
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
as well. The group
H/H^{\prime}
is abelian, and it is generated by at most
C(n,v)
elements of order at most
C(n,v)
(i.e. the classes of the elements in
\mathrm{Fix}(G)
). Hence,
H/H^{\prime}
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
, concluding the proof of Step 2.

Step 3:
We claim that
G/H
acts properly discontinuously on
Z/H
.

We first show that
G/H
acts freely. Indeed if
(g+H)\cdot x=H\cdot x
for some
H\cdot x\in Z/H
and some
g+H\in G/H
then there exists
h\in H
such that
(g+h)\cdot x=x
, so that
g+h\in H
and
g+H=H
. Hence, the action is free.

To conclude, it suffices to show that if
H\cdot x\in Z/H
and
g_{i}+H\in G/H
are such that
(g_{i}+H)\cdot x\to H\cdot x
, then
g_{i}+H=H
for
i
large enough. To this aim, observe that there are elements
h_{i}\in H
such that
(g_{i}+h_{i})\cdot x\to x
. By Step 1, it follows that
g_{i}+h_{i}
fixes
x
for
i
large enough, so that
g_{i}\in H
because
G/H
acts freely.

To complete the proof that
\#G\leq C(n,v)
, note that since
G/H
acts properly discontinuously on
Z/H
by Step 3,
G/H
is a subgroup of
\pi_{1}(C_{\infty})
. Thus it has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
. Combining with Step 2, we deduce that
G
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
as well.

We now conclude the proof of the theorem. Since
Z/G
is compact, we deduce that
Z
is compact.
Let
a_{i}\in\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\prime}
be elements such that
a_{i}\to z\in Z
as
(\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\prime},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}/r_{i})\to(Z,\mathsf{d}_{z})
.
Let
\varepsilon(n,v)>0
be given by Lemma
4.1
, so that the almost stabilizers
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,a_{i}}:=\{g\in G_{i}:\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(a_{i},g\cdot a_{i})<\varepsilon r_{i}\}
all have cardinality at most
C(n,v)
. Since we are assuming by contradiction that
\#G_{i}\geq L
, the index of
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,a_{i}}
in
G_{i}
is at least
S:=L/C(n,v)
. Consider elements
\{g^{j}_{i}\}_{j=1}^{S}\subset G_{i}
belonging to different left cosets of
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,a_{i}}
in
G_{i}
. Since
Z
is compact, for each
j\in\{1,\cdots S\}
, there exists
g^{j}\in G
such that
g^{j}_{i}\to g^{j}
.

We claim that the elements
\{g^{j}\}_{j=1}^{S}\subset G
are all distinct. If this is the case, we conclude that

(4.63)

L/C(n,v)=S\leq\#G\leq C(n,v)\,,

contradicting a suitably large choice of
L
.
We show that
g^{1}\neq g^{2}
, the other cases being analogous. If
g^{1}=g^{2}
, then
\mathsf{d}_{z}(g^{1}\cdot z,g^{2}\cdot z)=0
, so that

(4.64)

\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}_{i}(g^{1}_{i}\cdot a_{i},g^{2}_{i}\cdot a_{i})<\varepsilon r_{i}

if
i
is large enough. This implies that
g^{1}_{i}\in g^{2}_{i}+\Gamma_{\varepsilon,a_{i}}
, contradicting that the elements
g^{j}_{i}
belong to different left cosets of
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,a_{i}}
in
G_{i}
.
∎

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
4.1
: non-Euclidean volume growth case

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4.1
under the assumption that the space
X
does not have maximal volume growth. We observe that if
X
is compact, then
\pi_{1}(X)
is virtually abelian and thus amenable by
[
72
, Theorem 1.3]
, so that
\|X\|=0
by
[
46
, Corollary C, page 248]
.
In the following, we will assume that
X
is non-compact.
To prove the vanishing theorem in this case, thanks to Theorem
4.6
, it is sufficient to prove the following:

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n})
be a non-compact
\RCD(0,n)
space such that

(4.65)

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{R}(p))}{R^{n}}=0\,,

for some
p\in X
.
Then
X
admits a cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
which is amenable, open, amenable at infinity and has
\mathrm{mult}\big(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\leq n
.

As said before, Theorem
4.14
generalizes some results due to Gromov in
[
46
]
, where he assumed a uniform small upper bound on the volume of unit balls.
The rough idea behind the proof of Theorem
4.14
is the following. In view of (
4.65
),
[
34
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that that all the blow-downs of
X
have Hausdorff dimension
\leq n-1
. In particular, they have topological dimension at most
n-1
and thus they admit arbitrarily small open covers with multiplicity at most
n
. Ideally, one would like to combine the existence of such covers with small multiplicity with the Margulis lemma from
[
55
]
(and the subsequent partial generalization to
\RCD
spaces in
[
37
]
) to construct the sought cover of
X
.
There are several difficulties when trying to implement this strategy. The most severe ones are that:

i)

in general, it is not clear whether the pointed GH-approximations from the (rescaled)-space to its blow-downs can be chosen to be continuous, due to the possibly poor topological behaviour of blow-downs in the collapsed case;

ii)

the blow-down of
X
need not be unique and need not be invariant under rescaling. Thus it is hard to turn the local information obtained via a blow-down argument into a global one;

iii)

a local version of the Margulis lemma analogous to the one obtained in
[
55
]
in the smooth case is not available yet for
\RCD
spaces, although a more global Margulis lemma was recently obtained in such context in
[
55
]
.

The key tool to circumvent the difficulties in i) and ii) will be a recent result by Papasoglu stated below as Theorem
4.16
.
Using Theorem
4.16
we show in Proposition
4.2

that a metric space with volume growth of polynomial degree less than
n
, after a suitable conformal rescaling of the metric, has small
(n-1)
-Urysohn width (see Definition
4.2
) outside of sufficiently large compact sets.
To complete the proof of Theorem
4.14
we are going to combine such control on the
(n-1)
-width with Theorem
A.1
, where we obtain a weaker version of the Margulis lemma from
[
55
]
with no quantitative bound on the index of the nilpotent subgroup.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that a metric space
(X,\mathsf{d})
has
q
-Uryson width less than or equal to
W
, and write
UW_{q}(X,\mathsf{d})\leq W
if for every
\varepsilon>0
there exist a
q
-dimensional simplicial complex
Y
and a continuous map
\pi:X\to Y
such that every fiber
\pi^{-1}(y)
,
y\in Y
, has diameter
\leq W+\varepsilon
.

In the following, we will denote by
\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{n}
the
n
-dimensional Hausdorff content for
n\geq 0
. The next statement corresponds to
[
79
, Theorem 3.3]
.

Theorem 4.16
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
\varepsilon_{n}>0
with the following property. Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a proper metric space and
R>0
be such that for every
x\in X
there is an open set
U_{x}
with the properties:

(i)

B_{R}(x)\subset U_{x}\subset B_{10R}(x)

(ii)

\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{n-1}(\partial U_{x})\leq\varepsilon_{n}R^{n-1}
.

Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there exist a
locally finite

(n-1)
-dimensional simplicial complex
Y
and a
proper
continuous map
\pi:X\to Y
such that every fiber
\pi^{-1}(y)
,
y\in Y
, has diameter
\leq R+\varepsilon
. In particular,

UW_{n-1}(X)\leq R
.

The statement of
[
79
, Theorem 3.3]
does not mention that, in the previous theorem, the simplicial complex
Y
and the map
\pi
can be taken to be locally finite and proper, respectively. However, this is a consequence of the proof in
[
79
]
.

The following Coarea Inequality follows from
[
38
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Proposition 4.17

(Coarea inequality)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a proper metric space and
n>1
. Then for every
x\in X
and
0<\alpha<\beta<\infty
we have that

(4.66)

\int^{\beta}_{\alpha}\mathscr{H}^{n-1}(\partial B_{s}(x))ds\leq C_{n}\,\big(\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{\beta}(x))-\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{\alpha}(x))\big)\,,

where
C_{n}
is a dimensional constant.

To prove Theorem
4.14
the key idea is to apply Theorem
4.16
to a conformal rescaling of
(X,\mathsf{d})
. Lemma
4.2
below introduces the relevant conformal rescaling and establishes its basic properties. The forthcoming Proposition
4.2
establishes a the sought estimate for the Urysohn width of the conformal rescaling away from a bounded set.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},p)
be a complete, separable and geodesic pointed metric space. Given any curve
\gamma\in AC([0,1],(X,\mathsf{d}))
we set

(4.67)

\widetilde{L}(\gamma):=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1}dt\,,

where
|\dot{\gamma}|
denotes the metric derivative. Accordingly, we define the function
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}:X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
as

(4.68)

\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(x,y):=\inf\{\widetilde{L}(\gamma)\,:\,\gamma\in AC([0,1],(X,\mathsf{d})),\gamma(0)=x,\,\gamma(1)=y\}\,.

Then
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
is a distance on
X
. Moreover, there exists
0<\delta_{0}\leq\frac{1}{6}
such that for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and every
q\in X
with
\mathsf{d}(p,q)>10
, we have that

(4.69)

\widetilde{B}_{\delta}(q)\subset B_{2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\subset B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\subset\widetilde{B}_{6\delta}(q)\,,

where we denote by
\widetilde{B}
balls with respect to the distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
is symmetric, finite, and satisfies the triangle inequality. Thus, to prove that
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
is a distance we need only to check positivity. To this aim, consider
a,b\in X
with
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(a,b)=0
and assume by contradiction that
\delta:=\mathsf{d}(a,b)>0
. Let
\gamma\in AC([0,1],(X,\mathsf{d}))
be any curve connecting
a
and
b
and call
t_{0}\in[0,1]
the first intersection time of
\gamma
with
\partial B_{\delta/2}(a)
. Then we can estimate

(4.70)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1}dt\geq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1}dt

(4.71)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,a)+\delta/2+1}dt

(4.72)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\delta/2}{\mathsf{d}(p,a)+\delta/2+1}>0\,.

Since
\gamma
was arbitrary, we obtain the desired contradiction.

Until the end of the proof we fix
\delta_{0}\leq 1/6
with the following property: for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
, if
x>0
is such that
\log(1+x)\leq\frac{3}{2}\delta
, then
x\leq 2\delta
.

We start by proving the third inclusion in (
4.69
). Take
q\in X
with
\mathsf{d}(p,q)>10
, any
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and consider any
a\in B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)
. Let
\gamma\in AC([0,1],(X,\mathsf{d}))
be a constant speed geodesic (with respect to
\mathsf{d}
), joining
a
to
q
. In particular, we have that

(4.73)

|\dot{\gamma}|\equiv\mathsf{d}(a,q)\leq 3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)\,,\quad\gamma([0,1])\subset B_{\mathsf{d}(p,q)/2}(q)\subset X\setminus B_{\mathsf{d}(p,q)/2}(p)\,.

Thus, for every
t\in[0,1]
we have that
\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(p,q)
and therefore

(4.74)

\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(a,q)\leq\widetilde{L}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1}dt\leq\int_{0}^{1}\frac{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(p,q)}dt=6\delta\,.

Since
a
was arbitrary, we deduce that
B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\subset\widetilde{B}_{6\delta}(q)
.

For the first inclusion in (
4.69
), take any
b\in\widetilde{B}_{\delta}(q)
and consider a curve
\gamma\in AC([0,1],(X,\mathsf{d}))
joining
q
to
b
such that
\widetilde{L}(\gamma)\leq\frac{3}{2}\delta
. Since
\widetilde{L}
is invariant by reparametrisation, we can assume that
\gamma
has constant speed with respect to the distance
\mathsf{d}
, i.e.
|\dot{\gamma}|\equiv L
for some
L>0
. We claim that

(4.75)

\gamma([0,1])\subset X\setminus B_{2}(p)\,.

Arguing by contradiction, let
t_{1}\in[0,1]
be the first intersection time of
\gamma
with
\partial B_{2}(p)
and let
t_{0}\in[0,1]
be defined as

(4.76)

t_{0}:=\sup\{t<t_{1}\,:\,\gamma(t)\not\in B_{4}(p)\}\,.

With such choices we can estimate

(4.77)

\frac{3}{2}\delta\geq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\vee 1}dt=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))}dt\geq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{4}dt\geq\frac{1}{2}\,,

thus yielding the desired contradiction, since
\delta\leq\delta_{0}\leq 1/6
.
On the other hand, we have that

(4.78)

\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))\leq\mathsf{d}(p,q)+\mathsf{d}(q,\gamma(t))\leq\mathsf{d}(p,q)+Lt\,.

Thus, taking into account (
4.75
), we deduce that

(4.79)

\frac{3}{2}\delta\geq\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\dot{\gamma}|(t)}{\mathsf{d}(p,\gamma(t))}dt\geq\int_{0}^{1}\frac{L}{\mathsf{d}(p,q)+Lt}dt=\log\bigg(1+\frac{L}{\mathsf{d}(p,q)}\bigg)\,.

Then, our choice of
\delta_{0}
implies that
L/\mathsf{d}(p,q)\leq 2\delta
. We can conclude by observing that
\mathsf{d}(q,b)\leq L\leq 2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)
. Since
b\in\widetilde{B}_{\delta}(q)
was arbitrary, we proved that
\widetilde{B}_{\delta}(q)\subset B_{2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)
. ∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(X,\mathsf{d},p)
be a complete, separable, proper, non-compact and geodesic pointed metric space. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
and
\delta_{0}
be the distance and the parameter given by Lemma
4.2
. Assume that

(4.80)

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{R}(p))}{R^{n}}=0\,.

Then, for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
there exists
T(\delta)>20
such that the following holds. There exist a locally finite
(n-1)
-dimensional simplicial complex
Y
and a proper continuous map
\pi:(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p),\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\to Y
with fibers of diameter less than
\delta
. In particular,

(4.81)

UW_{n-1}(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p),\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\leq\delta\,.

Proof.

Our goal is to show that
(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p),\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.16
.

Fix any
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and take any
q\in X
such that
\mathsf{d}(p,q)>20
. Given any
\eta\in[0,\delta]
, observe that for every
q^{\prime}\in\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)
we have

(4.82)

\mathsf{d}(p,q^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{d}(p,q)-(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(p,q)\,.

Suppose now that
A\subset\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)
has
{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(A)=\rho\ll\delta_{0}
, where
{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}
denotes the diameter computed with respect to the distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
. In particular, there exist
a,b\in A
such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(a,b)>\frac{\rho}{2}
, thus
b\not\in\widetilde{B}_{\rho/2}(a)
. Taking into account (
4.82
) (which also ensures that
\mathsf{d}(p,a)>10
) and applying Lemma
4.2
, we obtain

(4.83)

B_{\rho\mathsf{d}(p,q)/8}(a)\subset B_{\rho\mathsf{d}(p,a)/4}(a)\subset\widetilde{B}_{\rho/2}(a)\,.

Thus,
b\not\in B_{\rho\mathsf{d}(p,q)/8}(a)
and, denoting by
{\rm diam}_{\mathsf{d}}
the diameter computed with respect to the distance
\mathsf{d}
, we conclude that

(4.84)

{\rm diam}_{\mathsf{d}}(A)\geq\frac{\mathsf{d}(p,q)}{8}\rho=\frac{\mathsf{d}(p,q)}{8}\,{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(A)\,.

In the following, for every
d,\zeta>0
, we denote by
\mathscr{H}_{\zeta}^{d}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\zeta}^{d}
the
\zeta
-approximate
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measures with respect to
\mathsf{d}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
, respectively. Accordingly, we denote by
\mathscr{H}^{d}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{d}
the respective
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measures.
Thanks to (
4.84
), for every
\rho\ll\delta_{0}
we obtain that

(4.85)

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\rho}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)\\
=\inf&\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(A_{i})^{n-1}\,:\,\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{i}=\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q),\,{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(A_{i})\leq\rho\right\}\\
\leq\inf&\Bigg\{\frac{8^{n-1}}{\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{n-1}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\rm diam}_{\mathsf{d}}(A_{i})^{n-1}\,:\,\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{i}=\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\,,\,\\
&{\rm diam}_{\mathsf{d}}(A_{i})\leq\rho\,\frac{\mathsf{d}(p,q)}{8}\Bigg\}=\frac{8^{n-1}}{\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{n-1}}\,\,\mathscr{H}_{\rho\,\frac{\mathsf{d}(p,q)}{8}}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)\,.\end{split}

Taking the limit as
\rho\to 0
, we conclude that for every
q\in X
with
\mathsf{d}(p,q)>20
and every
\eta\in[0,\delta]
there holds

(4.86)

\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)\leq\frac{c}{\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{n-1}}\,\,\mathscr{H}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta)\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)\,,

where, for notational simplicity, we put
c=8^{n-1}
.

Applying Proposition
4.2
with
x=q
,
\alpha=2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)
and
\beta=3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)
we obtain

(4.87)

\fint^{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}_{2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}\mathscr{H}^{n-1}(\partial B_{s}(q))ds\leq C_{n}\,\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))-\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))}{\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}\,.

Thus there exists
\eta(q)\in(0,\delta)
such that

(4.88)

\mathscr{H}^{n-1}(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))\leq C_{n}\,\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))-\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{2\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))}{\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}\,.

The combination of (
4.86
) and (
4.88
) yields

(4.89)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)

\displaystyle\leq c\,C_{n}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{3\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q))}{\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{n}}

(4.90)

\displaystyle\leq c\,C_{n}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{2\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(p))}{\delta\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{n}}\,.

Thanks to (
4.80
) there exists
T(\delta)>20
such that

(4.91)

\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)\leq\varepsilon_{n}\delta^{n-1}\,,\qquad\forall q\in X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p)\,,

where
\varepsilon_{N}
is the constant given by Theorem
4.16
.
Finally, we deduce that

(4.92)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\infty}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\cap(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p))\big)\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\infty}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)

(4.93)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n-1}\big(\partial B_{(2\delta+\eta(q))\mathsf{d}(p,q)}(q)\big)

(4.94)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}\delta^{n-1}\,.

Thanks to Lemma
4.2
we can apply Theorem
4.16
to the metric space
(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p),\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
and conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.14
.

We fix
\delta<\min\{\delta_{0}/2,\varepsilon/8\}
, where
\delta_{0}
is the parameter identified in Lemma
4.2
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)
is the constant appearing in Corollary
A
.

By Proposition
4.2
there exist a locally finite
(n-1)
-dimensional simplicial complex
Y
and a proper continuous map
\pi:X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p)\to Y
such that every fiber
\pi^{-1}(y)
,
y\in Y
, has diameter
\leq\delta
.
Thus, every
y\in Y
has an precompact open neighborhood
V_{y}
such that
{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\pi^{-1}(V_{y}))\leq 2\delta
. Indeed, if this is not the case, then we can find
y\in Y
and a sequence of decreasing open neighborhoods
\{V_{y}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
y
such that

(4.95)

{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\pi^{-1}(V_{y}^{i}))>2\delta\,,\quad\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\,\overline{V}_{y}^{i}=\{y\}\,.

Thus, for every
i
we can find
x_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(V_{y}^{i})
such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(x_{i},\pi^{-1}(y))>\frac{\delta}{2}
. Since
\pi
is proper, the sequence
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is contained in a compact set and thus (up to subsequences) it converge to a limit
\overline{x}\in X
.
By passing to the limit in the previous conditions, on the one hand we have
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\overline{x},\pi^{-1}(y))\geq\frac{\delta}{2}
. On the other hand, we have that

(4.96)

\overline{x}\in\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\,\pi^{-1}(\overline{V}^{i}_{y})=\pi^{-1}(y)\,,

resulting in a contradiction.

Consider the covering
\{V_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
of
Y
. Since
Y
is proper, because it is a locally finite simplicial complex, and has topological dimension
n-1
, there exists a
locally finite
refinement
\{\widehat{V}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\{V_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
with multiplicity at most
n
. After discarding all sets
\widehat{V}_{\alpha}
such that
\widehat{V}_{\alpha}\cap\pi(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p))=\emptyset
, we obtain a locally finite covering of
\pi(X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p))
, still denoted as
\{\widehat{V}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
, with multiplicity at most
n
.
For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
we define
\widehat{U}_{\alpha}:=\pi^{-1}(\widehat{V}_{\alpha})
. Observe that
\{\widehat{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
is a locally finite open cover of
X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p)
with
\mathrm{mult}\big(\{\widehat{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\leq n
. Set

(4.97)

A:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}\,:\,\widehat{U}_{\alpha}\cap B_{2T(\delta)}(p)=\emptyset\}\,.

Consider an enumeration
m:\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\to A

and define the open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
X
as

(4.98)

U_{0}:=B_{2T(\delta)}(p)\cup\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}\setminus A}\widehat{U}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad U_{i}=\widehat{U}_{m(i)},\quad i\in\mathbb{N}\,.

By construction,
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a locally finite open cover of
X
such that
\mathrm{mult}\big(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\leq n
.
Moreover, Corollary
A
guarantees that for every bounded (open) set
U\subset X
, and
u\in U
, the image of the natural map induced by inclusion
i_{*}:\pi_{1}(U,u)\to\pi_{1}(X,u)
is a virtually nilpotent and thus amenable subgroup.
Therefore, the cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is amenable. For the sake of clarity we note that the main result of
[
37
]
would be sufficient to check the last claim.

For the application of Theorem
4.6
,
we are left to prove that
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is amenable at infinity. For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
the set
\widehat{V}_{\alpha}
is contained in
V_{y(\alpha)}
for some
y(\alpha)\in Y
. Thus
{\rm diam}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(U_{i})\leq 2\delta
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, taking any
p_{i}\in U_{i}\subset X\setminus B_{T(\delta)}(p)
, Lemma
4.2
yields that

(4.99)

U_{i}\subset\widetilde{B}_{2\delta}(p_{i})\subset B_{4\delta\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})}(p_{i})\,,

where we used that
\delta\leq\delta_{0}/2
. We introduce sequence
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
by setting

(4.100)

W_{0}:=X\,,\qquad W_{i}:=X\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})}(p)\,,\quad i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\,.

We claim that conditions (i)-(iv) in Definition
4
holds. Condition (i) is trivial since
X
is proper, and condition (iii) follows from (
4.99
). Moreover, since
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is locally finite, we deduce that
\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
and therefore (ii) holds. To check (iv), we apply Corollary
A
to obtain that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
the image of the inclusion map

(4.101)

\pi_{1}\big(B_{4\delta\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})}(p_{i}),p_{i}\big)\to\pi_{1}\big(B_{4\delta\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})/\varepsilon}(p_{i}),p_{i}\big)

is virtually nilpotent. Our choice of
\delta
guarantees that
B_{4\delta\mathsf{d}(p,p_{i})/\varepsilon}(p_{i})\subset W_{i}
. Thus
U_{i}
is amenable in
W_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
. By Theorem
4.6
we conclude that
||X||=0
.
∎

5.
Classification of
\RCD(0,3)
spaces with empty boundary

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.10
.
Theorem
1.11
then follows by combining Theorem
1.10
with the results from
[
14
,
36
]
as shown below.

Proof of Theorem
1.11
assuming Theorem
1.10
.

If
X
is a non-compact topological manifold, the result follows by Theorem
1.10
. If
X
is a compact topological manifold, we consider two separate cases. If
\pi_{1}(X)
is infinite, then the universal covering
\widetilde{X}
of
X
splits off a line by
[
72
, Theorem 1.3]
(combined with
[
89
]
), so that
\widetilde{X}
is an Alexandrov space of non-negative sectional curvature by
[
67
]
. Being a quotient of an Alexandrov space of non-negative sectional curvature by a discrete group, also
X
is then an Alexandrov space of non-negative sectional curvature by, for instance,
[
1
, Corollary 8.7]
. If
\pi_{1}(X)
is finite, then
X
is a spherical manifold by Perelman’s solution of the Geometrization Conjecture.

Hence, we assume that
X
is not a topological manifold. By
[
19
, Theorem 1.8]
, at least one point of
X
has all the tangent cones homeomorphic to
C(\mathbb{RP}^{2})
. By
[
36
, Theorem 1.1 point 3]
,
X
is non-orientable in the sense of
[
14
, Definition 1.6]
. Hence, by
[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
,
X
admits a ramified double cover
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
, equipped with an involutive isometry
\Gamma:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}
such that
\widetilde{X}/\langle\Gamma\rangle\cong X
isometrically. By
[
36
, Theorem 1.1 and Lemma 4.8]
,
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{3})
is a
\RCD(0,3)
space without boundary and a topological manifold, and the set of fixed points of
\Gamma
is locally finite and non-empty.

Assume now that
X
is compact. In this case,
\widetilde{X}
is compact as well. By the previous part of the proof, either
\widetilde{X}
is an Alexandrov space of non-negative sectional curvature, or it has finite fundamental group. In the former case,
X
is an Alexandrov space with non-negative sectional curvature as well. Hence, we assume that
\widetilde{X}
has finite fundamental group. In this case,
X
is a spherical suspension over
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
by the same proof of
[
36
, Corollary 4.16]
(they only use the assumption
K>0
to obtain that
\widetilde{X}
has finite fundamental group).

Assume now that
X
is non-compact. In this case,
\widetilde{X}
is non-compact as well. By Theorem
1.10
,
\widetilde{X}
is either an Alexandrov space with non-negative sectional curvature, or it is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In the former case,
X
has non-negative sectional curvature as well, so that we may assume that
\widetilde{X}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Consider the compactification at infinity of
\widetilde{X}
, which, using the obvious identification, we denote
\mathbb{R}^{3}\cup\{\infty\}
. Consider the homeomorphism
\Gamma_{\infty}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\cup\{\infty\}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\cup\{\infty\}
induced by
\Gamma:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}
, and observe that
\Gamma_{\infty}(\infty)=\infty
. By
[
84
, Theorem 4]
,
\Gamma_{\infty}
has exactly two fixed points, so that
(\mathbb{R}^{3}\cup\{\infty\})/\langle\Gamma_{\infty}\rangle
is homeomorphic to a spherical suspension over
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
by
[
50
, Theorem 1.1]
. Hence,
X\cong\widetilde{X}/\langle\Gamma\rangle
is homeomorphic to
C(\mathbb{RP}^{2})
, concluding the proof.
∎

We turn to the proof of Theorem
1.10
.
The starting point, similarly to
[
81
,
63
]
, is the following:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{3})
be an
\RCD(0,3)
space which is a topological manifold. Then
\pi_{2}(X)=\{0\}
unless the universal cover of
X
splits isometrically as
\mathbb{R}\times Z
where
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature homeomorphic to
S^{2}
.

The proof follows from the verbatim arguments as in the smooth case discussed in
[
81
, Lemma 2]
. We discuss the details for the sake of completeness.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Assume that
\pi_{2}(X)\neq 0
. Denoting by
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{3})
the universal cover of
X
endowed with its natural
\RCD(0,3)
structure (see
[
72
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
89
]
),
\pi_{2}(\overline{X})\neq 0
as well, and
\overline{X}
is simply connected.
By Hurewicz’s theorem
H_{2}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z})\equiv\pi_{2}(\overline{X})
. Thus
H_{2}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z})\neq 0
and in particular
\overline{X}
must be noncompact, otherwise by Poincaré duality and the universal coefficients theorem we would have
H_{2}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z})\equiv H_{1}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z})=0
, since
\overline{X}
is simply connected.
By the sphere theorem
[
78
]
there exists an embedded
S^{2}\subset\overline{X}
which is homotopically nontrivial.
We claim that
\overline{X}\setminus S^{2}
has at least two connected components. If not, then there exists a loop in
\overline{X}
intersecting the
S^{2}
transversally exactly once (note that we can endow the
3
-manifold
X
with the unique smooth structure compatible with its topology). Thus its linking number with such
S^{2}
would be
\pm 1
, a contradiction since the linking number is homotopically invariant and
\overline{X}
is simply connected. Next we claim that no connected component of
\overline{X}\setminus S^{2}
can be compact. Indeed, if one of them is compact, then the
S^{2}
would bound a compact submanifold of
\overline{X}
. Therefore it would be homologically, and hence homotopically, trivial, a contradiction. Since
\overline{X}\setminus S^{2}
has (at least) two connected components,
\overline{X}
has two ends. Thus it splits a line, by
[
43
]
. More precisely,
\overline{X}=\mathbb{R}\times Z
as metric measure spaces, where
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z},\mathscr{H}^{2})
is a compact
\RCD(0,2)
space. By
[
67
, Theorem 1.1]
,
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature. Since we are assuming that
X
is a topological manifold (with empty boundary),
Z
must be a topological surface (with empty boundary) as well. Since
\overline{X}
is simply connected,
Z
is simply connected as well. Thus
Z
is homeomorphic to
S^{2}
, as we claimed.
∎

We now distinguish two cases, the case when
X
is simply connected, and the case when it is not. In the first case, the universal cover must split a line. We remark, that although the statement is completely analogous to the smooth, our proof departs from
[
63
]
in the last step, where we rely on Theorem
3.1
instead of minimal surfaces methods.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{3})
be a noncompact
\RCD(0,3)
space such that
X
is a topological
3
-manifold. If
X
is not simply connected, then the universal cover
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}})
splits a line and
(X,\mathsf{d})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature.

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
5
, either
\pi_{2}(\overline{X})=0
, or
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature, since it is a product of
\mathbb{R}
with an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature. In the second case,
(X,\mathsf{d})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature as well. Thus we can assume that
\pi_{2}(\overline{X})=0
. Under such assumption
\overline{X}
is contractible. Indeed, since
X
is a noncompact
3
-manifold,
H_{3}(\overline{X},\mathbb{Z})=0
. Since
\pi_{1}(\overline{X})=\pi_{2}(\overline{X})=0
, by Hurewicz’s theorem,
\pi_{3}(\overline{X})=0
as well. Hence by Whitehead’s theorem
\overline{X}
is homotopically equivalent to a point, i.e., it is contractible. By Smith theory,
\pi_{1}(X)
must be torsion-free. Therefore, if
X
is not simply connected, then
\pi_{1}(X)
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. By Theorem
3.1
,
\overline{X}
splits a line, i.e., it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\times Z
where
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z},\mathscr{H}^{2})
is an
\RCD(0,2)
space. By
[
67
, Theorem 1.1]
,
(Z,\mathsf{d}_{Z})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature. Therefore
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}})
and
(X,\mathsf{d})
are Alexandrov spaces with nonnegative curvature as well.
∎

We are ready to complete the proof of Theorem
1.10
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

If
\pi_{2}(X)\neq 0
or
\pi_{1}(X)\neq 0
then by Lemma
5
and Lemma
5
respectively
(X,\mathsf{d})
is an Alexandrov space with nonnegative curvature whose universal cover splits a line isometrically. The classification of
3
-dimensional noncompact Alexandrov spaces with nonnegative curvature obtained in
[
83
, Part 2]
implies that any such space admits a smooth complete metric with nonnegative sectional curvature inducing the same topology. Thus we can assume that
\pi_{1}(X)=\pi_{2}(X)=0
. By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
5
above,
X
is contractible under these assumptions. Since any
3
-manifold is triangulable, by Theorem
4.1
we have
\|X\|=0
. By
[
9
, Theorem 1]
,
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

It follows from the proof of Theorem
1.10
that the following holds: if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{3})
is an
\RCD(0,3)
space homeomorphic to a
3
-manifold and with Euclidean volume growth, i.e.,

(5.1)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{3}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{3}}>0\,

for some (and thus all)
p\in X
, then
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Indeed,
\pi_{2}(X)=0
by Lemma
5
and the Euclidean volume growth assumption. Thus the universal cover
(\overline{X},\overline{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{H}^{3})
is contractible and therefore
\pi_{1}(X)
must be torsion free as observed in the proof of Lemma
5
. On the other hand, any
\RCD(0,n)
space with Euclidean volume growth has finite
\pi_{1}
, as proved in
[
72
, Theorem 1.6]
(note that by
[
89
]
the revised fundamental group coincides with the usual fundamental group). Thus
\pi_{1}(X)=0
and the conclusion follows.

We remark that the same conclusion was obtained with a different method recently in
[
19
, Theorem 1.9]
.

6.
Further applications

In this section, we provide new topological restrictions for manifolds with non-negative Ricci curvature and
\RCD(0,n)
spaces. First, we exploit the vanishing Theorem
1.7
for the simplicial volume to obtain new restrictions on the topology of noncompact manifolds admitting a complete metric with
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
in dimension
4
.
Then, relying on Theorem
4.8
, we obtain restrictions for manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature (and
\RCD(0,n)
spaces) and maximal volume growth in any dimension. The two main results are Propositions
6
and
6
below.

Proposition 6.1
.

There exists an infinite family of noncompact, one-ended, simply connected
4
-manifolds which do not admit any complete metric with nonnegative Ricci curvature.

Proposition 6.2
.

For every
n\geq 4
, the manifold
S^{n-2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
admits no complete metric of non-negative Ricci curvature and Euclidean volume growth.

Remark 6.3
.

It is well-known that
S^{n-3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
admits a complete metric with nonnegative Ricci curvature and Euclidean volume growth for every
n\geq 5
. Such metrics can be constructed with a doubly warped product ansatz by viewing
S^{n-3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
as
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{2}\times S^{n-3}
away from a central
S^{n-3}
.

For the proof of Proposition
6
we will rely on the following:

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\overline{M}^{4}
be a smooth compact compact oriented manifold with boundary of dimension
4
. If
\|\overline{M}\setminus{\partial{\overline{M}}}\|=0
then all the connected components of
\partial{\overline{M}}
are graph manifolds.

Proof.

By
[
46
, Example, p. 17]
,
\|\partial{\overline{M}}\|=0
, see also
[
64
, Theorem 6.4]
and the discussion following it. Thus all connected components of the boundary of
\overline{M}
have vanishing simplicial volume. By Perelman’s resolution of the geometrization conjecture and
[
85
]
(see also
[
3
, Theorem 2.7]
) a closed orientable
3
-manifold with vanishing simplicial volume is a graph manifold.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6
.

Let
N
be any oriented closed hyperbolic
3
-manifold. By
[
57
, Theorem 2, Chapter VII]
, there exists a smooth compact connected and oriented
4
-manifold
\overline{M}
with boundary
\partial{\overline{M}}
diffeomorphic to
N
. By performing surgeries iteratively on a family of disjoint loops generating
\pi_{1}(\overline{M})
we can assume that
\overline{M}\setminus\partial{\overline{M}}
is simply connected. Moreover, it is clearly one-ended. We claim that any such manifold does not admit a complete Riemannian metric with
\Ric\geq 0
. Indeed,
N
is not a graph manifold, hence by Lemma
6
,
\|\overline{M}\setminus\partial{\overline{M}}\|>0
. The conclusion follows by Theorem
1.7
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

The very same argument, relying on the more general Theorem
4.1
, rules out also the existence of
\RCD(0,4)
structures on the class of manifolds considered in Proposition
6
.

Proposition
6
is a consequence of the following more general result and its subsequent corollary.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathscr{H}^{n},p)
be an
\RCD(0,n)
space with Euclidean volume growth

(6.1)

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{n}(B_{r}(p))}{r^{n}}\geq v>0.

Assume that there exists a compact set
K\subset X
such that
X\setminus K
is homeomorphic to
Y\times(0,+\infty)
for a path-connected topological space
Y
. Then, there exists
C=C(n,v)>0
such that
\pi_{1}(Y)
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
.

Proof.

Let
C(n,v)
be the constant given by
4.8
. Assume by contradiction that
\pi_{1}(Y)
has cardinality (strictly) bigger than
2C(n,v)
.
Let
x\in K
, and let
r>0
be large enough so that
K\subset B_{r}(x)
, and the conclusion of Theorem
4.8
holds. Let
C^{\prime}\subset X
be the interior of the path-connected component of
B_{5r}(x)\setminus B_{2r}(x)
containing
B_{4r}(x)\setminus B_{3r}(x)
, which exists by Theorem
4.8
. Let then
C\subset X
be the path-connected component of
C^{\prime}
containing
B_{4r}(x)\setminus B_{3r}(x)
. One can check that this exists by the same method used in Theorem
4.8
. In particular,
C
is an open path-connected set containing
B_{4r}(x)\setminus B_{3r}(x)
.
Set

(6.2)

A:=\big(C\cup B_{4r}(x)\big)\setminus K\,,\quad B:=C\cup\big(X\setminus\overline{B}_{3r}(x)\big)\,.

Observe that since
Y
is path connected these sets are path-connected. In addition, their intersection
A\cap B
is equal to the path-connected set
C
. Moreover,
A\cup B=X\setminus K
, and each element of
\pi_{1}(X\setminus K)
admits representatives in
A
and
B
. Consider a point
q\in A\cap B
and the push-forward maps induced by the natural inclusions:

(6.3)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q)\to\pi_{1}(X\setminus K,q)\,,\quad j_{*}:\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q)\to\pi_{1}(A,q)\,,

and

(6.4)

h_{*}:\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q)\to\pi_{1}(B,q)\,.

By Theorem

4.8
,
i_{*}(\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q))
has cardinality at most
C(n,v)
. Since
\pi_{1}(A)
and
\pi_{1}(B)
contain copies of
\pi_{1}(X\setminus K)
, which has cardinality bigger than
2C(n,v)
, there exists
g\in\pi_{1}(X\setminus K)
with representatives
g_{a}\in\pi_{1}(A,q)\setminus j_{*}(\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q))
and
g_{b}\in\pi_{1}(B,q)\setminus h_{*}(\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q))
.
By the Seifert-Van Kampen theorem, the natural map

(6.5)

k_{*}:\pi_{1}(A,q)*_{\pi_{1}(A\cap B,q)}\pi_{1}(B,q)\to\pi_{1}(U)

is an isomorphism. In particular, since
k_{*}(g_{a}g_{b}^{-1})=1
, we deduce that
g_{a}g_{b}^{-1}=1
in the amalgamated product, thus contradicting
[
66
, Theorem 2.6, page 187]
.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
(M^{n},g)
be a smooth compact manifold with non-empty connected boundary. If
M\setminus\partial M
admits a non-collapsed
\RCD(0,n)
structure with Euclidean volume growth, then
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is finite.

Appendix A
A Margulis lemma for
\CD(K,N)
spaces

In the proof of the vanishing theorem for the simplicial volume (Theorem
4.1
) we relied on the following Margulis lemma for
\RCD
spaces:

Theorem A.1
.

For every
K\leq 0
and
1\leq N<\infty
there exists a constant
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon(K,N)>0
such that for every
\RCD(K,N)
space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and
p\in X
, the image through the push-forward of the inclusion map

(A.1)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{10}(p),p)

is virtually nilpotent.

By scaling, Theorem
A.1
yields the following:

Corollary A.2
.

For every
1\leq N<\infty
there exists a constant
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon(N)>0
such that for every
\RCD(0,N)
space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
p\in X
and
R>0
the image of the inclusion map

(A.2)

\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon R}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{R}(p),p)

is virtually nilpotent.

Remark A.3
.

With the very same strategy as in the proof of Theorem
A.1
one can prove a version of Margulis lemma for non-branching and semi-locally simply connected
\CD(K,N)
spaces.

For smooth Riemannian manifolds, Theorem
A.1
follows from a stronger result due to V. Kapovitch and B. Wilking in
[
55
]
. They proved that there exist constants
0<\varepsilon(K,N)<1
and
C(K,N)>0
such that if
(M^{N},g)
is a complete Riemannian manifold with
\mathrm{Ric}\geq K
then the image of

(A.3)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(B_{10}(p),p)

has a nilpotent subgroup whose index is bounded above by
C(K,N)
. In the recent
[
37
]
the same effective virtual nilpotency statement was obtained for
\RCD(K,N)
spaces
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},p)
for the image of

(A.4)

i_{*}:\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)\to\pi_{1}(X,p)\,.

It is expected that fueling the strategy of
[
55
]
with the ideas introduced to deal with the synthetic framework in
[
37
]
should lead to a complete generalization of the Margulis lemma from
[
55
]
to
\RCD(K,N)
spaces. Here we obtain a weaker but local version of the Margulis lemma where the index of the nilpotent subgroup is not controlled effectively. While such statement seems non-optimal, it is sufficient for the purposes of the present paper. The proof follows a different strategy with respect to
[
55
,
37
]
, and it is based on
[
16
]
.

The proof of Theorem
A.1
is deferred to the end of the appendix. Besides
[
16
]
, the main technical tool will be a local Bishop-Gromov inequality valid for universal covers of open subsets of
\RCD(K,N)
spaces endowed with their natural length structure.

Remark A.4
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a length metric space (not necessarily complete). Let
\widetilde{X}
be a path connected topological space, and let
\pi:\widetilde{X}\to X
be a covering map. We define a distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
on
\widetilde{X}
as

(A.5)

\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(a,b):=\inf\{\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}}(\pi\circ\gamma):\gamma\in C([0,1],\widetilde{X})\,\,\,\gamma(0)=a,\gamma(1)=b\}\,.

With this definition, it is clear that
\pi:(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\to(X,\mathsf{d})
is a local isometry. Precisely, for every
p\in\widetilde{X}
there is a neighborhood
N_{p}\ni p
such that
\pi|_{N_{p}}
is an isometry.
It follows that for every
\gamma\in C([0,1],\widetilde{X})
we have

(A.6)

\mathrm{length}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\gamma)=\mathrm{length}^{\mathsf{d}}(\pi\circ\gamma)\,,

so that
\gamma\in AC([0,1],\widetilde{X})
if and only if
\pi\circ\gamma\in AC([0,1],X)
. Moreover,
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
is a length metric space.

Finally, if
\mathfrak{m}
is a measure on
X
, there is a unique measure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
on
\widetilde{X}
such that
\pi_{\#}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{N_{p}})=\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi(N_{p})}
for every
N_{p}
as above.

We denote by
v_{K,N}(r)
the volume of the ball of radius
r
in the model space for the
\CD(K,N)
condition.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space with
K\leq 0
and
1\leq N<\infty
.
Let
O\subset X
be an open, bounded, and path-connected set and call
\overline{\mathsf{d}}
the length distance induced by
\mathsf{d}
on
O
. Given a path-connected covering
\pi:\widetilde{O}\to O
, endow
\widetilde{O}
with the length distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
and the measure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
obtained by lifting
\overline{\mathsf{d}}
and
\mathfrak{m}
, cf. Remark
A
. For every
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{O}
, the function

(A.7)

(0,\mathsf{d}(\pi(\widetilde{p}),X\setminus O))\ni r\mapsto\frac{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r}(\widetilde{p})\big)}{v_{K,N}(r)}\,,

is non-increasing.
Moreover, if
\mathfrak{m}=\mathscr{H}^{N}
then the function in (
A.7
) is bounded from above by
1
.

Proof.

Observe that
(O,\mathsf{d})
and
(O,\overline{\mathsf{d}})
are locally isometric, meaning that for every
p\in O
there is a neighborhood
N_{p}\ni p
such that
\mathsf{d}
and
\overline{\mathsf{d}}
agree on
N_{p}\times N_{p}
.
In particular, since
(X,\mathsf{d})
is non-branching by
[
35
]
, the length space
(O,\overline{\mathsf{d}})
is non-branching as well.
Moreover, since
\pi:(\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\to(O,\overline{\mathsf{d}})
is a local isometry,
(\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
is non-branching as well.

Claim 1:
every pair
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}\in\widetilde{O}
such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})<\mathsf{d}(\pi(\widetilde{p}),X\setminus O)
is connected by a geodesic in
(\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
.

To prove Claim 1 observe that, since
(\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
is a length space, there is a sequence of curves
(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n})\subset AC([0,1],\widetilde{O})
such that

(A.8)

\mathrm{length}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n})\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})+\frac{1}{n}

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the sequence
(\gamma_{n})\subset AC([0,1],O)
defined as
\gamma_{n}:=\pi(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n})
for every
n
. Eventually, these curves will be contained in a compact subset of
O
. Thus, up to not relabelled subsequences,
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma_{\infty}\in AC([0,1],O)
uniformly. Moreover, by what we observed before,

(A.9)

\mathrm{length}^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}}(\gamma_{n})=\mathrm{length}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n})\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})+\frac{1}{n}

for every
n
and therefore
\mathrm{length}^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}}(\gamma_{\infty})\leq\mathsf{d}(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})
by lower semicontinuity of the length. Let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty}
be the lifting of
\gamma_{\infty}
starting from
\widetilde{p}
.
On the one hand, we have that

(A.10)

\mathrm{length}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty})=\mathrm{length}^{\overline{\mathsf{d}}}(\gamma_{\infty})\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})\,.

On the other hand, since
O
is semi-locally simply connected, the curves
\gamma_{n}
are eventually homotopically equivalent to
\gamma_{\infty}
. Therefore
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty}(1)=\widetilde{q}
. This concludes the proof of the claim.

Take any
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{O}
and any
r_{0}<\mathsf{d}(\pi(\widetilde{p}),X\setminus O)
.
Consider the
1
-Lipschitz function
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{O}\to\mathbb{R}
, defined as
\widetilde{u}(q):=\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},q)\wedge r_{0}
. We are going to consider the disintegration of the measure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
with respect to
\widetilde{u}
, following
[
21
, Section 7]
. In particular, we define the transport relation

(A.11)

\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}:=\{(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\in\widetilde{O}\times\widetilde{O}\,:\,\widetilde{x}\neq\widetilde{y}\,\text{ and }\,|\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{x})-\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{y})|=\mathsf{d}(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\}

and the associated transport set
\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}:=\mathtt{p}_{1}(\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}})
, where
\mathtt{p}_{1}
denotes the projection on the first factor. By what we observed before,

(A.12)

\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}=\overline{B}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{0}}(\widetilde{p})\,.

We introduce also the set of branching points

(A.13)

\widetilde{A}:=\{x\in\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}\,:\,\exists z,w\in\widetilde{X}\text{ with }(x,z),(x,w)\in\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}},(z,w)\not\in\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}\}\,.

Accordingly, we consider the non-branched transport set
\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}:=\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}\setminus\widetilde{A}
and the non-branched transport relation

(A.14)

\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}:=\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}\cap(\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}\times\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b})\,.

Since
(\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})
is non-branching, we have that

(A.15)

\widetilde{T}^{b}_{\widetilde{u}}=\overline{B}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{0}}(\widetilde{p})\setminus(\mathrm{Cut}(\widetilde{p})\cup\{\widetilde{p}\})\,.

With our choice of
\widetilde{u}
, all the defined sets are easily seen to be Borel.

Note that
\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}
is an equivalence relation on
\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}
. Denoting by
\widetilde{Q}\subset X
the set of its equivalence classes, every
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}
corresponds to a set
\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}\subset\widetilde{O}
which is a geodesic and thus isometric to an interval
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{R}
. We equip
\widetilde{Q}
with the
\sigma
-algebra induced by the equivalence projection
\mathfrak{Q}:\widetilde{T}^{b}_{\widetilde{u}}\to\widetilde{Q}
and with the measure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}:=\mathfrak{Q}_{\#}\big(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}}\big)
.
The Disintegration Theorem (see for instance
[
21
, Theorem 6.19]
) guarantees the existence of a (essentially unique)
consistent
(see
[
21
, Definition 6.18]
) disintegration, i.e. a measurable map
\widetilde{Q}\ni\alpha\mapsto\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{P}(\widetilde{O})
such that

(A.16)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\widetilde{T}^{b}_{\widetilde{u}}}=\int_{\widetilde{Q}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(d\alpha)\,.

Consider now any point
\widetilde{q}\in B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{0}}(\widetilde{p})
. By construction, there is an open neighborhood
U_{\widetilde{q}}
of
\widetilde{q}
such that the map

(A.17)

\pi_{\widetilde{q}}:=\pi|_{U_{\widetilde{q}}}:(U_{\widetilde{q}},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\to(O,\overline{\mathsf{d}})

is an isometry. Up to taking a smaller
U_{\widetilde{q}}
, we can assume that
\pi_{\widetilde{q}}:(U_{\widetilde{q}},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}})\to(\pi(U_{\widetilde{q}}),\mathsf{d})
is a bijective isometry.
Then, consider a
1
-Lipschitz function
u:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that
u(x):=\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}(\widetilde{p},\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}(x))
for every
x\in\pi(U_{\widetilde{q}})
.
Similarly as before, we can get a disintegration of the measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
(X,\mathsf{d})
, with respect to
u
(see
[
22
, Section 3]
for the adaptation of the discussion above to
\sigma
-finite measures). In particular, similarly as before we can define the objects
R_{u}
,
T_{u}
,
R_{u}^{b}
,
T_{u}^{b}
,
Q
,
\{X_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in Q}
and
\mathfrak{q}
, and according to
[
22
, Theorem 3.4]
we find a disintegration

(A.18)

\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{T_{u}^{b}}=\int_{Q}\mathfrak{m}_{\beta}\,{\mathfrak{q}}(d\beta)\,.

Such disintegration is
strongly consistent
(see
[
21
, Definition 6.18]
), meaning that for
\mathfrak{q}
-a.e.
\beta\in Q
the measure
\mathfrak{m}_{\beta}
is concentrated on
X_{\beta}
. Moreover, since
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is an
\RCD(K,N)
space,
[
22
, Theorem 3.6]
ensures that for
\mathfrak{q}
-a.e.
\beta\in Q
the space
(X_{\beta},\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m}_{\beta})
is isomorphic (as metric measure space) to
(I_{\beta},|\cdot|,h_{\beta}\mathscr{L}^{1})
, for an interval
I_{\beta}\subset\mathbb{R}
and a measurable function
h_{\beta}
which is a
\CD(K,N)
density.
Thus, by
[
21
, Appendix A]
, for
\mathfrak{q}
-a.e.
\beta\in Q
the differential inequality

(A.19)

\big(h^{1/(N-1)}_{\beta}\big)^{\prime\prime}+\frac{K}{N-1}\,h^{1/(N-1)}_{\beta}\leq 0

holds in a weak sense on
I_{\beta}
. Finally,
[
22
, Lemma 3.5]
guarantees
\mathfrak{m}(T_{u}\setminus T_{u}^{b})=0
.

Define the sets

(A.20)

\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}:=\{\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}\,:\,\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}}\neq\emptyset\}\qquad Q^{\widetilde{q}}:=\{\beta\in Q\,:\,X_{\beta}\cap\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})\neq\emptyset\}\,.

Both sets are easily seen to be measurable.

Claim 2:
there exists a measurable bijective map
\Pi:\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}\to Q^{\widetilde{q}}
such that

(A.21)

\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}})=X_{\Pi(\alpha)}\cap\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})\,.

Such map is defined as follows. Given
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}
, take any
x\in X_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}}
and define
\Pi(\alpha)
as the equivalence class of
\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(x)
.
To show that such definition is well posed, given any
\widetilde{q}_{1},\widetilde{q}_{2}\in U_{\widetilde{q}}
such that
(\widetilde{q}_{1},\widetilde{q}_{2})\in\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b}
, we observe that by definition

(A.22)

|u(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1}))-u(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{2}))|=\mathsf{d}(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1}),\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{2}))\,,

thus
\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1}),\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{2})\big)\in R_{u}
. Suppose that
\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1}),\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{2})\big)\in R_{u}\setminus R_{u}^{b}
. Then w.l.o.g. there exists
x\in X
such that
(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1}),x)\in R_{u}
and
(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{2}),x)\not\in R_{u}
. Up to replacing
x
with a suitable point on the geodesic connecting
\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(\widetilde{q}_{1})
and
x
inside
\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})
, we can assume that
x\in\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})
. Setting
\widetilde{x}:=\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}(x)
, we obtain that
(\widetilde{q}_{1},\widetilde{x})\in\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}
and
(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{x})\not\in\widetilde{R}_{\widetilde{u}}
, a contradiction. This proves that the map
\Pi
is well defined. Reverting the same strategy, one shows that
\Pi
is bijective and that (
A.21
) holds. The measurability of
\Pi
simply follows from (
A.21
).

Recalling that
\mathfrak{m}(T_{u}\setminus T_{u}^{b})=0
, an argument similar to the one discussed above shows that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}\setminus\widetilde{T}_{\widetilde{u}}^{b})=0
. In particular,

(A.23)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}}=\int_{\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}}\,\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(d\alpha)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})}=\int_{Q^{\widetilde{q}}}\mathfrak{m}_{\beta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})}\,\,{\mathfrak{q}}(d\beta)\,.

By definition of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
, we know that
(\pi_{\widetilde{q}})_{\#}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}})=\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})}
. Thus

(A.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}}=

\displaystyle\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}\big)_{\#}[\mathfrak{m}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi(U_{\widetilde{q}})}]=\int_{Q^{q}}\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}\big)_{\#}[\mathfrak{m}_{\beta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})}]\,{\mathfrak{q}}(d\beta)

(A.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}}\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}\big)_{\#}[\mathfrak{m}_{(\Pi(\alpha))}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi_{\widetilde{q}}(U_{\widetilde{q}})}]\,{[(\Pi^{-1})_{\#}\mathfrak{q}]}(d\alpha)\,.

This gives a disintegration of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}}
which is (strongly) consistent because (
A.18
) is strongly consistent. By essential uniqueness of consistent disintegrations, see
[
21
, Theorem 6.19]
, we deduce that

(A.26)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{U_{\widetilde{q}}}=\big(\pi_{\widetilde{q}}^{-1}\big)_{\#}(\mathfrak{m}_{\Pi(\alpha)}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\pi(U_{\widetilde{q}})})\,,\qquad\text{for }\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}\text{-a.e. }\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}\,.

In particular,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}|_{U_{\widetilde{q}}}
is concentrated on
\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}
, which is isometric to a closed interval
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}
. Moreover, by (
A.19
), for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}
-a.e.
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{q}}
the metric measure space
(\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}},\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha})
is isomorphic to
(\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}},|\cdot|,\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}\mathscr{L}^{1})
, where
\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}
is such that

(A.27)

\big(\widetilde{h}^{1/(N-1)}_{\alpha}\big)^{\prime\prime}+\frac{K}{N-1}\,\widetilde{h}^{1/(N-1)}_{\alpha}\leq 0\,\,\quad\text{weakly on}\quad\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}}\,.

Here and in the following, with a slight abuse of notation, given any open set
E\subset\widetilde{O}
we denote by
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap E
the subset of
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}
that corresponds via isometry to
\widetilde{X}_{\alpha}\cap E
. This last conclusion holds for every
\widetilde{q}\in B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{0}}(\widetilde{p})
and for the respective neighborhood
U_{\widetilde{q}}
.

Take any
r_{1}<r_{0}
. Since
\overline{B}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{1}}(\widetilde{p})
is compact we can find
\widetilde{q}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{q}_{M}\in B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{0}}(\widetilde{p})
such that

(A.28)

B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{1}}(\widetilde{p})\subset U_{\widetilde{q}_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup U_{\widetilde{q}_{M}}\,.

We let
\widetilde{B}
be the
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}
-null set defined as the collection of elements
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}
such that (
A.27
) does not hold for some
\widetilde{q}_{i}
and
U_{\widetilde{q}_{i}}
, with
i=1,\dots,M
. Thus, for every
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}\setminus\widetilde{B}
the differential inequality (
A.27
) holds on
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}_{i}}
for every
i
such that
