\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap U_{\widetilde{q}_{i}}\neq\emptyset
. Thanks to (
A.28
) we conclude that for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}
-a.e.
\alpha\in\widetilde{Q}
the inequality (
A.27
) holds on
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{1}}(\widetilde{p})
. Thus, for every such
\alpha
the function
\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}
is a
\CD(K,N)
density on
\widetilde{I}_{\alpha}\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r_{1}}(\widetilde{p})
. Therefore by Bishop-Gromov we have that

(A.29)

\frac{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\big)}{v_{K,N}(s)}\geq\frac{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{t}(\widetilde{p})\big)}{v_{K,N}(t)}\,,\quad\text{for every }s<t<r_{1}\,.

Thus, for every
s<t<r_{1}
we get

(A.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widetilde{Q}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\big)\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(d\alpha)

(A.31)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\widetilde{Q}}\frac{v_{K,N}(s)}{v_{K,N}(t)}\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{t}(\widetilde{p})\big)\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}(d\alpha)=\frac{v_{K,N}(s)}{v_{K,N}(t)}\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{t}(\widetilde{p})\big)\,.

The first part of the statement follows since
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{O}
and
r_{1}<r_{0}<\mathsf{d}(\pi(\widetilde{p}),X\setminus O)
were arbitrary.

Assume now that
\mathfrak{m}=\mathscr{H}^{N}
, so that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{N}
is the Hausdorff measure induced by
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
.
To prove the final part of the statement, it is sufficient to show that

(A.32)

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}^{n}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r}(\widetilde{p})\big)}{v_{K,N}(r)}\leq 1\,.

To this aim we observe that for
r>0
sufficiently small the restriction

(A.33)

\pi|_{B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r}(\widetilde{p})}:B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{r}(\widetilde{p})\to B_{r}(p)

is an isometry. Thus the claim follows from
[
34
, Corollary 2.14]
.
∎

We record below another useful technical result about coverings of balls in
\RCD
spaces.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},p)
be an
\RCD(K,N)
space for some
K\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq N<\infty
. Let
r\in(0,+\infty]
, and let
\pi:Y\to B_{r}(p)
be a covering, with the convention that
B_{\infty}(p):=X
. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
be respectively the distance and the measure induced on
Y
by lifting the length structure of
B_{r}(p)
and the measure
\mathfrak{m}
, cf. Remark
A
.
Let
\widetilde{p}\in\pi^{-1}(p)
be fixed, and let
F\subset Y
be the union of the interiors of the lifts to
\widetilde{p}
of the segments of
B_{r}(p)
having
p
as an endpoint. Then the following hold:

(1)

\pi|_{F}:F\to B_{r}(p)
is injective;

(2)

For every deck transformation
g:Y\to Y
,
F\cap g(F)=\emptyset
;

(3)

For every
s\in(0,r)
, we have that
F\cap\pi^{-1}(B_{s}(p))\subset B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})
;

(4)

For every
s\in(0,r)
, we have that
\mathfrak{m}(B_{s}(p))=\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p}))
.

Proof.

To prove item
1
, let
a,b\in F
such that
\pi(a)=\pi(b)
. Let
\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}\subset X
be segments having
p
as an endpoint whose interiors contain
\pi(a)=\pi(b)
, and such that their lifts to
\widetilde{p}
contain
a
and
b
, respectively. Since
\RCD(K,N)
spaces are non-branching due to
[
35
]
, either
\gamma_{a}\subset\gamma_{b}
or
\gamma_{b}\subset\gamma_{a}
, which implies
a=b
.

To prove item
2
, assume by contradiction that
x\in F\cap g(F)
. Then there are segments
\gamma_{\widetilde{p}},\gamma_{g(\widetilde{p})}\subset X
having
p
as an endpoint, and such that the interiors of the lifts to
Y
from
\widetilde{p}
and
g(\widetilde{p})
respectively both contain
x
. Again using that
\RCD(K,N)
spaces are non-branching, we deduce that
\gamma_{\widetilde{p}}\subset\gamma_{g(\widetilde{p})}
or
\gamma_{g(\widetilde{p})}\subset\gamma_{\widetilde{p}}
. Thus their common part has two different lifts starting from
x\in Y
, a contradiction.

To prove item
3
, let
x\in F\cap\pi^{-1}(B_{s}(p))
, and let
\gamma_{x}\subset B_{s}(p)
be the segment with endpoint
p
whose lift to
\widetilde{p}
contains
x
. The length of
\gamma_{x}
is the same as the length of its lift. Thus
x\in B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})
, concluding the proof.

For item
4
, by Vitali’s Covering Theorem there exists a countable family of disjoint closed balls

(A.34)

\{C_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\overline{B}_{s}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\widetilde{p})\subset Y

centered in points of
F\cap\overline{B}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})
such that
\pi|_{C_{i}}:C_{i}\to\pi(C_{i})\subset\overline{B}_{s}(p)
is a measure preserving isometry and

(A.35)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(F\cap\overline{B}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}C_{i}\Big)=0\,.

Vitali’s theorem can be applied since
(\overline{B}_{s}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}(\widetilde{p}),\widetilde{\mathsf{d}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}})
is uniformly locally doubling by Lemma
A
.
Hence, using item
1
in the last inequality and the fact that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}(\partial B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p}))=0
by Lemma
A
, we deduce that

(A.36)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\Big)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\cap C_{i}\Big)

(A.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(\pi(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\cap C_{i})\Big)\leq\mathfrak{m}(B_{s}(p))\,.

To prove the converse inequality, let
\Sigma\subset X
be the cut-locus of
p
. We have that
\mathfrak{m}(\Sigma)=0
(see, for instance,
[
21
, Remark 7.5]
). By the very definition of
F
,

(A.38)

B_{s}(p)\setminus\Sigma\subset\pi(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p}))\,.

Hence, using the collection
\{C_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
constructed above and the fact that
\pi:Y\to B_{r}(p)
sends
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
-negligible sets to
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible sets, we deduce that

(A.39)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(B_{s}(p))=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(B_{s}(p)\setminus\Sigma)\leq\mathfrak{m}\Big(\pi(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p}))\Big)

(A.40)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(\pi(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\cap C_{i})\Big)

(A.41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\cap C_{i}\Big)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\Big(F\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{s}(\widetilde{p})\Big)\,,

thus concluding the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Following the notation of Lemma
A
, let
\overline{\mathsf{d}}
denote the length distance induced by
\mathsf{d}
on
B_{10}(p)
. Endow the universal cover
\pi:\widetilde{X}\to B_{10}(p)
with the length distance
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
and the measure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
obtained by lifting
\overline{\mathsf{d}}
and
\mathfrak{m}
.
Consider the metric space
(Y,\rho)
, where
Y:=\pi^{-1}(p)
and
\rho
is the restriction of
\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}
to
Y
.

Claim
: There exists a constant
C(K,N)
such that
(Y,\rho)
has
packing constant

C(K,N)
, meaning that every ball of radius
4
in
(Y,\rho)
can be covered by at most
C(K,N)
balls of radius
1
.

To prove the claim observe that by Lemma
A
there exists a constant
C(K,N)
such that for every
\widetilde{p}\in\pi^{-1}(p)
we have

(A.42)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{9}(\widetilde{p})\big)\leq C(K,N)\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1/2}(\widetilde{p})\big)\,.

Fix any
\widetilde{p}\in\pi^{-1}(p)
and suppose that there are
M
disjoint balls of radius
1/2
with centers in
\pi^{-1}(p)\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{4}(\widetilde{p})
, i.e. we find
p_{1},\dots,p_{M}\in\pi^{-1}(p)\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{4}(\widetilde{p})
such that
B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1/2}(p_{i})\cap B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1/2}(p_{j})=\emptyset
whenever
i\neq j
. Using (
A.42
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{9/2}(\widetilde{p})\big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{1/2}(p_{i})\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{C(K,N)}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{9}(p_{i})\big)\geq\frac{M}{C(K,N)}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\big(B^{\widetilde{\mathsf{d}}}_{9/2}(\widetilde{p})\big)\,,

thus
M\leq C(K,N)
. In particular for any
\widetilde{p}\in Y
there are at most
C(K,N)
disjoint balls in
(Y,\rho)
of radius
1/2
with centers in
B^{\rho}_{4}(\widetilde{p})
.

Consider any
p_{0}\in Y
. If
B^{\rho}_{4}(p_{0})\setminus B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{0})\neq\emptyset
take
p_{1}\in B^{\rho}_{4}(p_{0})\setminus B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{0})
. Iterate this process by selecting at step
i
a point
p_{i}\in B^{\rho}_{4}(p_{0})\setminus\big(B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{0})\cup\cdots\cup B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{i-1})\big)
if
B^{\rho}_{4}(p_{0})\setminus\big(B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{0})\cup\cdots\cup B^{\rho}_{1}(p_{i-1})\big)\neq\emptyset
. Observe that at each step
i
the balls
B^{\rho}_{1/2}(p_{0}),\dots,B^{\rho}_{1/2}(p_{i})
are pairwise disjoint by construction. Therefore this process has to stop within step
C(K,N)-1
, thus producing a cover of
B^{\rho}_{4}(p_{0})
with at most
C(K,N)
balls of radius
1
.

Since
X
is semi-locally simply connected by
[
89
]
,
B_{10}(p)
is semi-locally simply connected as well. Thus we can
identify
\pi_{1}(B_{10}(p),p)
with the deck transformations of the universal cover
\pi:\widetilde{X}\to B_{10}(p)
. By restricting any such deck transformation to
Y=\pi^{-1}(p)
we get that
\pi_{1}(B_{10}(p),p)\leq\text{Iso}(Y,\rho)
, with a slight abuse of notation. By
[
16
, Corollary 11.17]
there exists a constant
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K,N)>0
such that for every
\widetilde{p}\in Y
the
almost stabiliser

\Gamma_{3\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p}):=\langle S_{3\varepsilon}(x)\rangle
, where
S_{3\varepsilon}(x):=\{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(B_{10}(p),p)\,:\,\rho(\gamma\cdot\widetilde{p},\widetilde{p})<3\varepsilon\}
, is virtually nilpotent. On the other hand, it is a standard observation that every loop in
\pi_{1}(B_{\varepsilon}(p),p)
is homotopically equivalent to a concatenation of loops of length less than
3\varepsilon
. In particular the image of the map (
A.1
) is isomorphic to a subgroup of the almost stabiliser
\Gamma_{3\varepsilon}(\widetilde{p})
. Therefore it is virtually nilpotent.
∎
